# Architecture

This document describes only what is implemented. Proposals live in `spec/`. When a spec is implemented, its content moves here in the same commit (CLAUDE.md "Design docs").

**State**: Built as of 2026-10-30: the Phase 0 foundations, Phase 1 compatibility core, Phase 2 non-agentic chat features and Phase 3 LCM implementation. This describes code, not owner acceptance of the daily-use milestone. The build includes worker-isolated storage, the message graph and independent forks, library editing and card forks, protected drafts and recycle bin (§3); auth and settings (§4–§5); registered providers, normalized usage/cost rollups and image token estimates (§6); card, lorebook and preset formats, macros and import/export (§8–§11); streaming chat, versions, guidance, notes, image editing/toggles and group-only greetings (§12); ST folder import (§13); library editors, snippets and raw-source selection copy (§14); registered context assembly, exact historical request inspection, image/cache estimates and cache-aware history (§15); and LCM planning, generation, condensation, editable summaries and approved backfill (§16). Message insertion is built through all nine units: storage, fork inheritance, positional reads and assembly, the bin, generation, LCM, history edits and ST re-import, and the registered insertion UI (§3.8, §3.10, §3.11, §3.13, §5, §13, §15–§16). General agentic RP rollouts and workshop/plugin installation remain unbuilt; their designs are in `spec/`.

---

## 1. Repository

npm workspaces, TypeScript, ESM throughout.

| Path | Contents |
|---|---|
| `packages/shared` | Code used by both server and client: the client-facing RPC contract (`api.ts`), its errors (`errors.ts`), and the domain schemas and pure logic (users, chats and state, library and merge, card and lorebook documents, drafts, the bin, providers and usage), plus UUIDv7 ids (`id.ts`). Imported as `@rp/shared/<file>.ts`. |
| `packages/server` | The Node server: HTTP, RPC over WebSocket, authentication, the database workers. |
| `packages/client` | The React single-page app, built with Vite and served by the server. |
| `spikes/` | Throwaway experiments with their findings (not part of the build). |
| `spec/` | Proposals and design records. |

**Runtime.** Node 24 runs the server's TypeScript directly through type stripping, so the code must use erasable syntax only (`erasableSyntaxOnly`). The client is bundled, and so are the database workers for production (§3.6). The server's main thread runs from source.

Bun was measured as an alternative (2026-10-10) and not adopted. On this server it gave the same RPC latency and throughput and 13% less memory, but it has no `node:sqlite`, stubs the event-loop delay API, and Effect's Node HTTP layer errors under it. Bundling the workers saves far more memory on Node.

**Toolchain.**
- TypeScript 6.0 (`tsc --noEmit` per package). TypeScript 7's native compiler isn't used yet, because typescript-eslint doesn't support it.
- ESLint with typescript-eslint, plus `eslint-plugin-react-hooks` including the React Compiler rules.
- Prettier.
- knip: fails on unused files, exports and dependencies, the shared package's exports included (`knip.jsonc` says why each exception exists).
- Vitest with `@effect/vitest`, and Playwright for the browser tests (§5).
- `npm run check` runs all of them. CI (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`) also builds the client and the workers, runs the browser tests, and builds the Docker image (without pushing it). It checks that the image refuses to start without exposure settings, and runs it with an admin password until its health check passes.
- `npm run size` prints one line of sizes: code lines in sources and in tests, comment lines, and doc lines (`scripts/size.ts`, tracked files outside `spikes/`). CI prints it too, so growth shows per commit.

**Dependencies are pinned to exact versions.** The owner's machine enforces a 5-day cooldown on newly published packages.

**Effect 4** (`effect` 4.0.x) is used on the server and in the client's data layer. Its RPC, HTTP, SQL, worker and reactivity modules are tagged `unstable` upstream (EFFECT.md §2), which is another reason versions are pinned exactly.

---

## 2. Server

`packages/server/src/main.ts` reads configuration from the environment and launches `ServerLive` (`server.ts`). That's one HTTP server with these parts:
- **`/rpc`:** the RPC endpoint (`AppApi` from `@rp/shared/api.ts`) over a WebSocket, JSON-serialized. Every UI action is meant to become an RPC in this group (DESIGN §7.7). Today it has `Health`, `Whoami`, the account RPCs (§4), the provider-kind, connection, credential, model, network-settings and usage RPCs (§6), `ListLibrary` (§11), the chat RPCs (§12), the editor RPCs (§14), the recycle bin (§3.11), composer-draft and notice RPCs (§12), and the request inspector (§15).
- **`/healthz`:** a plain-text liveness route, used by the Docker health check.
- **`/auth/login`, `/auth/logout`** (§4), **`/library/import`, `/library/export`** (§11.4) and **`/library/avatar`, `/library/media`** (§14.5): plain HTTP, for what a WebSocket message can't do well, a cookie and a file.
- **Everything else:** the client build, served as a single-page app (unknown paths return `index.html`, so deep links work).

**Content-Security-Policy.** `RequestGuard` puts one on every response: `default-src 'self'`; `script-src 'self'` (the build's own modules, nothing inline, nothing from elsewhere, so markup that got past sanitizing can't run script); `style-src 'self'`; `img-src 'self' blob:` (local attachment previews use blob URLs; an image URL in a message could report on the chat to its server; Markdown blocks images too); `connect-src 'self'` plus `ws://` and `wss://` on the Host the browser used, when it's a plain host name (`'self'` covers the WebSocket in current browsers; the explicit origins, older ones); `object-src 'none'`, `base-uri 'none'`, `form-action 'self'`, `frame-ancestors 'none'`. The browser tests run every screen under it and fail on any violation (`e2e/csp.spec.ts`). The Vite development server doesn't send it.

**Configuration** (`config.ts`, environment variables):

| Variable | Default | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `HOST`, `PORT` | `127.0.0.1`, `8080` | Listen address. `PORT=0` takes a free port, which the startup log names |
| `DATA_DIR` | `./data` | Database, instance lock, media files (§11.1) |
| `DB_READERS` | `2` | Reader worker count, at least 1 (§3) |
| `FORMAT_WORKERS` | `1` | Format worker count, at least 1: how many uploads are parsed, or exports written, at once (§11.3) |
| `DB_ALLOW_NETWORK_FS` | `false` | Override the refusal of filesystems not known to be local (§3.4) |
| `DB_WORKERS` | `bundle` in production, else `source` | Worker entry points, the format workers' too (§3.6) |
| `AUTH_MODE` | `single` | `single` or `proxy` (§4) |
| `AUTH_PROXY_HEADER` | `remote-user` | Identity header in proxy mode |
| `ALLOWED_IPS` | unset | IPs and CIDR ranges besides loopback that may connect in single mode (§4) |
| `TRUSTED_PROXIES` | unset (loopback) | The only addresses that may connect in proxy mode (§4) |
| `ALLOW_INSECURE_EXPOSURE` | `false` | Serve every address without protection (§4) |
| `LOOPBACK_REQUIRES_LOGIN` | `true` | Once an account has a password, loopback needs a login too (§4) |
| `ADMIN_PASSWORD_FILE`, `ADMIN_PASSWORD`, `ADMIN_USER` | unset | The first admin's password, for headless servers. Applies only while no enabled admin has one (§4) |
| `PUBLIC_ORIGINS` | unset | Origins the app is served at by name, e.g. `https://rp.example.com` (§4) |
| `CLIENT_DIST` | `packages/client/dist` | Static files to serve |
| `SECRET_KEY`, `SECRET_KEY_FILE` | unset | The server secret (below). Generated into `DATA_DIR/secret.key` when neither is set |
| `KEY_HOST_BINDING` | `true` | Keys go only to the hosts their credential was entered for (§3.12). A security setting, so it's set only here, never from inside the app |
| `FAKE_GENERATOR_TPS`, `FAKE_GENERATOR_TOKENS` | unset, `400` | Read only by the development entry point (`src/dev/main.ts`): chats generate synthetic text at this rate, about this many tokens each (§12). `main.ts` ignores them |

**Data directory** (`dataDirectory.ts`). Before opening storage or generating the server secret, startup creates missing path components once and checks directory write/search access. An unusable `DATA_DIR` exits with the path and filesystem cause. Recursive Node mkdir is avoided: on `/proc/nope` it loops on `ENOENT` instead of returning. `startup.test.ts` spawns the real server for missing, non-directory and unwritable paths, with generated and configured secrets.

**Secrets** (`secrets.ts`). Connection secrets (API keys, header values, proxy URLs) are encrypted at rest with AES-256-GCM, under a key derived (HKDF-SHA256) from the server secret.
- **Bound to where they belong.** Each ciphertext is bound to what it belongs to (`credential:<id>:key`, `connection:<id>:headers`, `connection:<id>:proxy_url`), so it doesn't decrypt anywhere else.
- **Format:** a version byte, a fresh 12-byte nonce, the tag, then the ciphertext.
- **Only the main thread holds the secret.** The database workers only ever see ciphertext.
- **The secret is automatic** (owner, 2026-10-10: setup must be trivial). It comes from `SECRET_KEY` or `SECRET_KEY_FILE` (a Docker secret) when set. Otherwise it's generated at first start into `DATA_DIR/secret.key`, mode 0600 (tightened if a restore loosened it), and never overwritten.
- **Honest scope:** with the key file next to the database, encryption protects a leaked database or backup on its own, not a copy of the whole data directory.
- **A changed secret** makes stored secrets unreadable (`SecretUnreadable`), which surfaces as "enter the key again". It never surfaces as a crash.

**Outgoing requests** (`net/`, spec/PROVIDERS.md §6.2). Every request the server sends goes through the `Network` service. ESLint forbids the global `fetch`, `undici` and `node:https` anywhere else in `packages/server/src`, so nothing can skip the proxy by accident.
- **The instance settings** are an optional proxy (HTTP, HTTPS or SOCKS5) and a bypass list of hostnames, `*.domain` suffixes, IPs and CIDR ranges. Local ranges are on the bypass list by default (loopback, RFC 1918, link-local, CGNAT, IPv6 ULA and link-local). A hostname bypasses by address only if every address it resolves to is on the list, and its connection is then pinned to those addresses (`bypassOnlyLookup`), so a second lookup can't land elsewhere. A host the admin listed by name or suffix goes direct whatever it resolves to, unpinned. With `forceProxy`, non-admins can't send direct or through their own proxy (§6).
  - They're stored in `app_settings`, with the proxy URL encrypted, since it can hold credentials.
  - The proxy is only ever shown as scheme, host and port.
  - Unusable settings are refused with `InvalidNetworkSettings`.
- **Each connection overrides the route** with `inherit`, `direct` or its own proxy. The route for a request (`route.ts`, pure and unit-tested):
  - the override wins
  - otherwise a target on the bypass list goes direct, matched by name, by literal IP, or by its resolved addresses (IPv4-mapped IPv6 counts as IPv4)
  - otherwise the instance proxy is used
  - a failed DNS lookup leaves the target to the proxy
- **The route is reported:** `fetchFor(override, onRoute)` gives a `fetch` for one connection's requests and reports each request's route, e.g. "direct: 192.168.1.20 is in the bypass list" or "via proxy socks5://vpn:1080".
- **One undici dispatcher per route** (`Agent`, `ProxyAgent`, `Socks5ProxyAgent`), reused across requests and closed with the layer. undici marks its SOCKS5 support experimental. SOCKS5 sends the hostname to the proxy, so DNS for proxied targets resolves remotely.
- **A response may stay silent for 30 minutes,** before its headers and between body chunks, on every route (an interceptor sets undici's `headersTimeout` and `bodyTimeout` per request, since proxy agents don't pass their options to their per-origin pools). undici's default of 300 s cut off local models still reading a long prompt, and the retry started the prompt again. Stopping a generation aborts at once, as before.
- `test/network.test.ts` runs real requests through a local HTTP proxy (CONNECT and absolute-URI forwarding) and a SOCKS5 proxy with credentials (`test/support/proxies.ts`), counting the tunnels.
- **pi-ai:** all four built-in wire adapters use our `fetch`. Startup removes SDK environment defaults; the app-owned Gemini REST adapter uses the same controlled transport (§6). Node environment proxies cannot choose a provider route.

**Stopping** (`SIGTERM`, `docker stop`). Node's `server.close()` waits for every connection, and an open tab's WebSocket never ends on its own, so a stop used to hang until it was killed, with the database workers mid-flight. Closing the server now:
- ends idle HTTP connections, and drops RPC requests that arrive from then on, unanswered, so nothing starts that the stop could cut off between its commit and its reply
- waits up to 2 seconds for the RPCs in flight to reply (the serialization layer counts requests in and exits out)
- sends each WebSocket a close frame with code 1001 ("going away"; the client reconnects), and destroys whatever is left a second later
- exits 0: an interruption by signal is how the server is meant to stop, and the default 130 would count as a failure to systemd (`main.ts`)

`shutdown.test.ts` stops a real server with a socket open (it exits 0 within seconds and leaves no WAL behind, the sign that the workers closed the database), and during a burst of writes (every committed write was acknowledged).

**Event-loop monitoring** (`eventLoop.ts`). A 10 ms timer measures how late each tick runs, into one-second buckets. `Health` reports the last minute: the longest single delay (`eventLoopMaxMs`) and the worst one-second p99 (`eventLoopP99Ms`). Reading them resets nothing, so callers don't affect each other. Node's `monitorEventLoopDelay` isn't used: it discards the first interval after a reset, so a 300 ms stall starting just after a bucket change showed as 0.3 ms. Tested: a single 300 ms block shows in the max, including 2, 10 and 25 ms after a bucket change, and still does after another read and a bucket change. Anything that blocks the loop delays every live stream, so this is a budgeted metric (CLIENT-FRAMEWORK §7).

---

## 3. Database

SQLite through Node's built-in `node:sqlite` and Effect's driver (`@effect/sql-sqlite-node`); see STORAGE.md for why not Turso. Code is in `packages/server/src/db/`.

### 3.1 SQLite never runs on the main thread

`node:sqlite` is synchronous: a query blocks the thread that runs it, for its full duration. On the main thread, that would stall every WebSocket stream for as long as any query runs. So all SQL runs in worker threads:
- **One writer worker** (`writer.ts`) holds the **only read-write connection**. It runs migrations at startup, then serves `WriteOps` one at a time (`concurrency: 1`). A crashed writer is respawned every second by Effect's worker pool, which logs nothing, so `writerProtocol` logs it on the main thread: the first stop with its cause, then failed restarts as a count (after 1, 2, 4, 8, … of them), then the recovery (`test/readerPool.test.ts`).
- **A pool of reader workers** (`reader.ts`, `DB_READERS`) each hold one **read-only** connection (opened with `readOnly`, plus `PRAGMA query_only`). They serve `ReadOps`. A request goes to an idle reader, so one slow query doesn't hold up the others.
- **The main thread** talks to both through typed Effect RPC over `worker_threads` (`Database.ts`: the `Writer` and `Reader` services). Errors cross the boundary typed: `StorageError { reason: busy | constraint | corrupt | other }` from the operation, `RpcClientError` if the worker itself fails.
  - `StorageError.message` carries SQLite's own message (e.g. `UNIQUE constraint failed: users.handle`). `corrupt` is `SQLITE_CORRUPT` or `SQLITE_NOTADB`; unique, check, not-null and foreign-key failures are `constraint`. The worker logs every one. The message never leaves the server: every client-facing handler turns storage failures into `ServerError` with a fixed message.

**The reader pool** (`readerPool.ts`) is our own RPC transport, not Effect's worker pool (`RpcClient.layerProtocolWorker`, still used for the single writer):
- **A reader is busy until it answers.** `node:sqlite` can't stop a running query. Effect's pool freed a reader as soon as its caller gave up (a timeout, a closed tab), so the next read went to the reader still running the old query. Measured with 2 readers: after a 2 s read timed out at 200 ms, the next light read waited 1,843 ms while the other reader idled. Here an interrupted read is forwarded to its reader, which stops at its next statement at best, and the reader takes nothing new until it answers.
- **A crashed reader gets nothing until it's back.** Effect's pool kept routing to it until it respawned (a light read waited 1,651 ms). Here it's out of the rotation from the crash until the respawned thread is ready, about a second later.
- **A crash fails no read.** It touches only the read its reader was running, which goes first in line to the next free reader, the respawned one included (`retryCrashed`, which the database readers opt into: reads are idempotent). A read that stops a second reader fails and isn't retried again, so a poison query costs two restarts, not a crash loop. Nor is a read whose caller gave up on it. Reads on other readers, and reads queued meanwhile, never notice.
- **What fails a request is typed:** `RpcClientError`, which the handlers turn into `ServerError` with a fixed message. An rpc's exit can only fail with the errors it declares, and a protocol error fails every request of the client, so the pool sends the error as an encoded defect of that one request, and `ReaderPool.client`, which builds the `Reader` service, restores it. A reader's own defect stays a defect.
- **Every crash is logged** on the main thread with the worker's error (e.g. its exit code) and what happens to its read, and the reader is respawned every second.
- **While no reader is up, queued reads fail** (`RpcClientError`) at each failed respawn, instead of waiting for a respawn that may never succeed. With every respawn failing, a read fails within about a second. A spawn that throws (a stale bundle, §3.6) is respawned like any other.
- **Startup waits for one reader to be ready**, and fails if every reader dies before any is.
- Requests queue in arrival order for the first idle reader. A request whose send fails fails alone. Sent to a ready thread, it gets a defect, and its reader is released. Sent first to a new thread, Effect's worker holds it until the thread is ready, and its failure stops the thread like a crash. Effect's worker keeps a message it couldn't send for its next thread, so each thread gets a new one. A buffer listed twice among a request's transferables fails a send, so they're listed once.
- An answer to a request its reader isn't running is dropped, with a warning. The workers answer a handler's defect with its request's Exit (`disableFatalDefects`), so a `Defect` message, which names no request, is the protocol failing: the pool treats it as a crash of that reader.
- Tested (`test/readerPool.test.ts`) with a test reader whose requests block like queries, without a clock. A reader can be held, in a request or before it starts, until the test releases it, and light reads must finish first: one sent to a held reader would wait for a release that never comes. After an interrupted read, every light read goes to the other reader. A read that stops its reader once is retried, on the reader that's up, or on the replacement while the other reader's held read finishes. A read that stops every reader fails with a typed `RpcClientError` after two stops. An interrupted read that then stops its reader stops only that one. A stray Exit from a reader finishes nothing, and a read whose reader sends a `Defect` is retried. With readers that die as they start, startup fails, and a read sent once every reader is down fails with the failed respawn's error. An earlier version asserted that light reads took under 300 ms, and waited 300 ms for both crashes to be handled.

Measured: the same CPU-heavy 800 ms query blocks the main event loop for **795 ms** when run on the main thread, and **0.8 ms** when run through a reader worker. `test/database.test.ts` checks this without a clock, through a test-only reader pool with the readers' connection setup (`test/support/diagnostics.ts`). The query on the main thread blocks the loop for over 100 ms. One reader is then held blocked until the test releases it, and 20 light reads through the other reader must finish first. If the blocked reader blocked the main thread, they never would. An earlier version asserted under 30 ms of main-thread lag, which failed at load averages of 40 to 60.

### 3.2 Storage operations are named commands

Operations (`ops.ts`) are declared as RPC groups and implemented inside the workers. **A write operation is one synchronous transaction executed entirely inside the writer.** You can't send a closure to a worker thread, so a transaction structurally can't span an `await`, a network call or an LLM request. That's the usual way transactions grow long and hold locks.

Operations that run over 25 ms are logged (`timed.ts`): a write holds the writer, so a slow one delays all writes.

**A read operation is one read transaction** (`query` in `tx.ts`, a deferred `BEGIN` on the read-only connection), so all its statements see the same snapshot. Readers still never block the writer or each other. Measured cost on a trivial point read through the workers: about 40 µs per read (≈190 µs to ≈230 µs sequential) and about 8% fewer reads per second at saturation; no difference on a list read.

### 3.3 Why `SQLITE_BUSY` can't happen inside the process

`SQLITE_BUSY` ("database is locked") means one connection needs a lock another connection holds. The setup rules out every in-process way for that to happen:

| Possible conflict | Why it can't occur |
|---|---|
| Two writers | There is exactly one read-write connection, and the writer worker serializes its operations. Within it, Effect's driver serializes statements and holds the connection for a whole transaction. |
| A writer and a reader | The database is in WAL mode, where readers never block the writer and the writer never blocks readers. Reader connections are read-only and can't take write locks. |
| A read transaction upgrading to a write | Readers can't write, and the writer opens transactions with `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, so there's no upgrade. |
| Checkpoints | Only automatic PASSIVE checkpoints run (the writer, at commit). PASSIVE never waits on or blocks readers. `journal_size_limit` bounds the WAL file. |
| Readers opening before the file exists or is migrated | Readers are spawned only after the writer answers `Ready`, which it does only after migrations. |

Tested (`test/database.test.ts`): 500 concurrent writes and 500 concurrent reads complete with no errors, and every completed write is visible to readers.

### 3.4 Outside the process

Other processes are the remaining risk, so they're kept out:
- **Instance lock.** At startup the main thread takes an exclusive SQLite lock on `DATA_DIR/instance.lock` (`locking_mode = EXCLUSIVE` + `BEGIN EXCLUSIVE`) and holds it for the process lifetime. It's an OS file lock, so the kernel releases it if the process dies; there's no stale lock file to clean up. A second instance on the same data directory exits immediately with `InstanceLocked`, instead of contending for the database. Examples: a second `npm start`, or two containers on one volume. This is tested both in-process and from a separate process.
- **Only filesystems known to be local are accepted** (`statfs` on Linux): ext2/3/4, XFS, Btrfs, ZFS, F2FS, tmpfs and overlayfs (Docker's container filesystem). Network and FUSE filesystems (NFS, CIFS/SMB, sshfs, rclone, virtiofs, Docker Desktop file sharing, WSL2's 9p `/mnt/c`, Ceph, AFS) may not provide the POSIX locks SQLite relies on, and WAL can corrupt there. Anything else is refused as unrecognized. `DB_ALLOW_NETWORK_FS=1` overrides this, and is also what a local filesystem not on the list needs (e.g. exFAT or NTFS on a USB disk, bcachefs, eCryptfs).
- **Nothing else should open the live database.** There is no built-in backup operation yet; the app holds its instance lock for the life of its database workers.
- **Backstop.** If an outside process does take a lock anyway, the workers' 5 s `busy_timeout` applies. It blocks only that worker thread, never the main thread, and anything that still fails surfaces as `StorageError { reason: "busy" }`.

### 3.5 Connection settings

| | Writer | Readers |
|---|---|---|
| Mode | read-write, `journal_mode = WAL` | `readOnly`, `query_only = ON` |
| Durability | `synchronous = FULL`: a committed transaction survives power loss | n/a |
| Other | `foreign_keys = ON`, `temp_store = MEMORY`, 64 MiB cache, `journal_size_limit` 64 MiB | `temp_store = MEMORY`, 32 MiB cache |

### 3.6 Worker bundles

Each worker thread is a separate V8 isolate that loads its own copy of every module it imports. Loading Effect's module graph from source costs about 85 MB per worker. `npm run build` therefore bundles the worker entry points into single tree-shaken files with rolldown (`packages/server/scripts/build-workers.ts` writes `packages/server/dist/workers/{writer,reader,formats}.mjs`; the format worker is §11.3). A bundled worker costs about 19 MB (measured in `test/database.test.ts`, which fails above 40 MB).

`DB_WORKERS` selects the entry points:
- `source` is the default outside production, so development never runs a stale bundle.
- `bundle` is the default when `NODE_ENV=production`. The server refuses to start if the bundles are missing (`WorkerBundleMissing`) or stale (`WorkerBundleStale`), with a message to run `npm run build`.

**Stale bundles are refused.** The build writes `stamp.json` next to the bundles (`workerStamp.ts`): the repository files among the bundled modules (43 today) plus `package-lock.json`, and a SHA-256 of their contents. At startup the server hashes the same files again, in about 3 ms, and again before spawning each worker thread, so a crashed worker isn't replaced by code from an `npm run build` run after the server started (`WorkerBundleStale`; the reader is retried every second, and reads fail meanwhile). Without it, `git pull && npm start` would run new main-thread code against old workers: migrations wouldn't run, and a new operation would fail every in-flight request with "Unknown request tag".

The tests run the core operations against both. The bundles are built once per test run, into a temporary directory, by a Vitest global setup (`test/support/globalSetup.ts`). A build usually takes about 2 s and 10 to 17 s of CPU, since rolldown uses every core. Once, at a load average of 85, a build in a test file's hook overran the hook's 60 s limit. The same setup points `TMPDIR` at a directory of the run's own. Tests get temporary directories only from `tempDir()` (`test/support/tempDir.ts`), which removes them when the test file ends, and anything left at the end of the run fails it.

**The server tests run with `--no-wasm-code-gc`** (`vitest.config.ts`). Node's type stripping is WebAssembly, so every source-mode worker runs Wasm. In Node 24's V8, Wasm code GC can free an import wrapper twice while another isolate is tiering it up ([nodejs/node#66366](https://github.com/nodejs/node/issues/66366), fixed upstream in V8 `9b8ca54d5a` and not yet in a 24.x release). The tests create and terminate workers constantly, so without the flag about 1 run in 14 of the database test files died with SIGSEGV or `Check failed: jit_page.has_value()`. With the flag, none did in 70 runs. Node 22 has a V8 version older than the bug. The server isn't exposed in production: its bundled workers run no Wasm, so only the main thread does. In development, the source workers also run Wasm, but they live as long as the server.

Measured whole-server memory with the main thread, the writer and 2 readers: about 380 MB from source, and **188 MB** with bundles (Docker image, idle). The main thread still runs from source.

### 3.7 Schema

Migrations (`migrations.ts`) run in the writer through Effect's `SqliteMigrator`. There is one, `0001_baseline` (`db/baseline.ts`): the final schema as a list of statements (tables, indexes, triggers, views, FTS tables), because `node:sqlite` prepares one statement at a time. It seeds no rows. It replaced a chain of 56 migrations, generated from the schema that chain produced on an empty database, and `test/migrations.test.ts` compares `sqlite_master` against a frozen dump of that chain (`test/fixtures/preSquashSchema.json`).

**While no one uses the app, a schema change edits the baseline, and the deploy starts from an empty data directory.** There is no upgrade path for existing databases. Once anyone keeps data, changes become migrations after `0001_baseline`, and the baseline stops changing.

**Old databases are refused.** Before the migrator runs, the ledger (`effect_sql_migrations`) is read: if it holds an id this code doesn't know (any database made before the squash, or by a newer build), startup fails with a message to start from an empty data directory, and nothing is run or changed. Migration numbers named elsewhere in this file (`0052`, `0053`, …) refer to steps of the replaced chain.

Conventions:
- **Tables:** all `STRICT`, with JSON columns guarded by `json_valid` CHECKs and timestamps in epoch milliseconds.
- **Ids:** UUIDv7 (`@rp/shared/id.ts`), so `ORDER BY id` follows creation order.
- **Concurrency:** mutable rows carry `row_version` for optimistic concurrency.
- **Mutual references** (`slots.selected_version_id` ↔ `versions.slot_id`, a summary ↔ its selected revision) use deferred foreign keys. The version row is inserted first, then the slot, in one transaction.

**Invariants enforced by the database** (`db/baseline.ts`). Triggers check each statement, so no single statement can break these, and `test/schema.test.ts` checks each one on a bare connection. A sequence of statements still can, for example deleting a selected model version and inserting another under its id in one transaction: no trigger can tell that from a legitimate insert. The writer is the only code path that writes, and these back it up:
- **Edits are non-destructive.** Changing content creates new rows; it never overwrites. Deliberate deletion is allowed, and foreign keys keep it consistent: for example, a slot can't be left selecting a deleted version, and deleting a chat removes everything in it, its chat-local overrides and drafts included.
  - Model-written versions are frozen once they stop generating, and a version never goes back to generating. Only deleting for good from the recycle bin empties one: `purged_at` set, under a bin item of the chat's owner that the version, or its slot, carries (a swipe, message or branch item; an import's copy carries the original's, migration 0042). `purged_at` is never cleared on versions and slots.
  - Amending a user-written version copies the previous content into `version_history` automatically.
  - Entity revisions and summary revisions are immutable.
  - A rollout's context snapshot is frozen once written.
  - State events can only be superseded, not changed.
- **The graph is append-only.**
  - A slot's parent is in the same chat, one level up.
  - A merge slot's extra parents (`slot_parents`) are in its chat and recorded before anything is appended below it, which keeps the graph acyclic: an edge out of a slot nothing points to can't close a cycle. Triggers can't run a recursive cycle check.
  - A slot and its merge parents never move.
  - A slot's chain (§3.8) is assigned by the database when it's inserted, never changes, and holds one slot per depth. A state event carries its slot's chain and depth, set the same way and fixed; a chat-wide event has neither (`test/chains.test.ts`).
  - A fork (§3.8) keeps the parent, base and moment it was made at, and its lineage, and never moves to another chat; a chat starts with one, made by a trigger, whose id is the chat's (`test/forks.test.ts`). A fork made from another is inserted with its lineage, itself first; a chat's first gets one of itself.
  - A slot keeps the selection it was inserted with (`slots_selection_frozen`, 0054): a fork's choice is an override (§3.8), so no single write can change what two forks show. An override keeps its fork, kind and target, and in place only takes a newer value and seq (`test/forkSelection.test.ts`).
  - A version's slot, writer and origin never change, and its source can only be cleared.
  - A slot stays inserted or not as it was inserted (`slots.inserted`, `slots_inserted_frozen`; spec/MESSAGE-INSERT.md §2.2). An `insert` override places an inserted slot of its chat: `slot_id` is the slot's parent (its anchor, null above the first message) with the parent's depth, the flag is set (1 placed, 0 not) and there's no value, bin item or changeset, on insert and on update (`fork_overrides_insert`, `fork_overrides_insert_update`).
- **References stay inside their chat.**
  - A slot selects only its own versions, which is what invariant 3 rests on, and so does a fork's selection override; an override is in its fork's chat.
  - A chat's active leaf is one of its slots, and it is the current fork's tip: triggers keep the two equal whichever one a statement moves (`db/baseline.ts`), so what still reads the chat's leaf reads the current fork. A chat's current fork is one of its forks.
  - A fork's parent, base and tip are in its chat, as are the forks slots, rollouts and queued messages name.
  - A lane forks from a slot of its chat.
  - A version's source ("edited from #7") is a version of the same slot. Deleting the source clears the reference.
  - A state event's slot is in its chat, and its version is in that slot.
  - A summary selects only its own revisions.
  - Rollouts (slot, leaf, parent), summaries (first and last slot, rollout), summary inputs (slot, and a version of that slot), summary parents, summarization jobs (slots, summaries), changesets (rollout), versions (rollout, changeset) and superseded state events (changeset) refer only within their chat. A reference into another chat would also block that chat's deletion with a foreign-key error.
  - A chat override is of an entity of the chat's owner.
  - Each holds on insert and on update. Rows others refer to never move to another chat: slots, lanes, state events, rollouts, changesets, summaries, summarization jobs and bin items. A chat override can only stop being one.
- **What a user owns stays theirs.** Nothing with an owner ever changes owner: chats, entities and connections (0010), and credentials, sessions, drafts, bin items, recovered texts, settings, media references, jobs and ledger rows (0011). A ref never moves to another entity.
- **A fork's relationship stays inside its owner's library** (0056). Its parent is another entity of the same owner and kind, never itself; the revision it copied is a revision of that parent; the chat it was made for is its owner's. On insert and on update. Longer cycles are the writer's to refuse (`SetCardParent`): a trigger can't run the recursive query.
- **Not enforced here:** ownership of the ids inside a state event's value (§3.8), and that a reference points at something not in the recycle bin. The writer checks both.

**Foreign keys are indexed on the referencing side** wherever the referenced row can be deleted; `test/schema.test.ts` fails on a new one without an index. SQLite checks a key from that side, so without the index, every insert or delete of a referenced row scans the referencing table. Measured on a bare connection (`0010` added the missing ones): appending a message with 100,000 slots in the database took 6.2 ms before (a scan of `slots` for `selected_version_id`) and 0.07 ms after; deleting a 2,000-slot chat among 20,000 slots took 6.5 s before and 55 ms after.

**Full-text search** (`db/baseline.ts`). FTS5 external-content indexes cover `versions.content`, `summary_revisions.content` and `entity_revisions.search_text`, using `unicode61` with diacritic folding and prefix indexes, and triggers keep them in sync.
- Each indexed table has an `INTEGER PRIMARY KEY seq` (a rowid alias) next to its public `id`. That's because `VACUUM` can renumber the implicit rowids of TEXT-keyed tables, which would silently corrupt an external-content index.
- Versions are indexed only once they stop generating, so streaming doesn't churn the index.
- Every indexed table's triggers cover inserts, deletes and (for mutable tables) updates; `test/schema.test.ts` checks this for every FTS table. A deleted row's `seq` can be reused by the next insert (SQLite can't add `AUTOINCREMENT` without rebuilding the table, and the rebuild would cascade-delete the rows referencing it), so the delete trigger is what keeps a deleted text from matching whoever's row takes its `seq` next. `0010` rebuilt `entity_revisions_fts`, which had no delete trigger.

### 3.8 Message graph operations

Contracts are in `db/ops.ts`, writes in `db/writes.ts` (writer worker), reads in `db/reads.ts` (readers). Domain types are in `@rp/shared/chat.ts`; state-event keys and the fold are in `@rp/shared/state.ts`.

- **Writes:**
  - `CreateChat`: pending or active, with participants joined at the initial position and an optional greeting as the root slot.
  - `AppendMessage`: a new slot in a fork (`forkId`, the current one if none is named), becoming its tip; the fork is chatted in, so it becomes the chat's current fork. The first message turns a pending chat active. Without `branch` the parent must be the fork's tip, and a tab that missed a newer message gets `LeafMoved`, with that tip, instead of silently forking. A branch is explicit (`branch: true`, any live parent): a fork is made at the parent first (`CreateFork`), from the named fork if the parent is on its path, else from the most recent fork through it, unless the parent is that fork's own tip, which it then just continues.
  - `CreateFork`: a fork of `fromForkId` (the current one if null) at a live message on its display path (`atSlotId`; null: before the first message). Its base and tip are that message, its parent the fork it came from, `branched_at` the chat's `state_seq`, and it becomes current: a fork made by forking is (UX-REWORK §3). It shows exactly what its parent shows up to there: in its transaction, each inserted slot its own display path would place that the parent doesn't show at or before the message gets an `insert` row of 0 (`placeAsShown` in `db/graphWrites.ts`, `hiddenAtFork`; spec/MESSAGE-INSERT.md §2.4). `SwitchBranch`'s fork for a line no fork follows does the same at its base.
  - Display slot enumeration is shared by assembly and history edits through `displaySlots` (`display_pieces` / `display_slots`). The lore-delay message count reads those slots too; `insertionAssemblyReview.test.ts` exercises a real embedded lorebook after an insertion and exact snapshot rebuild.
  - `InsertMessage` (`db/insertWrites.ts`; spec/MESSAGE-INSERT.md §2.4, §7): a message inserted below `anchorSlotId` (null: above the first message) in a named fork, one transaction. The anchor must be on the fork's display path and not deleted there, else `StaleWrite` (with the tab's `expectedRowVersion` and the anchor's as the fork shows it); a neighbour inserted meanwhile is no conflict, since the new message still goes directly below the anchor. It writes the version, then the slot (`inserted = 1`, parent the anchor, the anchor's lane, `fork_id` the fork), then the `insert` row with flag 1 (`writeInsert`), and chats in the fork. `Write { speaker, content, images? }` is a finished human-written version (`user_written`, verbatim), its speaker checked as an append's is (`checkSpeaker`: a live card of the user's, or none). `Duplicate { versionId }` copies the version the fork shows at the anchor (its own, or the fork's copy in its place; another is `StaleWrite`): origin `duplicate`, with the original's status, speaker, writer, text, context and display forms, reasoning, macro mode, frozen macros, connection, model and attachments, without its generation, flags, history or source. A model-written duplicate stays model-written, so it's frozen and an edit adds a version (spec §9 Q2, decided (a)). A generating or failed version is refused (`InsertRefused`). `Generate { speaker, direction? }` (unit 6) writes a model version still generating, as the speaker (checked as for Write) with the one-turn direction, origin `generated`; the caller starts its generation once the transaction commits, as for a swipe. Write and Generate may name the insertion editor's draft (`draft: { draftId, session }`, the `insert` key, §3.10), committed in the transaction only if it still holds exactly the content (Generate: the direction). It returns the slot, its position on the display path and the version as the fork shows it.
  - **The `message.insert` command** (`api/messages.ts`, `@rp/shared/messagesApi.ts`; spec §5, §6, unit 6). The `InsertMessage` RPC (`{ chatId, forkId, anchor, expectedRowVersion, body }`) dispatches through the registered command, so a plugin can replace it and an agent inserts messages the same way; `runCommand` passes the errors the RPC declares (`StaleWrite`, `InsertRefused`, `GenerationUnavailable`) through with their tags. `Generate` is refused up front without a generator. After the write it starts the generation (`Generations.start` in the fork, outside the transaction), then publishes the slot as the fork's `Slot` event with its `position` (`PathEntry.inserted` marks it), the chat, and `Lcm`. The anchor has no event: inserting below it changes none of its choices, so its row version doesn't move. A generated inserted message is assembled as a mid-history regenerate is, from the fork's display path up to the message before it (§15 "Inserted messages"); the model never sees what follows it (DESIGN §3.1 [D], owner 2026-10-30: messages are in causal order), and there is no stage or option for it. It doesn't hold the composer or the queue: the queue waits only behind its own slot, so a message sent at the tip meanwhile posts at once, and that reply leaves the inserted message out while it's generating, as it leaves out any generating version. A reply already generating when a message is inserted keeps the context it was assembled with; what was queued behind it posts at the tip and includes the insertion. Undo is `DeleteMessage` of the inserted message, which stops its generation with it (`stopDeleted`). Two tabs inserting at one anchor both succeed, the newer directly below it. `test/insertGeneration.test.ts` (against the mock provider: context up to the anchor, the speaker's turn as an impersonation for the user's card, a direction, retry, a reply generating at the tip and the queued one after it, a generation in another fork, Undo and restore, two tabs, refusals through the command, no generator) and `test/queue.test.ts` (neither the composer nor the queue waits on it; a queued message posts at the tip after an insertion) cover it.
  - `SetForkName` (normalized; blank clears it, `name_user` marks it the user's) and `SetForkPinned`, owner-scoped on a live fork. Naming advances the row version of the fork's first message, which shows the name among its alternates, so open tabs take it.
  - `SwitchBranch`: the alternate at an index among a slot's reachable siblings (structural ones: an inserted slot is never an alternate, `reachableSlot` in `db/graphReachability.ts`, and is alone at its own place, as `ListBranches` and the path entries' branch counts have it), and the fork to open through it: the live fork whose path holds it that was current most recently (`forkThrough` in `db/forks.ts`, the fork shown first). With none (a line only a restore brought back), one is made there from the fork shown, its tip on the alternate's newest replies. It changes no fork's history and doesn't make the fork current.
  - `AddVersion`: swipe, edit, or write your own, optionally selected in a fork (`forkId`, the current one if none is named); it's chatting in that fork. An edit's `sourceVersionId` must be a live version of the same slot.
  - `SetVersionFlags`: owner-scoped star/hide of a live version in a fork (`forkId`, else the fork through its message, `forkAt`), one writer transaction: a `star` or `picker_hide` override in that fork on the message's own version (a copy's flags are its original's), written only when the flag changes there. It returns the version as that fork shows it and the fork, and the API publishes it as that fork's `Version` event. Neither flag changes context eligibility.
  - `SetImageExcluded`: owner-scoped, takes one image (by media id) of a live version out of the context in a fork, or puts it back (`forkId`, else the fork through its message), one writer transaction: an `image_exclude` override in that fork with target `<original version id>:<media id>` (the message's own version, so a fork's amend copy keeps the choice for the images it keeps) and flag 1 or 0, written only when it changes there. Other forks, made before or after from elsewhere, are untouched; forks made from it later inherit it through the lineage like any override. The same image attached twice to one version is one media id, so both copies go out or come back together. It doesn't change the slot's row version, so an open editor stays current. Path entries carry `excludedImages` (the shown version's media ids taken out in the entry's fork), and the API publishes the slot's event, which also has the next context read again.
  - `AmendVersion`: a finished user-written version as a fork shows it (`forkId`, else the fork through its message), `StaleWrite` when the fork shows another text in its place or the version was amended since. In place (the trigger keeps the earlier text) when no other fork shows it (`shownElsewhere` in `db/forks.ts`: a fork other than this one, in the bin or not, whose path holds the message and whose picker there shows that version, i.e. it doesn't replace it). Otherwise the fork gets a copy X′ (origin `edited`, `source_version_id` the message's own version X, `fork_id` the fork, its other columns X's but its generation, `version_history` and attachments copied, X's text added to its history) and a `replace` X → X′ in that fork, so the other forks keep X's text and history. A copy only this fork shows (no fork made from it since) is then amended in place. Not chatting in the fork (owner, 2026-10-27).
  - `SelectVersion`: in a fork (`forkId`, the current one if none is named), with `expectedVersionId`, the version the tab saw that fork show there, failing as `StaleWrite` when the fork shows another. Selecting what it shows writes nothing. Either id may be a fork's copy: it stands for its original, which is what the selection names.
  - `SetSlotHidden`: hides a live message from the model or shows it again in a fork (`forkId`, else the fork through it; ST's ghost toggle), as the user's doing: a `hide` override of that fork (§3.11, `writeOverride`), so another fork's context and stepped history are untouched. What the fork's context reads is its row, else the slot's own `context_excluded` (frozen; an ST import's hidden messages have it set and `hidden_by_user` unset) as a published staged LCM unhide's overlay shows it. A hide is a history reduction in that fork (`recordReduction`: `history_changes` row of the fork, which counts in it and in forks made from it later). Already so in that fork, it changes nothing. `StImportUnhide` writes `hide` rows of 0 in the fork through its slot. Publishing an LCM enable run writes one `hide_range` override with flag 0 and the run id. Visibility resolves individual hides and published ranges together in lineage and sequence order, so only that fork and forks made afterwards inherit the unhide; a later hide wins. The enable dialog captures the fork id as well as its leaf; offer, staging and publication use that fork even when another fork ends at the same message. Publication refuses if an unnamed run now resolves to a different fork, keeping visibility and settings in the same fork. `StImportUnhide` also accepts an explicit fork. Staging stays invisible and publication never writes a row per message. Lineage compaction copies range references and preserves their precedence against individual hides.
  - `AddStateEvent`: a user's state change (origin `user`) at a live position, optionally attached to a version, scoped to `forkId` (else the fork through the position). `AddStateEvents` writes several in one transaction (the persona switch). Positions are checked against the fork, with a compatibility exception for positional writes on legacy imported paths. Legacy imported paths without fork rows retain `forkAt`'s current-fork fallback for positional writes (new ST imports have file-owned fork rows, §13), including writes addressed by the fallback ID the reader returned; a card swap still requires a position on its fork. Each event records immutable `fork_id` and the chat's next `state_seq` as `fork_seq`; rollout variable events record their rollout's fork too. Initial and imported events with null provenance remain shared base state. New initial user events, including `lcm.settings`, belong to the fork: summarization's switch and settings are per fork (owner, 2026-10-27). `ChangeLcmSettings`, `GetLcmSettings` and `SetLcmEnabled` accept the viewed fork, as do the cast, card-details and note-list reads and cast writes.
- **Reads:**
  - `GetChat`
  - `GetPath`: a window of a fork's path (the current fork's if none is named), or of the path to a named leaf; below.
  - `ListForks`: a chat's live forks, most recently current first, each with its name, pin, tip, the start of its tip's text, when it was made and last chatted in, and whether it's current.
  - `ListVersions`: a message's own versions in a fork (`forkId`, else the fork through it): each as the fork shows it, a copy in its original's place and position, with the fork's flags. The count on a path entry is the same set.
  - `StateEventsAt`: the events that apply at a slot in a fork (`forkId`; none named, the fork through the slot, `forkAt`), in fold order, found by the chain and depth of their slots in the path's segments (`stateEventsOnPath` in `stateRead.ts`, `pathEvents` in `db/pathSegments.ts`, which the assembler's `events` shares), so the cost is the path's branch points and events, not its length. The segments drive the join (`CROSS JOIN`): left to itself, SQLite read every event of the chat. Version-attached events count only while the fork shows their version, and events on a deleted message only once it's restored (DESIGN §3.4, proposed). Events naming a library entity, ref or revision that was deleted for good, or scoped to a card that was, are left out (`played_by_me.set` clears a purged selected persona), so restoring a message can't bring a purged card back. In a fork that places insertions, the path is its display path through the slot (`stateEventsThrough`, `displayThrough` and `displayEvents` in `db/displayPath.ts`; spec/MESSAGE-INSERT.md §3.1, unit 4): the pieces through the slot's position drive the same join by chain and depth, an inserted slot being the only one at its chain and depth, and each event carries its slot's position. A fork that places none reads `stateEventsOnPath` as before, after one index probe per lineage entry. `AddStateEvent`, `ForkCardForChat` and the fork-notes preview check the slot with `onForkPath`, so an event can be put on an inserted message the fork shows and not on one it doesn't. What reads a fork as it stands (the fork-notes preview's "now", `RenameHistoryTargets`, the cast at a fork's tip) reads through its display end (`displayEndOf`), an inserted message after the tip included. The note binding a chat note's editor resolves (`noteAt`) is the nearest before the slot in display order. The composer’s cast, card and note reads request `StateEventsAt.displayEnd`: when the named slot is the fork’s structural tip (null included), it reads through the display end. Plain `StateEventsAt` remains a precise read through its named slot. Composer cast changes set the same flag on `AddStateEvent`/`AddStateEvents`; the writer resolves the display end inside the transaction so a new change follows events on a trailing insertion. The live-slot check applies to the slot written to, after that resolution: a deleted tip that retains a shown insertion (§3.11) resolves to the live inserted message after it, where the change folds before the next turn, while an explicit position at a deleted message is refused, since its events don't fold. Version-attached events and chat-wide LCM settings keep their explicit positions. This also covers an insertion above the first message in a fork with no structural tip.
- **Fork-scoped events and swaps** (`stateRead.ts`, `forkState.ts`, `@rp/shared/state.ts`; fork independence unit 6, card forks unit 2). Events with provenance apply only within the fork's bounded lineage, like overrides; a `suppress_event` override with flag 1 removes an event in that fork, flag 0 restores it. The writer's overlay helper supports null positions for these rows, and database triggers check that they name an event of the same chat. Changeset undo uses this storage support; its history UI is documented in §3.13.
  - Positional events fold in path depth and per-position sequence order; in a fork with insertions, in display position order (`eventOrder(e, position)`), so an inserted message's events fold after its anchor's and before the next message's. Branch-owned keys (`branchOwned`, currently `card.swap`) are written at slot null without a version or scope and fold last, in `fork_seq` order, including when reading before the first message. `StateEventsAt(leafSlotId)` chooses the viewed fork when reading an earlier position; an explicit `forkId` distinguishes forks sharing a tip. The assembler, chat groups, cast and notes use the same visibility and suppression rules. Frozen assembly rebuilds retain their originally pinned event IDs.
  - `card.swap { entity, replaces }`, exposed by `ChangeCast`'s `Swap`, substitutes a live card in the same family, with ownership checked for both IDs (`replaces` may be binned). A missing cast member is a no-op. Cast order, absence, played-by-me and the card's lorebook binding and embedded-lore switch follow the replacement; the original's card binding stays dormant and comes back on swapping back. Later positional cast and lore-switch writes resolve the displayed card to its pre-swap cast identity before storage, so the final swap fold preserves those changes. Purging an original or intermediate card keeps only its routing IDs and cast flags where a live replacement needs them; its definitions and dormant bindings stay purged. As before, a swap whose incoming card was purged is omitted, unless that card is only an intermediate on the way to a live replacement. New swipes and continuations resolve their writing card through the swaps, and the assembler does the same for a request at an earlier message. Existing message authors stay unchanged. Card chat-membership indexes, lists and usage counts include swaps.
  - Later forks inherit branch-owned events from any fork point through the lineage bound; existing forks do not. Deleting or restoring a message cannot remove a swap. Neither branching nor deleting copies positional swap events. At the 64-entry lineage cap only, `fork_event_snapshots` records references to inherited immutable event IDs, alongside the existing effective-override copy. Descendants inherit these references through their bounded lineage. This preserves assembly pins and suppression targets without duplicating events or walking message history.
  - `forkStateEvents.test.ts` covers shared-tip and initial events, per-fork settings, branch-owned state at earlier positions and in assembly, later/earlier/empty forks, delete/restore, selected-version effects, family and user refusals, suppression, and lineage compaction. Shared fold tests cover card position and flags, lore keys and dormant bindings; `stateKeys.test.ts` includes both swap IDs; schema tests cover provenance and snapshot invariants. The review regressions additionally cover cast changes after swaps, purged originals and swap chains, the viewed fork's next-context note preview, changed-fork enable publication, and client LCM/cache responses at shared tips. The LCM graph oracle tracks per-fork settings and amended-version replacements, and addresses rendered chunks in the same fork as its context assertions.
- **Human note inheritance on fork** (`db/forkNotes.ts`, `ForkNotesDialog.tsx`; U21, owner 2026-10-28). `PreviewForkNotes` compares the source fork at the chosen message and its current tip, using the same event visibility, suppression and note-switch defaults as assembly. It lists live chat notes with human binding/switch changes after the point and card notes with changed chat activation, including cards in the cast or played-by-me set at either endpoint. A card removed after the fork point still gets its note choices. Version-attached effects after the point stay causal and are excluded from the carry-back choice. Card text remains library-owned. Chat-note revisions carry their complete document, including placement and shared-note links; linked text retains shared notes' global semantics.
  - The registered `message.branch` action opens a fork dialog: every changed note defaults to **As it was then**, with **As it is now** alongside it. **Set all notes** changes every row, and individual overrides show a mixed state. Accordion rows expand to the existing CodeMirror merge view, forced side by side at both desktop and phone widths, with explicit then/now headings and on/off labels. Development builds alone expose the button-to-diff comparison variant. Cancel writes nothing; with no changed notes the action creates the fork immediately after the preview.
  - `CreateFork.notes` carries the reviewed comparison and per-note selections. In one writer transaction it recomputes and compares a canonical JSON projection, refuses stale or invalid selections with `ForkNotesChanged`, creates the fork, and writes only the chat-note revision bindings and note switches that a now choice changes from then at its base. Then choices use positional inheritance without new events; unchanged bindings and switches retain their version attachments and default-on behavior. Missing choices default to then. Source and existing sibling forks are untouched; later forks inherit those choices through the normal bounded lineage. Calls without selections retain positional inheritance. No schema change is needed.
  - `forkNotes.test.ts` covers mixed/all-now/default choices, API round trips, card activation defaults, stale/unknown selections with rollback, version-attached effects, membership and later inheritance, including removed/played cards and version-attached bindings/switches after swiping an inherited message. Isolation probes cover the preview at both RPC and worker boundaries. `e2e/forkNotes.spec.ts` exercises defaults, set-all, mixed overrides, CodeMirror diffs and persisted text at 1440/390 in light/dark; `FORK_NOTES_DEV=1` runs both variants through Vite, and `SCREENSHOT_DIR` writes screenshots outside the repository.
- **Ownership.** Every operation takes the acting user and touches only that user's rows. Anything else is `NotFound`, so another user's data isn't even confirmed to exist. That includes:
  - someone else's card as a speaker or participant
  - every entity, ref, revision, connection and model a state event's value names (`db/stateRefs.ts`), with the kind each key expects (a note for `note.activate`). Note keys also reject a note owned by another chat of the same user, as declared by `chatOwned`; card-owned notes remain usable across chats. The stricter `chatOwned: "required"` declaration on `author_note.set` accepts only notes whose upstream document names this chat as owner; card and shared notes are refused without changing the previous choice. Each key declares its id fields, with their table, kind and links (`stateKeyIds` in `@rp/shared/state.ts`), and the checker reads only the declarations. A new key doesn't compile until it's declared. Every id schema (`EntityId`, `RefId`, `ConnectionId`, …) is made by `idSchema` (`@rp/shared/id.ts`), which annotates its table (`ID_TABLE`); ESLint forbids `Schema.brand` anywhere else in `src`. `packages/shared/test/state.test.ts` walks each key's value schema, record keys included: an id the declaration doesn't name, an id below a top-level field, an id inside a template literal or a suspended schema, or a declared field that isn't an id schema fails it. An id typed as a plain string isn't seen, so state values use the id schemas. `test/stateKeys.test.ts` checks every key's ids through the writer against an oracle written apart from the declarations: another user's id, an id of another kind, one that doesn't belong to the value's other ids, and things in the bin (allowed only for `removes` keys) are each `NotFound`. Keys that add something need it live; keys that remove something (`removes`: `participant.leave`, `presence.set`, `note.deactivate`, `lorebook.detach`) check ownership only, so a deleted or purged card can still be taken out of a chat.
  - another user's version as an edit's source
- An event's `scope` is a role (`narrator`, `summarizer`) or a participant, which must be a live card of the user's, checked like a value's ids (`scopeIds`). Lane and `agent:<entity>` scopes (DATA-MODEL §5.1) aren't accepted until something uses them.
- `orchestration.mode`'s `config` and `var.set`'s `value` are free-form and not checked: whatever later reads an id from them must check ownership itself.
| `author_note.set` | Which chat-owned note is the chat's author's note (`{ note }`; null: none): set by an ST import and by the Notes tab |
- **Registered state keys** (`@rp/shared/state.ts`, shared/server `test/pluginState.test.ts`). `StateEventData` preserves the typed built-in keys and their default fold. Every built-in registers its value schema, id declaration, positional/branch scope and projection through `registerStateKey`, the same API used by plugins. `stateKeys` supports scoped overrides, disablement and restoration; declarations and branch ownership resolve through its active entries. New keys use `<plugin>/<key>` and registration validates names and declarations with `stateKeyProblems`, failing with `StateKeyRegistrationError`. Plugin values normally fold into `ChatState.plugin[id]` (`reduce(current, value, scope)`, else last value); `apply(state, value, scope)` can wrap or replace a projection into chat state. Removed entries and stored values incompatible with an overridden schema are skipped without deleting events. `WritableStateEvent` checks current availability and value validity. The private worker wire uses `StoredStateEventData`, allowing registration to be installed before validation; the writer then refuses unavailable keys and invalid values and checks every declared id against the acting user inside its transaction. Positional events follow the path and selected version; branch-owned events have no slot, version or scope and fold after positional events in chat sequence order, using the same frozen fork lineage as `card.swap`.
- **State modules in workers** (`db/stateModules.ts`). `Database.layer` exposes `StateModules` alongside `Writer` and `Reader`. Register a trusted module reference (`id`, absolute `module` URL, `exportName`) in a scope; its `InstallStateModule` export receives `stateKeys` and installs entries through that registry. Registration installs on the server, and each private worker request carries the complete active module stack, overwriting inherited headers. Each worker installs it in a request scope before entering the database transaction and removes it afterward. This preserves overrides, disablement, scope cleanup and worker respawns, for source and bundled workers. The bridge compiles plugin schema predicates with the installed Effect interpreter that dynamic modules import: bundled Effect has private refinement parser results incompatible with another copy. Schema predicates execute synchronously in the writer; imports execute outside transactions. Already admitted requests retain their snapshot; future requests after scope exit see restored registrations. ESM caches module code, so changed code needs a new URL. This is trusted module execution, not installation/consent UI. Shared tests cover declarations, built-ins, reduction, disablement and schema changes; worker tests cover save/fork/reload, positional versus branch ownership, fold order, ownership refusal, scope cleanup and built-in overrides in both worker modes.
- **Paths are read by chain segment** (spec/FORK-INDEPENDENCE-DELTA.md §2.1, unit 1). Each slot has a chain (migration 0052): a slot inserted as the first child of its parent, in its parent's lane, continues the parent's chain; a later child or a root starts one, whose id is its own. The database assigns it (`slots_chain_assigned`), so every insert gets one whatever wrote it. A chain is a run of slots at consecutive depths, so the path from the root to a slot is a few segments `(chain, depths lo to hi)`, one per branch point it passes. `pathSegments` (`db/pathSegments.ts`) finds them newest first: a chain id is its head's id, so the next segment up is two lookups by id (the head's parent, and that parent's chain head). Each step must go strictly up, so a corrupted parent link can't make it loop. A slot is on the path iff a segment has its chain and covers its depth.
- **Display paths** (spec/MESSAGE-INSERT.md §2.3, unit 1; `db/displayPath.ts`, shared by readers and the writer). A message can be inserted between two others: it's an ordinary slot below the message it was inserted below (its anchor; a root above the first message), marked `inserted`, and an `insert` override with flag 1 places it in the fork it was inserted in, which forks made from that fork later inherit through the lineage. What a fork shows is its display path: the structural path (root to tip by parent links, by chain segment as below) with the fork's placed insertions spliced in. `pathPieces(sql, chat, fork, tip)` returns it as pieces in order, each a structural run `(chain, lo, hi)` or one inserted slot, with its start position: the tip's segments, then the fork's effective `insert` rows with flag 1 (`placedInsertions`: one range scan of `fork_overrides_point` on `(fork_id, kind)` per lineage entry, so the fork's insertions, not the chat's length), then `splice`. The gap of a slot P is the inserted slots anchored at P, newest first by id, each followed by its own gap, then P's structural successor; the gap of null comes before the root. So inserting below P always puts the new message directly below P. An insertion whose anchor isn't shown isn't reached; one that is on the structural path itself (a fork made at or below it) is where the path has it. `positionOf(pieces, slot)` and `onDisplayPath(pieces, slot)` take the slot's id, chain and depth: a run holds a slot by chain and depth, an inserted piece by id. Whether a fork shows a slot is `onForkPath` (`db/forks.ts`): for a slot that wasn't inserted it's `onPath`, at its cost, so chats without insertions read exactly as before; `forkThrough` (and so `forkAt`), `shownElsewhere`, `forksShowing`, `CreateFork` and a branch's choice of fork use it, and their candidate forks include those whose tip is above an inserted slot. `displayEnd` is the last piece's slot: the tip, or an inserted slot when everything structural after its anchor is gone from the fork's view. `hiddenAtFork(parent, at, child)` lists what a fork made at `at` mustn't place so that it shows exactly its parent's display path up to `at`: every inserted slot the new fork's own pieces would show that the parent doesn't show at or before `at`, which includes a slot the parent has structurally below `at` while inheriting its `insert` row. Pieces include messages deleted in the fork; whether one is shown is for the caller, as on the structural path. `depth` stays structural. Path windows read by position (below, "By position"), and so do state events (`StateEventsAt` above) and context assembly (§15 "Inserted messages"); the bin's handling of inserted slots is in §3.11, and LCM's in §16 "Inserted messages". `test/displayPath.test.ts` compares `pathPieces` with an oracle that keeps each fork as a list (append at its end, insert directly below the anchor, a fork made at P starts as its parent's list up to P) over random sequences of appends, insertions, forks at any shown message or before the first, and deletions, and over a fork lineage compacted past `LINEAGE_CAP`: order, positions, membership of every slot of the chat, the display end and the deletions.
- **Forks** (spec/FORK-INDEPENDENCE-DELTA.md §2.1, unit 2; `db/baseline.ts`, `db/forks.ts`). A fork is what the user sees as one chat line, from the first message to its tip. It's a row (`forks`), because a leaf can't be its identity: a tip moves with every message, moves up when its last message is deleted (`relocateLeaf`, per fork), and can be a message another fork shares (one made at a message before anything is sent in it, ST's checkpoint). Its history is its path: the slots from the root to its tip, read by chain segment as below. A slot is on a fork's path iff a segment of its tip holds it (`onPath`), which costs the path's branch points. Forks share the slots, versions and every other row of their common history. Which version a message shows, amends of a user's message another fork shows, and stars and picker flags are per fork (below); swipes belong to the message, so a swipe made in any fork is in every fork's picker there (CHATS.md "Forks", owner 2026-10-26). Deletion and hiding are per fork too (below, §3.11). The rest of what belongs to a fork (summaries and history edits) is later units of the delta; events are fork-scoped (§3.8), including new ST imports (§13). Each fork has its own composer (§3.10).
  - **The current fork** is the one the chat opens on: the one last chatted in or made by forking, whichever is more recent (UX-REWORK §3, owner). "Chatted in" is adding a message or a version there (owner 2026-10-27): a send, a reply, a queued message when it's sent, a swipe, a continue, an edit or a written version (`AddSelectedVersion`, `AddVersion`). Selecting an existing version isn't. `chats.current_fork_id` is set in the same transaction as `CreateFork` (`created_at`) and as those writes (`last_chatted_at`). When it goes to the bin (§3.11), the live fork with the latest of the two takes over; a chat's last fork doesn't go (below). `chats.active_leaf_slot_id` stays as the current fork's tip (§3.7), for what still reads the chat's leaf: the assembler, LCM, the cast, chat lists.
  - **Per-fork selection** (spec/FORK-INDEPENDENCE-DELTA.md §2.2 and §2.3, unit 3; `db/baseline.ts`, `db/forkState.ts`). What a fork changes is a row of its own in `fork_overrides`, never a change to a shared one. Each per-fork write takes the next `chats.state_seq` as its `seq`, in its transaction. A fork's `lineage_json` is `[[itself, null], [parent, branched_at], [grandparent, parent's branched_at], …]`, computed when it's made (`insertFork`) and never changed: it sees its own rows, its parent's up to when it was made, and so on up. Entries bounded at 0 are left out. The effective value of a key is the first lineage entry with a row for it, the newest there, else the base column (`effectiveOverrideSql`, one lookup by `fork_overrides_point` per entry). So a choice in F never shows in G, made before or after; a fork made later inherits it, from any point of F's path, since a selection is keyed by slot, not position; and nothing a fork does later reaches a fork made from it.
    - `SelectVersion`, `AddSelectedVersion` and `AddVersion` with `select` write a `select` row in their fork (`writeSelect`). A new slot's first selection is its own column, set at insert (`StartChat`'s greetings insert their versions first). A fork's own row is updated in place, taking the new seq, while no fork was made from it since (no child with `branched_at` ≥ its seq: a grandchild reads it through its parent's bound, which is earlier), so swiping back and forth at a tip nobody forked from leaves one row. Past 64 forks (`LINEAGE_CAP`), a new fork's lineage starts afresh with a copy of its parent's effective rows, which costs the overrides, not the path.
    - A slot's row version as a fork shows it is the slot's plus the newest seq of the fork's selection, replacement, deletion and visibility overrides (`slotInFork`, `selectionIn`): it moves with the fork's choice, amend, hide or restore and never with another's, so a tab of G isn't refused for something done in F. Every per-fork write takes a newer seq, so it only grows. `AddSelectedVersion` checks the same value that path entries carry, along with `shownForkId`, the fork whose choices they show. Image-version regression tests cover writing after hide, unhide and restore, rejecting older editors and keeping sibling forks unchanged.
    - Deleting a swipe is the deleting fork's (§3.11); only when no live fork shows it does it also select a neighbour in the forks in the bin that showed it (the bin writer), and so does deleting one for good in every fork that showed it. An ST re-import writes its file's fork only, with origin `import`; effective local choices in that fork stop it (§13).
    - **Copies and flags** (unit 4; `db/baseline.ts`). A fork's copy of a version (`versions.fork_id`) is shown only where a fork's effective `replace` names it, in its original's place: in the path, the picker (`ListVersions`), the assembler (where it counts as its original: a swipe's number and count, `{{currentSwipeId}}` and `{{lastSwipeId}}`, are the message's own versions'), LCM's paths and freshness, notes, a fork's tip text, the alternates' first words, chat summaries and bin excerpts (`shownInSql`, `shownJoin` in `db/forkState.ts`: the selection, then the replacement of what it selects, each one lookup per lineage entry). Replacements are keyed by the message's own version, not by position, so a fork made later inherits an amend from any point of the parent's path (§2.3 of the delta). A state event attached to a version applies where the fork selects it or shows it as its copy (`attachedApplies`). Stars and picker flags are `star` and `picker_hide` overrides on the message's own version, read through the lineage like a selection; the version's own columns are the base. Deleting a copy deletes the swipe it stands in for (the message's own version, with every fork's copies of it), and deleting that for good empties the copies too (§3.11). New ST re-imports keep the shared suffix and write only the file fork's selection override. The legacy suffix-copy helper (`stImportFork.ts`) is retained only to resume old interrupted records that already started copying (§13). `test/forkVersions.test.ts`, `test/forkVersionsReimport.test.ts`.
    - Readers take the fork they're in: the one named, else, for a read at a slot or leaf (`GetSlot`, `ListBranches`, `StateEventsAt`, the assembler, LCM's operations), the fork through it (`forkAt`: the current fork if its path holds it, else the most recently current one whose does), provided once per operation as `ForkView` (`atLeaf`; `AssembleContext` also takes a `forkId`). What reads the chat's current line (groups, chat summaries, bin excerpts, a fork's tip text) reads the current fork's or that fork's own.
  - **Generations and queued messages** record their fork: a generation the fork it was started in (`GenerationRequest.forkId`: a send's, a swipe's or continue's, a queued reply's), which its context is read in, its summaries are planned in before and after it (the Summarizer's position carries it to `PlanSummaries` and the enqueue writes), and its rollout records, so the user chatting in another fork meanwhile changes nothing in it; without one, the current fork when its path holds the reply's leaf, else the most recent fork through it. A queued message records the fork it was sent in, where it posts later (§12).
- **Paths are read in windows.** `GetPath` reads the last `limit` depths of the path (at most 1000): only the segments reaching into them, then a range of `slots_chain` per segment, so its cost depends on the window and the branch points inside it, not the chat's length. Deleted messages count toward the limit but aren't returned, so a window can be short or empty; `olderLeafSlotId` is where the next older window ends, null at the root.
  - Measured on a 2,001-slot chat: a 200-slot window takes about 22 ms end to end (before chains, walking up parents; the window's rows are the same). `test/graph.test.ts` checks that paging back reconstructs the whole path without gaps, and `test/pathScale.test.ts` does it for a 100,000-slot path with 200 branch points, in windows of 1,000 and, through the branch points, of 7. The bound is checked without a clock, since a 40 ms budget failed at load averages of 40 to 60. Cycles are planted among slots older than a window (with the schema's trigger dropped): a parent link, and a chain head's parent pointing below its own chain. A walk past its limit would never end, so a window must still come back, read in a child process (`test/support/windowProbe.ts`) that is killed if it stalls, since nothing else stops SQLite mid-statement. `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` of GetPath's query (`pathWindow` in `reads.ts`) must show every table reached by index, the slots by a range of `slots_chain`.
  - A window is read as its fork shows it: each slot's selection, the replacement of what it selects and that version's two flags are one lookup each per lineage entry, so it costs the window and the fork's lineage, not the chat's length, its forks or its overrides. `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` shows the lineage read from the fork's own JSON and each override found by `fork_overrides_point`. The lookup's joins are `CROSS JOIN`s, which keep the lineage outside: SQLite 3.53 (Node 24.22) otherwise takes `fork_overrides_target`, which reads every fork's overrides of the target.
  - Rows visited are counted (`test/pathScale.test.ts`: temporary views shadow `slots`, `versions`, `state_events`, `forks` and `fork_overrides` and count each row read, which keeps their indexes usable) on fixtures of 2,000 and 100,000 slots that differ only in length, each with 200 forks, lineages 40 deep, 20,000 selection overrides, 10,000 stars and the chat's first fork's copies of its last ten messages, which forks made from it inherit. A 200-slot window visits 1,222 rows at the tip and 1,262 through 40 branch points as the slots' own selections show it, and 2,432 and 2,684 in the deepest fork, the same in both.
  - Reading all 2,001 slots in one call took 113 ms, of which the SQL was 13 ms; at 20,000 slots the SQL alone is about 180 ms. Hence the windows.
  - **By position** (spec/MESSAGE-INSERT.md §2.3, unit 3).
