A window is the last `limit` positions of the fork's display path (`PathEntry.position`, from 0; `depth` stays structural), so a fork that places insertions is read through its pieces: `GetPath` and `ReadPath` (`readDisplay` in `reads.ts`) take the pieces up to the display end for a null leaf, up to a named leaf (the tip included) where the fork shows it, or along the path to a leaf it doesn't show, then read the positions in the window with `displayWindow`: the overlapping pieces as JSON driving one range of `slots_chain` per structural stretch and one lookup by id per inserted slot. The cursors (`olderLeafSlotId`, `older`, `newer`) are the slots at the positions next to the window, which may be inserted ones; `Around` a slot outside the requested path opens a fork that shows it (`forkThrough`), as before; a target past an explicitly named leaf is outside that path even when it is on the same fork's full display path. `test/forkInsert.test.ts` covers that boundary with and without insertions in the initially viewed fork. A fork that places none (`placedInsertions` is empty: one lookup per lineage entry) is read exactly as before (`pathWindow`), its positions its depths, so chats without insertions read the same rows. The cost with insertions is the branch points, the fork's insertions on the path and the window: every read reads all of them, because positions count from the root. `walkTowards` and its only-child check ignore inserted children. `ListForks` and the writes that return a fork (`forkOut`) take each fork's tip text from the last message it shows (`lastShown` and `withDisplayEnd` in `db/forks.ts`): an inserted slot after the tip when everything structural after its anchor is gone from the fork's view, skipping inserted ones deleted there; the tip itself stays structural. `test/forkInsert.test.ts` pages `GetPath` (windows of 1 to 100) and `ReadPath` (`Bottom` then `Older`, `Around` then `Newer` and `Older`) through a fork with insertions above the first message, nested, mid-path, deleted and after the tip, checking every entry's position, and checks a fork without insertions numbers by depth and the forks' tip texts. `test/pathScale.test.ts` adds a fork with 1,000 insertions (every tenth below the one before) to the 2,000- and 100,000-slot fixtures: paging back by 1,000 at the tip and by 7 through the branch points reconstructs the display path built from first principles; the rows visited are the same in both fixtures: 602 for the segments (201 branch points), 3,001 for the insertion lookup (each insertion's row, slot and anchor, and the fork's row), and 2,870 and 2,800 for 200-position windows at the tip and around depth 1,100; and `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` of the insertion lookup shows `fork_overrides_point` on `(fork_id, kind)` and no table scan.
- **Append cost.** About 2.6 ms per message end to end, including the RPC hop and a commit with a full flush (`synchronous = FULL`, ZFS).

**Summaries belong to the viewed fork's state** (fork independence unit 7; `db/lcmFork.ts`, §16). Summary material and revisions remain shared immutable records. `summary_revision` and `summary_choice` overrides select text and replacements through the same bounded lineage as messages; the shared selected revision is frozen by a database trigger. Summary reads and UI actions accept `forkId`, including coverage at a shared tip. A summary's optimistic row version includes its effective revision and choice sequences, so another fork's edit doesn't invalidate the tab's base. A fork created later inherits the choice, including through lineage compaction. Queue rows and their rollouts record the planning fork; assembly, completion, failure, startup recovery and completion planning retain it. Queue overlap and serial ordering, reply maintenance pins, scheduling coalescence and backfill approval/pause/spending are per fork.

**Known limits:**
- **Operations addressed only by a leaf choose their fork** (`forkAt`): the current fork when its path holds the leaf, else the most recently current one through it. Two forks at the same tip need an explicit fork to disambiguate. Summary UI calls provide it; a background summary job uses its stored fork. Legacy synthetic jobs with null provenance retain the current-fork fallback.

- **`StateEventsAt` reads every event on the path,** because state can come from any ancestor. Since chains it no longer walks to the root (that was linear in chat length, roughly 180 ms of SQL at 20,000 slots): at the tip of the scale fixtures, with 200 branch points and 56 events applying, it visits 710 rows at 2,000 and at 100,000 slots, where the walk visited 2,310 and 100,310. A chat with events on most messages still costs their number; DATA-MODEL §5.2's checkpoints of folded state would bound that.
- **Results cost about 0.1 ms per path entry** to encode and decode as Schema classes across the worker boundary, and the decode side runs on the main thread. If large windows show up in the event-loop budget, the transfer types should become plain structs.

### 3.9 Library operations

Versioned documents (DESIGN §4, DATA-MODEL §3) for cards, presets, lorebooks, notes and preset overlays. Cards fork into independent entities; the other kinds retain layered variants and chat overrides. Contracts are in `db/ops.ts`, writes in `db/libraryWrites.ts`, reads in `db/reads.ts`, and the logic both share in `db/library.ts`. Domain types are in `@rp/shared/library.ts`; the merge is in `@rp/shared/merge.ts`.

**The model.**
- **Revisions** are immutable snapshots with parents, like git commits.
- **Refs** are movable pointers. Cards have exactly one `upstream` ref; the variant and override kinds are only for other entity kinds:
  - an `upstream` ref is the base line (imported, or the original of something the user created)
  - a `variant` follows another ref
  - a `chat_override` follows a ref for one chat only
- A ref that follows another records the revision it last merged from (`merge_base_revision_id`).
- **An upstream ref follows its imports.** Imports form their own line of revisions, each one's parent the previous import. The upstream merges the latest import, and its merge base is the import it last merged. So a re-import merges with edits made on the upstream itself, as a variant's merge does.
- **"Update available"** is derived, not stored: what the ref follows (for an upstream, its latest import) differs from the merge base.
- Documents are any JSON object for now. Per-kind schemas arrive with the importers (PLAN phase 1).
- A content hash over canonical JSON (sorted keys) means an edit, re-import or merge that changes nothing creates no revision. A merge that brings nothing new only moves the merge base and reports `up_to_date`.

**Operations.**
- **Writes:**
  - `CreateEntity`
  - `EditRef`: a new revision on the head, with `expectedRowVersion`. It commits its draft even when it changes nothing.
  - `ForkRef`: a new non-card variant
  - `ForkCard`, `SetCardParent`: card forks, below
  - `CreateChatOverride`: non-card refs only
  - `PromoteOverride`: non-card override to variant
  - `ImportFile` (§11.1): a new import revision, merged into the upstream; it returns the upstream's outcome and its followers'
  - `MergeFollowed`
  - `ResolveMerge`: commit a hand-resolved merge, refused if the followed ref moved again since the preview
  - `SetEntityTags`: folders; `persona` is the persona folder
  - `SetEntityArchived`
- **Reads:**
  - `ListLibrary`: by kind, tag and archive state; chat overrides excluded
  - `GetRef`
  - `RefHistory`: first-parent chain
  - `PreviewMerge`: the resolver's view
  - `ListChatOverrides`
  - `CardFamily`: below
- **Ownership** is enforced as in §3.8. Binned entities and chats are `NotFound`.

**Non-card changes flow to followers.** After any change to a ref (an edit, a merged import, a merge), every ref following it is merged in turn, transitively: upstream, then variants, then the chat overrides that follow those. Each follower ends as one of:
- **fast-forward:** it had no edits of its own since the last merge, so it simply moves to the new revision, with no merge revision
- **merged:** a merge revision with two parents
- **conflicts:** left exactly as it was, with "update available" set until the user resolves it

An upstream merging a new import ends the same ways. The outcomes are returned, so the UI can report what happened.

**Schema versions (interim, until per-kind migrations exist).** Every revision of an entity has the schema version it was created with. A re-import (`ImportFile`) refuses a document of another `schemaVersion` before writing anything, so no merge ever spans versions.

**Card names are revision content.** `name` is the display name, `nickname || name` is the model's name (CCv3), and `character_version` is the subtitle. For cards only, `entity_refs.label` and `subtitle` are derived query caches: database triggers refresh them on insertion, head movement and attempted metadata updates. Other entity kinds retain ordinary library labels. Existing label/subtitle RPC arguments remain compatible: card forks and `RenameRef` write standard fields in a new revision; card creation reads the document's names. The card editor edits all three through normal drafts and explicit Save, rather than a separate library rename. Fork names take precedence over unsaved name/version drafts; those drafts remain on the source, while other selected drafts move to the fork. Card forks are independent entities; they have no followers.

Cards' `label` and `subtitle` on `entity_refs` are caches of the head revision's standard fields (`name`, `character_version`); the `card_names_insert` and `card_names_update` triggers keep them in step, so a display-name change or a new head never leaves them stale. A display-name change preserves the former model name as `nickname` if there was none.

The client checks display-name/version pairs against live and archived library card refs for New card, Fork, a name/version edit and the card History browser’s Restore. Each submission reads those refs directly again before writing; same-origin browser tabs broadcast library invalidations and a focused tab refreshes its name check. It excludes the current ref and permits an imported collision when that pair is unchanged. The disabled action has a nearby explanation until either field differs. The client's check prevents sequential stale-tab submissions, without making simultaneous check/write pairs atomic. Imports and storage allow duplicates; `ForkCard` alone also checks on the server, in its transaction (above). Group members and group tiles include the selected card version. Chat sidebar summaries subscribe to library changes as well as chat changes. Chat search and summaries share `chatCast`: both resolve active-path bindings before reading display name, version and model name. Search filters before pagination, preserving literal substring matching and ASCII case folding. An SQL prefilter first narrows the chats to a superset of the matches (the title, the head of any ref of a participating card, or a revision a `card.binding` in the chat pins), so `chatCast` resolves only those; a search that matches nothing reads no casts.

**Card forks** (spec/CARD-FORKS-DELTA.md §2, unit 1; `db/cardForkWrites.ts`). A fork is a card of its own: a new entity with one `upstream` ref and its own revisions, chats, tags, notes and bin state. Its only tie to its parent is the relationship on its entity row (0056), outside its content, so nothing flows between them: the fork follows nothing, has no update available, and edits to either never reach the other. Cards have no variants or chat overrides (unit 4). The fresh baseline guards insert and update of card refs: only one upstream, no followed ref and no chat. The shared ref tables and columns remain for presets, lorebooks and notes. No data migration or card-specific startup refusal is installed; deployment starts fresh from the edited baseline.
- **`ForkCard`**, one writer transaction, from a card ref's head. Its display name (nonempty), in-character name and version accept the same unrestricted string lengths as the card document and `ForkCardForChat`; the dialog does not truncate them:
  - one revision in the new entity, `parents: []`, origin `user`, `origin_ref` `{"forked": <source revision>}`, the message `Forked from <name · version>`: the head's document with the three names given (`name`, `nickname`, `character_version`). Media ids, the embedded lorebook, `extensions.world`, modules and agent flags are in the document, so they come along; media rows are per user, not per entity, so nothing else is copied;
  - `forked_from_entity_id` and `forked_from_revision_id` (the source), `forked_at`;
  - the parent's tags, the persona folder included;
  - each of the parent's own notes (an upstream note whose `owner.card` is the parent, not in the bin) as a new note entity of the fork's: one revision from its head with `owner.card` the fork, `origin_ref` naming it, its label and tags (DESIGN §4, owner's Q2: notes are forked with the card);
  - with `moveDrafts`, the ref's active drafts but its three name fields, which the dialog decides and which stay on the parent. A draft that started from the head copied starts from the fork's first revision, the same content; an older base stays the parent's revision. A draft's base is read from the ref's entity or the one it was forked from, by `CommitEditorDrafts`, `SaveEditorDraft` and `GetRevision` alike (`draftBase` in `db/library.ts`), so typing on and the save dialog's comparison work on the fork, and a field the parent changed since the draft started still conflicts there.
  - It copies no history (the fork's starts at the fork), no `source_path`/`source_hash` (a re-import never reaches it), no archive state, chats or `last_used_at`.
  - It's refused (`RefOperationRefused`, `EditRefused` over RPC) for a display name and version another of the user's cards has, live or archived and not in the bin, checked in the same transaction as the write: the same rule the client checks (below), so a fork made through the RPC can't skip it. Other kinds are refused: cards only, as the owner decided (Q3).
- **`ForkCardForChat`** (`db/cardForkWrites.ts`, `ChatSetupApi`): one writer transaction resolves the selected fork's state at the supplied slot, filters purged references with the same routing rules as `GetCast`, requires the source card in its cast, and copies the revision that `cardInChat` reads (including a pinned revision). It shares `ForkCard`'s copy steps and name/version clash check; the new entity records `forked_in_chat_id`. A fork-owned `card.swap` at slot null replaces the card through the existing lineage rules (§3.8). `note.activate`/`note.deactivate` events for copied notes are replayed onto the copies at their original slots and version attachments, owned by the selected fork, including switches on unselected versions or deleted slots so swiping or restoring retains them. Queued message speakers and reply speakers, and the composer's speaker and next-reply choice, move to the new card **only in that fork**; sibling forks' queues and composers remain theirs. Queue row versions advance so an old queue editor cannot silently undo the swap. Already-written message versions keep their speakers. `GetCast.cards` also returns the cards named by the line’s swaps so messages still show their original card’s name and avatar after a reload; `participants` and `playedByMe` define the active cast, card tabs and note groups. The API publishes `State`, the changed composer choices and the queue, and refreshes cache estimates; the client invalidates library, chat-card and note queries. A failure at any write rolls back the card, notes, swap, queue and composer updates together.
- **`SetCardParent`** ("Mark as a fork of…", or with null "Unlink"): sets or clears the relationship of a live card, `forked_from_revision_id` null since nothing was copied. Neither card's content changes. Refused: the parent not the user's live card (`NotFound`: another user's, another kind, in the bin), the card itself, or a parent that is already its descendant (a recursive walk up from the parent). Marking a card with the parent it has changes nothing. Both return the family.
- **The family** (`cardFamily` in `db/library.ts`, read `CardFamily`): every card connected by `forked_from_entity_id`, one recursive query from a live card of the user's: up its parents to the root (the member with none), then down from the root to every fork of a fork, so the tree is the same from any member. `UNION` keeps even a cycle the writer should have refused from looping. Each member has its parent, the ref it opens in, its display name and version, its state (`live`, `archived`, `binned`, `purged`), what it was forked from (revision, chat, when), and the user's live chats it joined with the last message among them (the grid's index seek, as `CardUsage`). Binned and purged members stay in the tree: a purged one has no ref or names left, and its forks keep it as their parent, without the revision they copied. A card in the bin can't be asked for (`NotFound`), as anything else in it.

**Three-way merge** (`merge3`, shared so the server's merge and the resolver's preview always agree):
- Objects merge per key. Only own keys count, so `__proto__` or `constructor` are plain data.
- **Arrays merge per element when every element has a unique `id`, `identifier` or `uid`.** That covers preset prompts and order, older cards' modules and lorebook entries, so additions on either side survive and deletions apply unless the other side edited the element (then it's a conflict). **Order** follows the side that reordered the elements all three share, or ours if neither did, with the other side's new elements placed after their predecessor there. Both reordering differently is a conflict at the array, and ours' order is kept.
- Other arrays are atomic.
- **Text changed on both sides merges word by word** with diff3 (`node-diff3`), keeping whitespace. Edits to different sentences of the same prompt combine; overlapping edits conflict. A conflict keeps ours in the merged value and is reported with its path, e.g. `["prompts", "identifier=main", "content"]`, and each side's value; a side with no value there (added or deleted) has none.
- `test/library.test.ts` and `packages/shared/test/merge.test.ts` run the owner's case from DESIGN §4 end to end: a softened impersonation rule and an added `{{summary}}` prompt survive an upstream re-import that rewrote another sentence of the same prompt, changed a sampler and added a prompt.

**Known limits:**
- Arrays without element identities, like a card's `alternate_greetings` or `tags`, are atomic: changing them on both sides is a conflict.
- `ListLibrary` filters by kind, tag and archive state; card discovery/search is in §12, and full-text history search in §3.13. The Library page has no general full-document search control or server-side library-text search RPC. Importing and exporting files is §11; editing, and "used by N chats", §14.

### 3.10 Drafts

Drafts with server-persisted undo history, for every text field (DESIGN §6.2). The patch model and history policy are in `@rp/shared/drafts.ts`, storage in `db/drafts.ts` (shared by the writer and readers), and the history table in migration `0003_draft_history`, which replaced the `drafts.undo_log` blob.

**Invariants.** DESIGN's "input boxes can be trusted with your life" is these five, which every change to drafts, the composer (§12) or the editors (§14.6) keeps:
1. **Nothing typed is lost.** Every text typed into a composer or an editor field is, at every moment, reachable from the UI: in the field, in its draft's history, in the recovered texts, or in the message, queued entry or revision it was committed into. Until it reaches the server, this browser's copy of it counts, since opening the field brings it back. Only the user removing it from a field that showed it (typing over it, picking an earlier state) or deleting its target for good takes it away.
2. **A commit names exactly what it commits.** A send (posted or queued) names the draft its text came from by id and the session it was typed in; the writer derives the commit text from the message content or Generate direction actually stored, and binds the draft to that chat's composer; an editor commit names each field's draft by id and the row version this tab saved, which changes with every change of its text. Only that draft, holding exactly that, is committed; no other draft or text changes.
3. **A save names the draft it was typed into**: its id, row version and session, never only a key or a tip. Another draft there now (the one named moved to a variant, and a new one started) is a conflict: the save goes on top, the other text one step back.
4. **A browser copy goes only once acknowledged.** A copy in IndexedDB is deleted only when the server acknowledged exactly that copy (by its id), or when a newer copy of the same draft (a composer's draft, an editor's field, a summary's edit), typed from its text, replaces it; never on a failure, a `NotFound`, navigation, expiry, capacity pressure, logout, another account signing in, or a newer copy of another draft. The user can inspect, copy out, retry or explicitly delete a copy in Browser recovery. Authored settings and pending first messages also have server drafts (below). Legacy browser-only copies remain until their editor transfers them, they are sent or saved, or the user deletes them; Browser recovery links pending-message copies to their page. After a pending first send succeeds, additional unsent text is atomically re-filed as a browser copy addressed to the saved chat before navigation; opening its composer atomically replaces that handoff with a draft-addressed copy, retained until its exact save is acknowledged. Legacy browser-only copies and first-send handoffs don't hold up logout, which keeps the session only while a copy that has a server draft to go to can't be saved; they wait for the user's next login in this browser.
5. **Lost text is found where it was lost.** Text a save went over is one step back in that field's history. Every draft save, restore and discard returns recovery metadata (`Draft.recovered`); composer saves also return their recovery boolean (agreeing with `Draft.recovered > 0`), and keepalive returns `x-drafts-recovered`. A write that sends text to the recovered texts says so (`recovered`), and the composer or editor shows it, with a link to the bin. A replay that displaces text saved elsewhere returns `Draft.displaced` (the other text and its save time, otherwise null). The client keeps the conflict beside that composer or field until acknowledged or switched; typing and refresh do not dismiss it.

One client rule carries 1 and 3: **a field's base** (the draft id, row version and session its saves name) **changes only together with the text it shows, or when the server answers a save with exactly the text sent.** Text typed on a base the server has moved past is saved on top of the newer text, never as typing on it; so a stale answer (a replayed save's, a refresh's) can't make a tab's save coalesce over text it never showed. A copy that replaces another names that copy's base; once the server confirms the text a newer copy was typed from, the copy is made again on the confirmed version.

**The model test** (`test/protocol/drafts.model.test.ts`) runs two tabs, two chat destinations and two browser-copy owners with the real `ComposerDraft` and `EditorStore`, in-memory IndexedDB, and a real server with database workers and sockets. Random operations cover typing, timers, refreshes during unsaved/in-flight edits, account-scoped recovery/logout flushes, keepalive, reloads, offline periods, reordered/lost/duplicated requests, sends and queued directions, branches and appends, undo/revert/revisions/conflicts/variants, bin restore, chat and branch purge, and real server restarts. The oracle compares complete text against reachable fields, their histories, recovered texts, stored message/direction and revision values; an obsolete composer's row is not a UI endpoint. It checks commit session, destination chat and text, browser-copy owner partitioning, both composer recovery surfaces, each copy's complete draft/version/session/revision base against its ancestry and exact acknowledged text, including identical pending and in-flight payloads, every draft-writing response's recovery metadata, receipt persistence and exactly-once replay, and explicit copy ancestry with historical acknowledgements even after a successor copy is removed. A shared test clock advances the writer's history clock without main-thread SQLite writes. Directed cases exercise whitespace through keepalive/reload, stale identical-text sessions, receipt replay across restart, background movement, both queue purge paths and retention beyond 1000 states. They also send with another chat's identity, flush copies under two owners, refresh a field before holding its save and typing further, and deliver a `next` save after two completed queued sends. Directed replay cases require the client conflict to remain through more typing until acknowledged or switched; switching similar texts must have a separate undo step. `e2e/offlineDrafts.spec.ts` stops the real server by PID, types into composers and editor fields, closes persistent browser contexts, and restarts the same data directory and port. Six scenarios reopen the original profile with ordinary recovery, another profile's conflicting draft, or a message/revision committed elsewhere: they check server persistence, both conflict times, preview, switch and undo, the sent message and recovered offline text, and the editor revision conflict picker. The browser suite exercises real account changes, expired sessions, recovery retries and logout notices. They hold an older save while Send clears the box, replace copies through overlapping completion callbacks, reject unrelated-field ancestry, and remove an acknowledged successor before its failed predecessor. `npm run check` runs these plus 4 seeds of 150 steps; `DRAFT_MODEL_SEEDS`, `DRAFT_MODEL_STEPS`, and `DRAFT_MODEL_SEED` select longer runs or one seed. Failures shrink to a short trace (`DRAFT_MODEL_DEBUG` adds drafts and copies, `DRAFT_MODEL_SHRINK=0` skips shrinking).

**Keys.** A draft is keyed by user and target:
- `composer` (a chat, with the fork in `field`; `composerKey()` builds it)
- `version_edit` (a version)
- `scratchpad` (a slot)
- `snippet` (a note ref, with the viewed fork in `field` for a chat-scoped snippet)
- `entity_field` (a ref plus a JSON path in `field`)
- `summary_edit` (a summary)
- `user_field` (a private user workspace key plus a field; settings and text before its target exists)
- `queue_edit` (a chat, with the queued item id in `field`; kept after posting or withdrawal)
- `insert` (a chat, with the fork and the anchor in `field` as `fork:anchor`, the anchor empty above the first message; `insertDraftKey`): the text of a message being inserted (spec/MESSAGE-INSERT.md §3.1). Its owner is the user's chat with a live fork of it and an anchor of the chat; it's listed with the chat's message editors and removed with the chat. `InsertMessage` commits it

The first save checks that the target belongs to the user. Independently supplied draft ids are checked even when the enclosing chat/ref belongs to the caller: an existing foreign id is `NotFound` before version comparisons, for saves, restores, discards, editor revision commits and keepalive, with no mutation or recovery. `copyFrom` must name an owned composer in the same chat. Editor revision bases must belong to the ref's own entity. The legitimate late-save exception is an absent draft id after movement/purge: a composer save to a still-owned chat recovers its submitted text; an editor save can recreate an owned field while retaining its original base. No foreign row is treated as absent. Drafts are never visible to other users.

**Saving.**
- The client saves debounced while the user types (`SaveDraft`). Each save supplies the draft id, row version and session as last seen, with nulls before a field has a draft. Composer saves always name an existing identity and a durable operation id.
- **The writer decides a conflict in the save's transaction** (`drafts.ts` `saveOnto`), one rule for every kind of draft, live or from a closing page; no client reads and retries:
  - Typed on the draft as it is: saved, coalescing with typing as below.
  - Another tab changed the text since (`drafts.text_version`, the row version the text last changed at, past the one named): saved as its own entry, marked `displaced`, so the other tab's text is one step back, and goes to the recovered texts when the session ends or the entry is dropped, however short it is.
  - The session it was typed in has ended since (`drafts.session` counts them; each commit or discard ends one): a field's text starts the next session with the revision it was typed against, so committing it meets the edit conflict on that base; a composer's goes to the recovered texts (below).
  So a stale tab never overwrites newer text unseen, and nothing it goes over is lost.
- The server diffs the new text against the stored text into one reversible patch (`start`, `removed`, `inserted`) and records it in `draft_history`. Patch boundaries never split a surrogate pair, so stored text stays valid UTF-8.
- History is a row per entry, not a blob, so a save never rewrites the whole history.
- Text is capped at 1,000,000 characters (`DraftTooLarge`).

**History.**
- **Typing coalesces.** A save within 5 s of the last entry extends it, until the entry spans 60 s, so continuous typing is one entry per minute rather than one per save.
- **A destructive change always gets its own entry,** so the text before it is one step back. Destructive means removing more than 200 characters or more than half the text, e.g. select-all plus a stray key.
- `DraftHistory` lists the restorable states newest first, with lengths and short excerpts. `GetDraftState` returns one state's full text, built by undoing newer entries from the current text.
- Walking history back needs only lengths, since destructiveness depends only on them. Earlier text is rebuilt only as far back as needed, as slices of the current text, so no step copies the whole text.
- `RestoreDraftState` moves the draft back to that state, and records the restore as its own entry, so it can be undone too.
- At most 1000 entries and 1,000,000 changed characters are kept per draft, oldest dropped first. The text from before a dropped destructive or displaced entry goes to the recovered-text section first.

**Committing.** `AppendMessage`, `SendOrQueue` (posted or queued), `AddVersion` and `EditRef` take an optional `commitDraft`: the draft's id and session (`drafts.ts` `commit`). The storage boundary derives the commit text from the stored version content/direction, queued content/direction or revision field, and checks the draft's target against that destination. Insertion commits also match the exact fork and anchor field; appends and queued sends accept an insert draft only below their named tip, and composer drafts only in their source fork. An unrelated draft is neither ended nor returned as the send's draft, even when its chat, text and session match. `insertDraftReview.test.ts` covers posted and queued Write/Generate paths and mismatches within a chat. The draft is committed **in the same transaction** only if it holds exactly that text in that session, so a reload never shows already-sent text in the composer, and a send never ends text it didn't take: another tab's text typed on since, a replacement draft at the same tip, a session sent already. Otherwise it stays as it is, and the write returns it as it left it, with how many texts committing sent to the recovered texts (`recovered`). `ClearDraft` and `RestoreDraftState` name the draft's id as well as its row version.

**History only lives while a draft is being written** (owner, 2026-10-10). Once committed, the text is preserved in a version or revision, so a commit or discard deletes the draft's history and empties its text. The row stays, so other tabs' row versions keep working. What a commit or discard would otherwise lose goes to the recycle bin's **recovered-text section** (§3.11):
- the text from just before **each** destructive change in the session (hours of typing in a card field wiped, then saved)
- the text another tab's save went over (a displaced entry)
- a discarded draft's text

This applies to every kind of draft: what a bad save loses is the editing session, and the previous version being intact doesn't change that. The next save after a commit starts a fresh session. `ListActiveDrafts` lists drafts with unsent text.

**The composer belongs to a fork** (DESIGN §6.2, owner; spec/FORK-INDEPENDENCE-DELTA.md unit 2):
- Its key is the chat plus the fork (`composerKey`; migration 0053 moved each from the tip it was keyed by to the fork ending there). A recovered text whose fork isn't known has the field empty.
- Each chat and each fork has its own composer, and a half-typed message waits where it was left. Two forks at the same message have two.
- Appending, deleting and restoring move a fork's tip, never its composer, so nothing has to move or merge: a model reply leaves text typed meanwhile where it is, and a send's recovery text stays with its fork.
- `OpenComposer` (by fork, the current one if null) with `copyFrom` copies the text into a new fork's composer when one is started, never overwriting text already there. A new composer also inherits its source composer's speaker and reply pick; an existing one keeps its own choices, including a reset to turn order.
- Switching between existing forks carries nothing.

**Saving composer text names the draft it was typed into**, not the fork (`SaveComposer`, `drafts.ts` `saveComposer`, by the rule above):
- `OpenComposer` makes an empty draft for a fork that has none, so every text has a draft to name. A fork that has one is read in a reader; only a new one takes the writer.
- A save carries the draft's id, and the row version and session the client had. The text goes onto that draft.
- A save from a session that has ended (its text was sent, or queued, from another tab), or onto a draft that is gone, isn't taken as the start of the next session: its text goes to the recovered texts, and the reply says so (`recovered`) with the draft as it is now.
- `next` marks text typed after a send of that session that may not have arrived (a page closed while it was out): it starts the session the send ended, or, if the send never arrived, goes on top of the text it would have taken, which is then displaced. Pending saves carry `operationId`; the writer records its receipt in the save transaction (`composer_save_receipts`, migration `0015`). Replaying it returns the current draft without applying or recovering the text again, even after that text was sent. Its original displacement metadata is reconstructed from history while that session and entry remain, so a retry still explains the conflict; receipts keep only the session, history position and time. The enclosing recovery boolean repeats the receipt's original recovery flag; when a draft remains, `Draft.recovered` repeats that flag as 0 or 1. A fresh save reports the actual recovered count. Draft reads and writes that displace no text return `displaced: null`. The displaced text's time comes from the previous history entry (the initial draft's creation time before its first change), rather than a client comparison. Receipts live until the chat is deleted. All composer saves require `operationId` and `next`; there is no identity-less replay path. A next save can reopen only the immediately following session.
- `SendMessage` and `Generate` name the draft as `composer` (id, session) and return it as the send left it (`draft`): committed, or still active with another tab's newer text.

**Cleanup.** Draft targets aren't foreign keys, since one column points into several tables. Triggers delete a draft when its target (chat, slot, version, summary, ref) is deleted (`test/schema.test.ts`).

**Authored settings and pending text (backlog U02/U05).** `EditorApi` exposes `GetUserDraft`, `SaveUserDraft`, `UserDraftHistory`, `RestoreUserDraft` and `EndUserDraft`. They reuse the named worker operations above; the key is `user_field`, scoped by the authenticated user. Target strings name private workspaces, not references granting access to a connection, model or chat. Requests also name the account that typed them; RPC and closing-page writes refuse a different logged-in account, even on the first save before a draft id exists. Foreign draft identities are still refused. The baseline and frozen equivalence fixture include this kind. These workspaces survive removal of their named target, preserving unsaved text.

`features/editor/userDraft.tsx` shares a subscribed store across each editor's lifetime. It saves changes in order, first recording the starting text so destructive edits have an initial undo state. Each typed change has an IndexedDB `userDraft` copy, including the observed draft id, row version and session. Closing sends its pending copy through `POST /library/drafts`; Browser recovery and logout retry that same payload. Opening reads the server draft and replays copies without refreshing their bases; a device with no browser copies reads the active server text. Old account-instruction `setting` copies transfer when that editor opens. The displayed text keeps its own draft base through refused replays and typing during undo/restore; an unseen server response cannot advance that base. Each save acknowledgement immediately re-files later typing on the acknowledged base, even if another queued operation delays its save. The UI offers Undo field change and a field-history list with restore actions; both use server history and leave refused local text in place until it reaches the server. Displaced text has a persistent notice with preview, switch and acknowledgement actions; recovery results link to recovered texts in the bin. Open private draft editors also receive saves, restores, endings and closing-page saves from other sessions through `UserDraftChanged` on the account-scoped `SubscribeNotices` stream. The editor registers `editor.user-drafts` in `userNoticeReceivers`, independently of optional reply-alert channels. Clean editors take newer text and its observed base; an editor with queued or refused local writes retains its text and shows a preview with Load the other device’s text and Keep my text. Loading first saves the local text into field history; keeping writes on the original observed base, preserving the displaced remote text through the normal draft rules. An ending event contains empty storage, not the saved setting or JSON form. It leaves the displayed text intact and offers Keep my text or Reload saved value. Reload first persists the displayed text, discards that exact draft version into recovered texts, and reloads the page to read the actual saved resource; a refusal or typing during the choice prevents the reload. It never feeds an ended row’s empty content into a form. Events at or below the observed row version are ignored. `UserDraftSync` on subscription/reconnect and backlog overflow refreshes open workspaces; account isolation applies to text events too. `client/test/userDraft.test.ts`, `server/test/notices.test.ts` and `e2e/privateDraftSync.spec.ts` cover live refresh, out-of-order events, reconnect reads, unsaved conflicts and both choices, including fresh browser contexts at 1440 and 390 pixels.

The account and per-fork summarization instructions retain their explicit Save buttons; the chat instruction's workspace names both the chat and viewed fork. Saving flushes the submitted text, writes the setting, and ends only that draft version. These are separate writer transactions: if ending fails after the setting saves, the draft remains available. Text typed during Save stays in its browser copy and queued draft; failed setting saves leave the active draft untouched. “Use account default” discards the instruction draft only after the reset succeeds, recovering its text.

The same mechanism protects connection settings (including name and base URL), the model's entire custom-settings form (including incomplete numbers and invalid JSON), new connection and model forms, card creation/fork/rename names (in the library and chat), the empty-chat title, chat/group/branch naming, and credential names and allowed-address text. Multi-field settings and small name forms keep a JSON snapshot as their undoable field, so restoring brings back the complete form, including the connection/model/ref row version it was edited from. For these snapshots, coalescing stays within the same changed JSON field paths. Destructiveness is also measured against each string field, so erasing a short name cannot disappear in a patch coalesced against a much larger settings form; its prior snapshot is marked for recovery and retained on commit. Parsing this extra form structure is bounded to shallow, small trees; raw instruction/prose drafts retain the ordinary string policy. Connection and model forms advance later snapshots' settings row version only after their own Save succeeds, including queued typing and browser copies, so the next edit remains saveable after a reload. A model editor kept open in the chat panel also advances its committed snapshot after its own Save. “Load the latest” on a stale model draft recovers that draft and reads the current model instead of reopening the same stale base. Secret inputs are excluded. Existing connection sections and account/chat setting contributions remain registered through their existing slots. Search/filter text stays transient.

Before a chat exists, `StartChatPage` uses its card or group pending-copy key as the private workspace, with field `First message`. A reload or another device finds that prose without creating a chat. The existing browser copy and first-send carry protocol stay in place: after a successful first send, extra text is addressed to the created chat before the pending server draft ends. No provider call is started by draft storage.

`client/test/userDraft.test.ts` covers refused replay bases, typing during undo, immediate copy rebasing, persistent displacement and form versions after Save. `server/test/userDrafts.test.ts` covers persisted history, destructive recovery, scopes and version-fenced ending; the isolation matrix probes all five RPCs in both directions. `e2e/userDrafts.spec.ts` covers server reads after panel close/reload and from fresh browser contexts, both instruction editors' refused and delayed saves, model JSON still being typed, connection edits typed during Save across reload, fork-dialog retry after a refused draft write, persistent conflict preview/switch and recovery links, and the desktop/phone light/dark layouts.

The streaming above covers private `user_field` workspaces (authored settings, names and pending first messages). Library fields, message edits, summaries and existing-chat composers keep their own refresh/recovery lifecycles; they do not subscribe to private-workspace text events.

### 3.11 Recycle bin

**Insertion boundaries and previews.** A deleted tip that retains a shown insertion remains a valid parent for `SendOrQueue`, as for `AppendMessage`. Sending or generating proceeds immediately past a deleted anchor, even when its selected version is still generating: the queue never waits on a deleted reply. State reads accept a deleted insertion anchor (its replies still need the state at that boundary), and next-context reads accept an explicitly named deleted tip of a live fork. `lastShown` in `db/forks.ts` supplies the display-end text for `ChatSummaries`, fork bin items and affected-fork warnings, as for the fork list; `ChatSummaries` message counts use display positions when the fork places insertions. The sidebar's `ShellLibrary` walks those same pieces, consuming structural runs once and reading the single inserted pieces with the fork's selections and deletions; its preview, visible-message count, navigation endpoint and activity include inserted messages. Chats without insertion rows retain the structural walk. `forksShowing` and `shownElsewhere` exclude purged forks explicitly: clearing a purged fork's tip alone does not remove an inherited insertion above the first message from its pieces. `test/bin.test.ts` covers sends and Generate at deleted complete/generating tips, preview text, purge retention and amend copies, exact fork counts and byte sizes, row-version bumps and restore/fork deletion/purge/eviction interleaved through lineage compaction. `test/deletedTip.test.ts` exercises cast, next context, regenerate and send through RPC with a synthetic provider, and a cast join and a macro binding made at the deleted tip reaching the next turn's request after the insertion. `e2e/insertedBin.spec.ts` covers deleted-tip sends and swipes, the bin warning and restore at 1440 and 390 pixels in both themes.

Deleting is deliberate and fundamentally non-destructive (DESIGN §6.2): deletes go to a per-user bin, from which they can be restored or deleted for good. Schema in migrations `0004_recycle_bin` and `0009_bin_index`, logic in `db/bin.ts` (migration `0014_deleted_subtree_roots` retains subtree barriers after purge), handlers in `db/binWrites.ts`, types in `@rp/shared/bin.ts`. The writer builds one bin, with the draft store it recovers text for, and shares it across its handler groups.

**Deleting marks, it doesn't remove.**
- A delete creates one `bin_items` row. A chat or entity is marked with `deleted_at` and the item's id. A message, swipe or fork is deleted in a fork (below, since unit 5); branches deleted before that carry the marks of their whole subtree and restore as they did.
- Every read skips marked rows, and what a fork deleted in its own view (`slotDeletedIn`, `versionDeletedIn`, `shownVersionDeletedIn` in `db/forkState.ts`). Paths walk through deleted messages without showing them, so their replies stay where they were. Writes treat deleted things as `NotFound`.
- Restoring clears the marks, so things come back exactly where they were, with their versions and attached state.
- **Deleting is per fork** (spec/FORK-INDEPENDENCE-DELTA.md §2.4, unit 5; `db/bin.ts`). A message or swipe deleted in a fork is a `delete_slot` or `delete_version` override of that fork with `flag = 1` and the bin item, whose origin names the fork (`forkId`, `forkName`); restoring writes the row with `flag = 0`. Another fork that shows it keeps it, in its path, picker and context. Only when no unpurged fork shows it, including a fork in the bin (`forksShowing` in `db/forks.ts`) is the slot or swipe also marked (`deleted_at`, `bin_item_id`) under the item, which changes nothing another restorable fork shows and keeps the walks that read marks as they were (alternates in the switcher, tips following a restore). The marks are frozen otherwise (triggers in `db/baseline.ts`: `slots_deletion_per_fork`, `versions_deletion_per_fork`, `slots_hide_frozen`). Deleting a fork's tip message moves that fork's tip up to the nearest message it still shows (`relocateLeaf`, per fork's view), and only that fork's. It stops at a deleted message whose gap still holds an inserted message the fork shows and hasn't deleted (`liveGaps` in `db/bin.ts`: each such slot and its anchors up to a structural one), so that message stays on the display path; the tip stays on the deleted message, and an append in the fork goes below it, after its gap (`AppendMessage` accepts a deleted parent only when it's the fork's own tip, which happens only then; spec/MESSAGE-INSERT.md §2.4). Without insertions it's the walk it always was. **Inserted messages** (spec/MESSAGE-INSERT.md §3.1, unit 5) are deleted, restored and purged as any message: `forksShowing` and `shownElsewhere` read display paths, so a fork that shows one by inheriting its `insert` row counts for the mark and in `BinItemForks`. Restoring one in its fork puts it back in its gap, without moving the tip or making a fork, since whether the restored message is still on the fork's path is `onForkPath`. Making room keeps an item whose message a fork other than the deleting one still shows on its display path. `test/bin.test.ts` ("the bin with inserted messages") covers these, deleting a fork that inserted a message or was made at one, an anchor deleted mid-path and at the tip, and a random oracle through the writer (each fork a list; appends at its end, insertions below a shown message or above the first, forks at a shown message, deletions of any shown message, tips included) checking every fork's view after every step.
- **Restoring a message** deleted in a fork clears its row and any mark under fork items. The fork's tip that went up from it comes back down onto that message; if the fork went on since, the message isn't on its path any more, so it comes back as a fork of its own (`insertFork`: parent the fork, base the message's parent, tip the restored message), which doesn't become current. A swipe's restore writes `flag = 0` and normally leaves the selection. If a global purge removed this fork's last available swipe, its automatic `delete_slot` override records that purged selection in `value_id`; restoring a swipe clears that automatic deletion, selects the restored swipe, and brings its message back. Restoring a mark that a slot's own column holds, as it was: every live fork whose tip has nothing below it follows what came back, on the newest reply at each step (`followRestored`), and a branch brings back its forks as they were. `test/forkDeletion.test.ts`, `test/binApi.test.ts`.

**Deletes need no confirmation:** each returns the bin item for the "deleted, open the bin" toast. The behaviors:
- **Swipes:** `DeleteVersion` deletes one swipe in a fork (`forkId`, else the fork through its message), the normal delete (owner). If the fork showed it, the previous swipe shows, as in ST, or the next one if it was the first. Deleting the only version the fork has left deletes the message in that fork. A fork's copy (§3.8) is deleted as the swipe it stands in for; the bin item names that swipe, its size counts the copies, and a restore brings each fork's copy back in its place. Deleting it for good empties the copies and their histories too (owner, 2026-10-27: delete for good erases the text everywhere).
- **Messages and forks:** `DeleteMessage` deletes one message in a fork, and its replies stay. `DeleteFork` deletes one fork (owner, 2026-10-27: only that fork goes; forks made from it keep their shared history): the fork is marked (`forks.deleted_at`, `bin_item_id`) under an item of kind `branch` whose origin names it, and the messages of its path that no other unpurged fork has (`sharedDepth` in `db/forks.ts`: below the deepest slot another restorable fork's path shares, including forks in the bin) are marked under the item, the first of them also `branch_deleted` (the barrier a deleted branch's root always was). Inserted messages count by display path (`insertedUnseen`): one the fork shows by its `insert` row is marked too when no other unpurged fork shows it, and one on its structural path past the shared part (it was made at or below it) is left unmarked when another fork shows it by its own row. The item's message count and size follow. Restoring the fork clears the marks other forks' items put on the inserted messages it shows, as on its structural path. A chat's last live fork isn't deleted (`CannotDeleteLastFork`). If it was the current fork, the live fork most recently current takes over (§3.8). Its composer and any save in flight stay its own, so Undo restores it with its draft.
- **Forks' tips:** deleting a message moves the tip of the fork that deleted it, if it ended there, to the nearest message that fork still shows; the forks' composers stay theirs. A Reload reads the current fork for a tab that was showing it; a tab explicitly showing another fork keeps it. The header follows the chat's current fork after deleting the one shown. Its menu's **Delete this fork** deletes the fork shown.
- **Chats and entities:** `DeleteChat` and `DeleteEntity`.

**Deleting for good** (`PurgeBinItem`, `EmptyBin`; the UI confirms these):
- **A chat** is deleted, with everything in it and its other bin items. A connection whose preset was one of its chat overrides loses that preset.
- **A fork** deleted on its own keeps its row, losing its name and tip (`purged_at`), as forks made from it read its overrides; the messages marked under its item are emptied, so another live or restorable fork keeps its history. A branch deleted before forks keeps its forks' rows the same way.
- **An entity** loses its refs, revisions and tags. The entity row stays, so messages that named it as their speaker still point somewhere.
- **A message, fork's messages or swipe** have their versions emptied: content, reasoning, display forms, amend history and attachments. Slots stay as placeholders, so the conversation's structure and any summaries over it remain valid. This is the one exception to model output being frozen: a version may be emptied only as part of a purge of its own bin item (§3.7). **A message or swipe deleted in a fork is erased in every fork** (owner, 2026-10-27, Q3 (b)), the ones that still show it included, after a warning that names them: the purge marks the slot (or swipe) under its item for every fork (`deleted_at` and `purged_at` mean deleted everywhere, `slotDeletedInSql`), a swipe that forks showed is replaced by another in each (`dropSelections`; one with none left deletes the message in its own view), and a fork whose tip it was moves up. The warning is `BinItemForks` (`@rp/shared/binApi.ts`: the forks other than the deleting one that show an item, forks in the bin included, each with its name and the start of its tip), read by the bin page's Delete for good and Empty the bin dialogs, which wait for a successful read and name every affected fork. Successful purges return affected chat ids from the writer and publish Reload, State and the updated Chat to open tabs, or Deleted for a removed chat. The dialogs send the affected fork ids with confirmation; the writer recomputes the set in the purge transaction and returns `BinChanged` if it changed. Internal callers may omit this optional acknowledgement; the regular UI only sends things to the bin. Making room (below) never erases what another fork shows: that item just leaves the bin, its deletion staying in its fork (an item with another bin deletion of the same content is skipped, so eviction cannot destroy that other item's restore). An ST re-import that forks a shared path (§13) copies deleted messages, swipes and branches with the original's bin item: restoring the item brings back every copy, and deleting it for good empties every copy (`test/stImportForkBin.test.ts`).
- `EmptyBin` takes the exact item ids shown for confirmation. In the writer transaction it compares them with the user's current bin; additions, removals or replacements return `BinChanged` without purging anything. The client loads all pages before enabling Empty the bin, captures the ids when the dialog opens, and reloads on closing a changed confirmation. Purging those ids is set-based; unposted queue text is recovered before any purge can remove it.

**Two independent sections**, each evicting its own oldest entries past its own limits, never the other's:
- **Deleted items** (`ListBin`): 10,000 items or 2 GB by default. Eviction deletes the oldest for good, never the item the delete just made, each in a savepoint: a purge that fails is rolled back, logged and skipped, so it never fails the delete that triggered it. If what can go can't make room, nothing goes and the bin stays over its limits (DESIGN §6.2, proposed).
- Sizes are UTF-8 bytes. A deleted item's is what deleting it for good frees: its versions' text columns, amend history and attachment alt text; for a chat also its rollouts and their payloads, summaries, state events and changesets; for an entity its revisions and their search text. Messages and swipes already in the bin count only under their own items.
- **Recovered text** (`ListRecoveredTexts`, `GetRecoveredText`, `DeleteRecoveredText`, `ClearRecoveredTexts`): 2,000 texts or 200 MB by default. It's produced passively by draft commits, discards, history retention and composer text saved after its draft was sent (§3.10), and by removing unposted queue entries, so it never shows a toast.
- Each entry records where it came from: a title, an excerpt and origin details for deleted items, a source label like `Composer in "Harbor"` for recovered text. So text can be copied out without restoring.
- Both lists page newest first, with a cursor of the last entry's time and id, so entries from the same millisecond are never skipped.
- **Configurable capacities** (U13): `GetBinLimits` reads the account's four positive safe-integer limits from `user_settings` (`bin.limits`), falling back to the defaults above. `ChangeBinLimit` changes one field in one writer transaction, preserving the others. The registered `bin.capacity` contribution in the bin page exposes item and byte limits independently for both sections. Saving a limit never purges anything; the writer reads the saved limits on later deletes and recovered-text additions and applies the existing eviction rules, including the oversized-item and shared-fork protections. `Database.layer({ binLimits })` supplies the same fallback to both reader and writer workers; reading or saving a single capacity preserves the other configured limits, including before an account has saved preferences. No new schema is needed. Restart, RPC validation, section independence and ownership are covered by `binLimits.test.ts`, `bin.test.ts` and the isolation matrix.

**The client's RPCs** (`@rp/shared/binApi.ts`, `api/bin.ts`): `ListBin`, `RestoreBinItem`, `PurgeBinItem`, `BinItemForks`, `EmptyBin`, `ListRecoveredTexts` (every source), `GetRecoveredText`, `DeleteRecoveredText`. Restoring something inside a chat publishes `Reload`, `State` and `Chat` to it, so open tabs read it again, its state included. **The bin page** (`/bin`, linked from the header and every delete's toast; `features/bin/`): deleted items, newest first, a chat named by its readable name and the day ST started it, never ST's file name (`binTitle` in `features/bin/titles.ts`, shared with the toast: labels are built from `kind`, `origin.chatTitle` and branch message counts, formatting the whole title with `readableChatTitle`; embedded quotes are preserved. Recovered sources use their known `sourceKind` prefix and one outer quoted title, preserving embedded quotes and leaving library-field labels unchanged), with Restore (then a link to where it went back to) and Delete for good, and Empty the bin, both confirmed with what deleting for good does to that kind; recovered text, each shown whole in a dialog to copy, and deleted for good after a confirmation.

### 3.12 Connections, models and the usage ledger

The provider layer's storage (spec/PROVIDERS.md §2, §3.1, §9.1), in `db/baseline.ts`.

**`credentials`** (migration `0006_credentials`): keys, each stored once and used by any number of connections.
- **The columns:** `key_enc` (encrypted, bound to `credential:<id>:key`), `key_hint` (its last 4 characters; empty for keys under 12, where 4 characters would be most of the key), a name, `origins_json`, the origins (scheme, host and port) the key may be sent to, and `connection_owned` (0008): the credential was made from a key typed into one connection.
- **The rule** (owner, 2026-10-11): a key is sent only to an origin its credential allows.
  - The origins are recorded when the key is entered.
  - Adding one takes the key typed again (`AllowCredentialHost`). The main thread checks it against the stored key with a constant-time comparison.
  - Code acting as the user without knowing the key (a stolen session, an XSS, a plugin in the page) can edit connections, but can't redirect a key to a new host.
  - Changing a path on the same host needs nothing.
  - **Replacing a key states its origins again** (`ReplaceCredentialKey` takes them). Origins approved under the old key don't carry over: otherwise a stolen session could swap in a junk key, approve its own host with it, and wait for the user to re-enter the real one.
    - **The client must make the user confirm each origin** in the replace-key dialog, starting from the hosts of the connections using the credential. It must not pre-fill and submit the stored list: a stolen session can replace the key and the origins together, and the server can't tell that from the user. A stolen session can also re-point those connections first, so the dialog flags every origin that wasn't in the credential's list before.
  - **Header values are bound the same way.** `headers_origin` (0008) records the origin they were set for, and they're refused anywhere else until set again. Gateway tokens, Azure's `api-key` and Cloudflare Access secrets live in headers. Headers from before 0008 have no origin and must be set again.
  - **A key typed into a connection** (`SetConnectionKey`) is bound to the base URL of the row version the user saw. A stale form is a `StaleWrite`, so a key isn't bound to a URL someone changed in the meantime. `SetConnectionHeaders` works the same way, and both pass the `StaleWrite` to the client as itself. The connection's previous key is deleted if the connection owned it and nothing else uses it, so a rotated (possibly leaked) key doesn't stay at rest. Purging a connection deletes its own key the same way. A credential the user created and attached is never deleted this way.
  - **Redirects stay within the origin** (`Network.fetchFor`). undici strips only `authorization` and cookies on a cross-origin redirect, so `x-api-key`, `x-goog-api-key` and custom headers would follow an open redirect on an allowed host. Same-origin redirects are followed (at most 5), each routed afresh using the request's pinned network settings. A redirect to another origin is a `RedirectRefused` error, reported as `unusable` and never retried. Google uses the same redirect policy and pinned transport.
  - **A connection's own proxy can't carry a key over `http`.** Setting one needs no key, and over `http` the proxy reads every byte. Such a connection is `unusable` until it uses `https` or the instance proxy.
  - **Why:** keys are money, so making them easy to steal makes the frontend worth attacking. ST is a target for exactly this reason.
  - **Switching it off** is possible only from outside the app: `KEY_HOST_BINDING=false`. Most settings are meant to be changeable from inside, including by the user's agent; security settings are the exception.
  - **Server-side code can still read keys** (spec/PLUGINS.md §6.1). This protects against everything that acts through the browser.

**`connections`**: one provider kind, one wire API, one base URL, and at most one credential per row.
- `kind` and `api` follow pi-ai's catalog, so the shared schema will validate them rather than a CHECK list.
- `credential_id` references the key the connection sends (`ON DELETE SET NULL`). The database allows only the connection owner's credentials.
- **Other secrets are columns of their own,** written encrypted:
  - `headers_enc`, with only `header_names_json` in the clear, because header values often carry credentials
  - `proxy_url_enc`
- **The database keeps them consistent:**
  - `proxy_mode = 'custom'` always has a proxy URL, and the other modes never do
  - a purged connection (`purged_at`) holds no credential, headers, proxy or base URL; it stays as a tombstone so old messages and ledger rows still name their model
- **The per-connection key columns are retired.** `api_key_enc` and `key_hint` stay in `connections`, but triggers keep them empty. They can go the next time the baseline is edited.
- `cache_revision` (0013) is a non-secret cache identity revision. Endpoint, route, credential, headers, compatibility and deletion/restoration changes advance it; instance network edits advance all connections. Snapshots pin the id and revision, without transport settings or secrets.
- `deleted_at` and `bin_item_id` are for the recycle bin, whose `bin_items.kind` now includes `connection`. The table was rebuilt for that, keeping its rows.

**`models`**: what the upstream reports (`upstream_json`, rewritten by every refresh) and what the user edited (`overrides_json`, never touched by a refresh) are separate columns, merged when read. A model the list stops reporting gets `missing_since` instead of being deleted. A model never moves to another connection or key. Assembly pins the reasoning/developer-role flags and a canonical digest of all resolved request-conversion capabilities (reasoning, vision, tools, thinking levels, output limit, compatibility and sampling). Arbitrary compatibility values stay out of snapshots; any capability change conservatively invalidates the prefix estimate.

**`llm_requests`**: one row per request to a model, retries and failed attempts included.
- **Opened as `running`** before the request is sent, and closed with its status, timings, normalized token counts (`input_uncached`, `cache_read`, `cache_write` with its long-TTL subset `cache_write_long`, `output` with its `reasoning` subset), the provider's raw usage object, any provider-reported cost, and the computed cost with its currency and formula. `running` exactly when `ended_at` is unset.
- **Unknown counts are `NULL`, never zero.** Provider-reported numbers have no CHECKs, so a quirky upstream can never make the closing write fail and leave a billed request unrecorded.
- **A closed request is a record:** only its cost can be recomputed, and a cost always names its currency and formula.
- **Attribution stays inside the user's data:** a request names only its own user's chat, a rollout and a version of that chat, and a model of its connection. Attribution can be cleared, never moved.
- **The ledger outlives what it's attributed to:** deleting a chat for good keeps its requests and clears their chat, rollout and version (`ON DELETE SET NULL`, indexed so a large chat deletes quickly).
- Indexed by user and by connection over time, for totals and later limits. The inspector explicitly uses the rollout index, then sorts that rollout's attempts; its query plan is tested.
- A request-local secret inventory is populated when keys, headers and proxy settings resolve. It includes all header values, custom and instance proxy URLs, encoded/decoded usernames and passwords, and Basic credentials. The prepared transport pins the same network settings across redirects and retries, so capture cannot miss a proxy changed between preparation and HTTP. This inventory scrubs both captured text/names and ledger metadata.
- Provider-controlled `response_model` and `upstream` are scrubbed with every nonempty request secret before being capped at 256 characters. The inspector projects only status, displayed counts, usage source and cost, so legacy metadata cannot expose a key through it.

**Operations** (`db/providerWrites.ts`, `db/providerReads.ts`; types in `@rp/shared/providers.ts` and `@rp/shared/usage.ts`):
- **Connections:**
  - `CreateConnection`: the caller picks the id, since secrets are encrypted bound to it
  - `UpdateConnection`: with `expectedRowVersion`
  - `SetConnectionKey` (a typed key or none, with `expectedRowVersion`), `SetConnectionCredential`, `SetConnectionHeaders` (with `expectedRowVersion`), `SetConnectionProxy`
  - `DeleteConnection`: through the bin. A purge leaves the tombstone described above.
  - `ListConnections`, `GetConnection`: secrets appear only as `hasKey`, `keyHint` and header names
  - `ResolveConnection`: the ciphertext and the credential's origins, for the provider layer only
- **Credentials:**
  - `CreateCredential`, `RenameCredential`
  - `ReplaceCredentialKey`: updates every connection using it, with the origins stated again
  - `AddCredentialOrigin`
  - `DeleteCredential`: connections using it are left without a key
  - `ListCredentials`: with how many connections use each
  - `ResolveCredential`: the ciphertext only, for checking a retyped key
- **Models:**
  - `SyncFetchedModels`: adds new models, refreshes `upstream_json`, and marks unlisted ones missing. Models only ever added by hand are never marked.
  - `AddUserModel`
  - `EditModel`: with `expectedRowVersion`
  - `SetModelHidden`
  - `ListModels`, `GetModel`
  - `resolveModel` (shared) merges the sources, user edits first. It reports each field's source, and fields no source knows as `unknown` with a safe default.
- **Ledger:**
  - `OpenLlmRequest`, `CloseLlmRequest`
  - `InterruptRunningLlmRequests`, for startup
  - `ListLlmRequests`
- **Ownership:** as in §3.8, everything on another user's connection, model or ledger row is `NotFound` (`test/providers.test.ts` runs every operation as a second user).
  - A connection's preset override must be one of the user's own live refs. Another user's ref, and a ref that doesn't exist, are the same `NotFound`, so ref ids can't be probed.
  - A key given at creation is stored in the same transaction as the connection.
  - `ListLlmRequests` pages by `(started_at, id)`, so requests started in the same millisecond aren't skipped.
  - A model list that repeats a key keeps the last entry.
- **Generation rollups** (`db/generationWrites.ts`, `FinishRollout`): the transaction sums the rollout’s `llm_requests` counts and cost into `rollouts` and writes the same totals, request count and currency to its version’s `usage_json` (§15).

---

### 3.13 History edits

Fork independence unit 8 (`shared/historyApi.ts`, `db/historyReads.ts`, `db/historyWrites.ts`, `api/history.ts`) implements DESIGN §10.3's mechanical editing backend and search panel.

- `PreviewHistory` reads only a live owned fork's path. Messages and unselected swipes have separate switches; summaries include every sealed, unsuperseded summary covering that path, using the fork's effective revision. Version reads use the same replacement and deletion overlay as the picker. Pages inspect at most 100 versions within a 100-depth window; a cursor holds depth and version sequence, or summary revision sequence. No whole path of texts is transferred to the main thread. Literal ASCII whole-word searches use FTS5 as a prefilter, also scanning non-ASCII texts because JavaScript word boundaries differ from unicode61. Other literals and regexes scan the windows. Regex compilation, matching and capture replacement run in a reader-only VM with a 150 ms deadline, including a single pathological match. Invalid patterns and excessive per-text match counts return `HistoryRejected`. Multiple pairs match the original text; non-overlapping matches are used, earliest position first and pair order breaking ties. Replacements support captures, named captures, whole-match/prefix/suffix substitutions and preserve case. As in the editor, matching uses Unicode code points and letter/number word boundaries, falling back to plain regex mode for legacy escapes; preserve case leaves uncased matches alone.
- **Inserted messages** (spec/MESSAGE-INSERT.md §3.3, unit 8). A fork that places insertions (`placedInsertions` non-empty) is searched along its display path (§3.8 "Display paths"): `PreviewHistory`'s windows are 100 positions read through `displaySlots` (`db/displayPath.ts`: CTEs `display_pieces` and `display_slots`, one range of `slots_chain` per structural stretch and the inserted slots by id), ordered by position, and the hits' `depth`/`toDepth` and the cursor's `depth` are positions there. A summary is in the fork's history when its first and last messages are on the display path; its `depth`/`toDepth` are their positions (`displayPositionSql`, through the pieces alone). `HistoryEditCost` counts from a position the same way; `retainedDepth` (the history start's structural depth, until assembly reads positions) is taken as a position, so with insertions before the start the estimate counts a few messages too many, never too few. `historyCurrent`, which `ApplyHistoryEdits`, `ListHistoryEdits` and `UndoHistoryEdit` check every target with, uses `onForkPath`, so an inserted message is a target only in the forks that show it: elsewhere, naming its version is `NotFound`. Changesets are keyed by version, so applying and undoing an inserted message's edit are unchanged. A fork without insertions is read by the structural statements as before, where positions are depths. `historyEdits.test.ts` covers a match in X replaced and undone only in its fork, a fork made before the insertion and one made after it, a summary of X found by position only where X is shown, and random appends, insertions, forks and deletions over more than one window, where every fork's search finds its messages in the order and at the positions its path read shows them, and the estimate counts from a position. Review regressions also fork at X through 70 generations (past lineage compaction), check inherited replacements and independent undo, reject edits and undo in a fork ending before X, and compare unchanged hits, costs and changeset contents in a fork without insertions after another fork inserts a message.
- `ApplyHistoryEdits` validates the preview's identities, text and fork-local deletion state again, then commits all applications in one writer transaction. New versions copy attachments and persisted metadata, with origin `changeset`, the original version as source, and their fork. Each fork gets `replace` overrides, including unselected swipes. A group shares a replacement row for a shared input/version and resulting text across its applications. Summary changes create revisions selected through the overlay. An application that processed summaries marks its replacements `summaries_done`; ordinary edits do not. The item's after snapshot records the input version identities and amend counts preserved through consecutive edits that covered summaries. The summary reader checks these rather than treating a later rename as a repair of an earlier amend or message-only rewrite. Higher-level renamed summaries can follow fresh renamed children only when both revisions came from the same application call; reusing a panel group does not make an independent later child edit fresh; fi7 owns the rest of summary selection and job scoping.
- `changeset_groups` belongs to an account. Each `changesets` application records fork, sequence and group; items record stable target plus before/after identity, text and slot. Repeated writes in an open panel reuse an application while no child can inherit its earlier state; otherwise a new immutable application in the group preserves that child's history. `UndoHistoryEdit` restores before values only while both after identity and text still agree, reporting conflicts. It accepts item ids or a message slot; the latter includes covering summaries transitively. Undo writes only in the viewed fork and records `changeset_reverts`. `ListHistoryEdits` follows bounded fork ancestry (also across lineage flattening), labels inherited records with the originating fork's name, and derives applied/partly undone/undone from effective values. Versions named by before/after snapshots are retained: a subsequent amend copies them even when no other fork currently shows them. Undo also restores the prior `summaries_done` and changeset provenance, so undoing a later message-only edit restores earlier rename freshness. Purposeful purge clears retained version text from item snapshots; deleted state events clear their snapshots too.
- State items use `state_event`, with an existing event target or an empty target/before to add chat-wide state. The after text is a schema-checked foldable event, and referenced entities are ownership-checked. Replacements suppress the previous event and add a fork-owned event; undo suppresses added events and restores earlier suppression locally. Branch-owned event placement is enforced.
- `RenameHistoryTargets` folds effective cast state in every live owned fork: forks count as chats, including ones sharing a tip, while deleted forks and unrelated cast members are excluded. Its result length is the rename-dialog count. `RenameHistory` accepts none/this/all, previews literal replacements in the reader, then applies the matching forks atomically as one group. None creates no history. It changes history only; the card editor's name save is separate.
- **Card rename offers (U18).** After a card revision saves, the `cardSavedContributions` continuation registry (`features/editor/cardSaved.ts`) receives the old and saved effective in-character names (`nickname || name`). `card-rename.history`, registered by `features/cardRename/`, offers No (no history write), only this chat (exact fork), or all N chats using the card (`RenameHistoryTargets`, forks counted separately). A display-only name or version change with the same effective name offers nothing. Edit from a chat carries its chat/fork in the editor URL; opening directly in the library offers a named chat/fork picker for the single-chat choice. Fork for this chat and choosing a differently named family member use the same contribution, limited to that fork or No; the latter reads the effective name from the swap's returned cast, including any pinned binding. Chat continuations render through an account-scoped app-tool host so a cast swap unmounting its action row cannot lose the offer. Fork creation and the card revision finish before history replacement; failures leave that saved result intact and the history action retryable. The dialog keeps a per-account browser recovery copy of the outstanding intent and registered card/editor and chat-card actions offer Resume history replacement after a close or reload; No or a successful replacement clears that offer. Outstanding offers are retained per card in order; these browser copies are not cross-device server drafts. Successful replacement links each selected fork to its History edits panel, with the resulting before/after diffs and existing whole/per-item undo. Continuations and recovery actions can be overridden or disabled independently through their registries. `CardSavedFlow` subscribes to the continuation registry and resolves its effective entry when rendering saved, queued and recovered offers, so replacing or disabling the workflow also affects existing offers; recovery never bypasses that registry. A recovered offer names its original chat and fork beside the single-chat choice, even when resumed from another chat. `e2e/cardRename.spec.ts` covers unchanged model names, none/this/all (including siblings), clearing nickname, diff/undo, failed-action recovery without a second card revision, and a fork-and-rename's isolation. The choice and result dialogs are checked at 1440 and 390 in light and dark; `cardForkForChat.spec.ts` covers declining after a fork or family swap. `cardRenameContributions.test.ts` probes disabling and overriding the contribution. `cardSavedFlow.test.ts` checks rendering existing offers through the effective registration and hides recovery while the workflow is disabled or no longer applies. The browser tests also cover recovery from another chat retaining and naming the original fork, and successful history replacement after a family swap.
- The chat menu, History edits tab and Ctrl/Command+H open `HistoryPanel` beside the chat, or in its phone sheet.
