It has Find/Replace pairs, case/word/regex/preserve-case options, live counts and pattern errors, grouped message/swipe/summary previews, individual match skips, navigation, Replace/Replace all, inline before/after highlights in loaded messages, a toast undo and persistent history diffs with message/all undo and individual summary/state-item undo. Skips update both panel and inline previews, and closing the panel clears inline previews. `HistoryEditCost` estimates the messages after the first changed context position and affected summary suffix, with approximate text tokens; it excludes hidden/deleted messages. It is an estimate of history re-reading, not a provider billing quote. A summary result (including Previous/Next) opens the registered Summaries panel focused on its row, even outside the selected layer, with its level, range and before → after highlights. The retained search and individual skips survive that tab switch; closing the panel clears previews. `summaryPreviews` registers `history.summary-preview`, which renders only while History is available and the preview names the row's effective revision; message previews remain visible only on the History tab. New input invalidates the apply buttons until its own preview finishes. All writes publish Reload/Lcm and refresh cache estimates.
- Tests: `historyEdits.test.ts` covers scope, swipes, earlier-point inheritance, per-message and per-fork undo, later-amend conflicts, shared rename copies, stale atomic refusal, summary revision/freshness, Unicode FTS boundaries, pagination, grouped applications across a fork, rename cast counts/scopes, state suppression, Unicode words/code points, regex failure/deadline, stale fork-local deletions, and summary freshness across prior amends, message-only edits and reused groups. `historyScale.test.ts` scans 100,000 synthetic messages in 1,000 bounded regex windows. Every new RPC has two-way ownership probes in `isolation.test.ts`. `e2e/historyEdits.spec.ts` covers live preview, replacement, persisted history and undo at 1440 and 390 px in light and dark, including preview cleanup, per-match skips and summary-only undo.

## 4. Authentication

`auth/Accounts.ts` implements the `Authentication` RPC middleware declared in `@rp/shared/api.ts`. It provides `CurrentUser` to every handler, resolved on every call. Over the WebSocket, the headers are those of the upgrade request. A session is still checked on each call, so a logout reaches connections that are already open. `accounts.test.ts` checks that every RPC in `AppApi` carries the middleware: one added to the group after `.middleware()` would skip it.
- **The isolation matrix** (`test/isolation.test.ts`) has an entry for every RPC in `AppApi`, and fails on an RPC without one or an entry whose RPC is gone. An entry is one of:
  - a probe, run both ways between an admin and a user over real sockets: one reads, changes or references the other's connection, credential, model, preset or ledger row, and is refused or sees nothing. Everything the owner can list (and, for an admin, the users and network settings) is the same afterwards.
  - an admin-only probe: a user is `Forbidden`
  - `global` (no user's data) or `createOnly` (names nothing that exists), with the reason

  The HTTP routes that act on a user's data, `/library/import`, `/library/export`, `/library/avatar`, `/library/media` and `/library/drafts`, have probes of the same kind, run the same way.
- **Message headers are dropped** (`TransportHeadersOnly` in `server.ts`). Effect's RPC server appends the headers a client puts in each message after the upgrade request's, and the later entries win. Before this, any client could name any user in proxy mode by sending `remote-user` in a message. The serialization now empties message headers when decoding, so identity can only come from the upgrade request (`rpc.test.ts` checks this).
- **`AUTH_MODE=single`, built-in login** (DESIGN §10.1):
  - **No account has a password:** loopback and `ALLOWED_IPS` are the implicit local admin `local`, without logging in. That's local use. It's checked on every call, not only when a connection opens: a socket opened from elsewhere while login was required doesn't become the local admin if the last password goes.
  - **Once an enabled user has a password,** a session is required. With `LOOPBACK_REQUIRES_LOGIN=false`, loopback skips login and acts as the oldest enabled admin. Loopback is judged by `x-rp-peer`, which `RequestGuard` sets from the socket's address and which replaces any copy the client sends. Behind a reverse proxy on the same host, every client arrives from loopback, so this setting would make everyone the admin.
  - **Login and logout** are HTTP, since only a response can set a cookie: `POST /auth/login` (JSON `{handle, password}`, at most 4 KiB) and `POST /auth/logout`. A login must be `application/json`, so a cross-site form can't send one, on top of the Origin check. Over plain HTTP beyond loopback, passwords cross the network in the clear: put TLS in front.
  - **The session cookie** `rp_session` is 32 random bytes, `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Strict`, `Secure` when the page is on https, and lasts 30 days. The database stores only its SHA-256, so a copy of the database holds no live session.
  - **Login attempts** count before the password is checked, so a burst can't outrun the count. The limits are 10 per address and handle and 50 per address in 15 minutes, then `429` with `Retry-After`; a success forgives its address and handle.
    - Keying by address and handle means nobody can lock someone else out by name. Behind a same-host proxy, all clients share one address, so they also share its limits.
    - At most two password checks run at once. scrypt shares libuv's 4-thread pool with DNS and file reads, so further attempts get `429` instead of queueing.
    - Malformed handles and over-long passwords fail like a wrong password, before any hashing, and count only against their address.
    - A check holds its permit until scrypt finishes, even when the client disconnects, since the hash can't be cancelled.
    - The counts are capped at 10,000 keys: stale ones go first, then the oldest.
    - An unknown handle takes as long as a wrong password (a dummy scrypt check).
    - Checking the current password in `SetPassword` is throttled the same way, so a stolen session can't guess at full speed.
    - The counts are kept in memory.
  - **Passwords** use scrypt (N=2^15, r=8, p=3) on libuv's thread pool, so the event loop stays free. Tests lower the cost (`PasswordCost`, a context reference nothing outside the code sets), since at full cost one test with a dozen hashes took over 30 s at a load average of 75. The hash is stored as `scrypt$N$r$p$salt$hash`. A password needs 8 to 1024 characters, and nothing else (NIST SP 800-63B).
  - **No sign-up:** an admin creates accounts (`CreateUser`, `SetUserDisabled`). An admin without a password of their own can't: the new account's password would make login required, and the admin couldn't log in. Users set their own password with the current one, if they have one; an admin can set anyone's (`SetPassword`). Changing a password or disabling a user ends that user's sessions, and the last enabled admin can't be disabled.
  - **Headless servers:** `ADMIN_PASSWORD_FILE` or `ADMIN_PASSWORD` (with an optional `ADMIN_USER`) gives the first admin a password at startup. The account is the named user, else the oldest enabled admin (so the local admin keeps its data), else a new `admin`. It applies only while no enabled admin has a password, so a password changed in the app survives restarts. Proxy mode ignores it, with a warning.
  - **Caches:** sessions aren't cached, so a revoked one stops working at once. Users are cached per process and cleared on every account change, all of which go through this process. A lookup that started before a change doesn't write its stale answer back.
- **`AUTH_MODE=proxy`:** the user is whoever the reverse proxy names in `AUTH_PROXY_HEADER` (Authelia's `Remote-User`). A missing header is `Unauthorized`, and so is one containing a comma or a control character. Node joins a repeated header with `, `, so a proxy that added its header next to a client's would otherwise produce a name made of both.
  - The header is trusted because only the proxy can connect (exposure, below).
  - Login, passwords and account creation don't apply. Disabling a user does.

**Exposure** (`net/exposure.ts`, DESIGN §11). Who may connect at all, decided per connection from the socket's source address, never from a forwarded header. Configuration is environment-only, as for every security setting.
- **Single mode:** loopback, plus `ALLOWED_IPS`. Without `ALLOWED_IPS`, every address may connect while an enabled user has a password, since everyone then has to log in. This is checked per connection, so if the last password goes, access beyond loopback goes with it. An allowlist stays a restriction even with passwords.
- **Proxy mode:** `TRUSTED_PROXIES` only, or loopback when it's unset. Anyone else who could connect could send the identity header and be any user. `ALLOWED_IPS` is a startup error here.
- **Refused peers** get a bare `403` written to the socket on connect, which is then destroyed before it's read. Ending it isn't enough: the HTTP parser is already attached, and on a half-open socket it would still dispatch the peer's requests (`exposure.test.ts` checks that no handler runs).
- **At startup,** listening beyond loopback (`HOST` other than `localhost`, `127.0.0.0/8` or `::1`) with nothing beyond loopback allowed is an `UnsafeExposure` error naming the fix. So are entries that aren't IPs or CIDR ranges (a prefix must be 0–3 digits: `Number("")` is `0`, so `10.0.0.0/` would otherwise allow everyone).
- **`ALLOW_INSECURE_EXPOSURE=true`** serves every address. Combined with `ALLOWED_IPS` or `TRUSTED_PROXIES`, which it would silently undo, it's a startup error.

**Cross-site requests** (`crossSiteProblem`, a global router middleware). A page from any site can open a WebSocket to the user's loopback, and a page whose domain is re-resolved to `127.0.0.1` (DNS rebinding) is same-origin with itself. So every request gets a `403` unless:
- **`Origin`**, when present, is in `PUBLIC_ORIGINS`, or names the `Host` the request was sent to. That second route doesn't apply to a public host, so `http://` can't stand in for a public `https://`. Clients that send no `Origin` aren't browsers, so they have no session to borrow.
- **`Host`** is `localhost`, an IP literal (rebinding needs a name), or a public origin's host.
- **Proxy mode with `TRUSTED_PROXIES` set skips the `Host` check.** Only the named proxies can connect, so no rebound page reaches the server. And proxies often forward their own upstream name (nginx's default `Host` is `$proxy_host`). With `TRUSTED_PROXIES` unset, loopback is trusted, so the check stays. A page rebound to loopback is same-origin and could `fetch()` with its own identity header. A same-host proxy forwards `127.0.0.1` or `localhost`, which pass. Behind a proxy under a domain, set `PUBLIC_ORIGINS` to its public origin. Without it the server warns at startup: browsers are refused unless the proxy forwards the `Host` they used.
- **`PUBLIC_ORIGINS` entries** must be exact origins (scheme, host, optional port; a trailing slash is allowed). A path, credentials or a wildcard is a startup error, not something silently dropped.
- Vite's dev proxy keeps `Host` as `localhost:5173`, which matches the page's origin.

Users are created on first sight (`EnsureUser`): the local user, and proxy identities. The first user of an instance becomes `admin`. A disabled user is refused.

**`GetSession`** returns the caller's user and how they're signed in: `session` (logged in, so logging out applies), `local` (no account has a password), `loopback` (`LOOPBACK_REQUIRES_LOGIN=false`) or `proxy`. It's resolved again from the same upgrade headers as the middleware, so it only ever describes the caller (`session.test.ts`). The client uses it to route to login, and to show the login page's "no login needed" state.

In proxy mode, a WebSocket keeps the identity it opened with until it disconnects, so logging out of the proxy doesn't end an open tab's access. That's deliberate (DESIGN §10.1).

---

## 5. Client

`packages/client`:
- **Build and routing:** React 19 with React Compiler 1.0 (Babel plugin), Vite 8, and TanStack Router (code-based routes, built from the route registry below).
- **Server state:** comes through one `AtomRpc` client (`rpc.ts`) over the same WebSocket protocol, with reconnect on transient errors. Components read it with `@effect/atom-react`.
  - **Queries** render through `AsyncView` (`actions.tsx`): loading, an error with a retry, or the value. Each query names reactivity keys (`connections`, `credentials`, `models`, `network`, `users`). `allOf` combines queries a page needs into one atom, so they're requested together rather than one after another.
  - **Writes** run through `useAction`, which tracks the pending state and the error, and invalidates the keys the write changes, after a success and after a `StaleWrite`, so the user sees the latest version. It calls the client directly rather than through `AtomRpc.mutation`: that atom is shared per RPC, so a second call would interrupt the first.
  - **Errors** keep their tags (`errors.ts`) and are shown in a sentence each. The server's own messages, such as the last-admin or own-password-first rejections, are shown as given: the client doesn't repeat the server's rules.
- **Sessions** (`session.ts`, §4). The app shell reads `GetSession`. `Unauthorized`, from it or from any later call, sends the user to `/login?next=…` (a path on this site only). Login and logout are `fetch` calls to the HTTP endpoints, followed by a full page load (a failed logout says so and loads nothing), so the WebSocket reopens with the new cookie and nothing cached for the previous user survives.
- **Registries** (spec/PLUGINS.md §8; `registry.ts`, `routes.tsx`, `slots.tsx`). The same rules as the server's (§6): entries by id, a registration overrides the same id, `disable` hides one, and each returns a function that undoes it, in any order.
  - **Routes:** each screen registers an id, a parent (a layout entry, or `root`), a path relative to it and a component, and optionally a navigation label and order. The router is built from the registry at startup. A layout's navigation lists its children, admin-only entries for admins (the server still decides).
  - **Slots:** a named place in a screen that renders its registered entries in order. The first is `connection.sections`: every section of a connection's page is an entry.
  - **Chat panels** (`features/chat/panels.tsx`): descriptors register stable IDs, labels, order, icons, a placement (`tools`, the default, or `cast`), availability, components and optional preloading. The chat feature installs This chat, Model, Prompt preset, Notes, Lorebooks, Summaries, Portrait and History edits as tools and Cast in the cast strip through this API; the character feature installs the `card:<entityId>` tab family in the cast strip through a matcher and a tab-list function whose entries carry a portrait and, on the persona, the word "you". URL and remembered-tab validation resolve against the registry, and the same descriptors supply header buttons, tab labels and bodies. Exact IDs can override or disable one tab of a family; an explicit disable blocks family fallback. Validation with a viewed chat requires a tab actually offered by that descriptor, including command targets and panel navigation. Composer settings buttons and a character tab’s Notes link disappear while their target panel is unavailable. `lazyPanel` supplies a per-mounted-panel loading/error boundary and retry with a fresh lazy import; Notes preloads when the panel opens. Visited bodies remain mounted while switching tabs, retaining drafts. Disabling or making a descriptor unavailable removes its tabs and prevents its body from opening.
  - **Chat commands** (`features/chat/commands.tsx`): registered IDs, labels, order, icons, availability and handlers receive the viewed session and fork, history navigation and message navigation. The history feature contributes `history.search-replace` and `history.edits`, both opening the existing history workspace. The search-and-replace command declares Mod+H; menu and keyboard invocation resolve the same effective registration, including overrides and disabling. Commands can declare a target panel; both history commands do, so hiding or making History unavailable also hides the menu entries and disables Mod+H.
  - **Editing/version shortcuts** (`replyCommands.ts`): ArrowUp in an empty composer edits the last loaded message; Ctrl/Cmd+ArrowUp edits the last message on your side, from the empty composer or outside inputs. At the tip, it searches older path windows if needed and opens the target; a changed fork, tip, path or cast cancels the read. Both resolve `message.edit`, including its availability, override and disablement. Message rows and commands share `cast.ts`'s side classification: a played card is on your side even when the model wrote it, and another cast card is a reply even when you wrote it. ArrowLeft/ArrowRight outside inputs step versions of the last finished reply through the effective `messageVersions` registration; hidden versions are skipped and Right past the last generates another. Buttons and gestures share `stepVersion`, which rechecks the viewed fork and selection after a list read. Ctrl/Cmd+Enter in a message editor and its Save button invoke registered `message.save`. Commands ignore typing in other inputs, shadow editors, composition, repeats, extra modifiers and dialogs. The composer’s Keyboard shortcuts disclosure lists effective registered shortcuts; the corresponding controls also describe theirs in tooltips.
  - **Composer controls** (`features/chat/composerControls.tsx`): entries declare an ID, placement, order, availability and component, with an optional invocation handler. The chat feature registers generation settings, speaker/recent speakers, regenerate, stop, generate, send, guidance and reply selection through this API; `guidanceEdit.tsx` registers the guidance-edit bar in the `banner` placement, above the input. The composer supplies the viewed session and fork, speaker/reply choices, presentation state and callbacks; draft submission, recovery and fork checks stay in its host. Buttons and Enter both resolve the current handler by ID, so replacing or disabling Send/Generate also changes keyboard invocation. Empty Enter and the empty Send button share `replyNow`: with no text or attachments and guidance off they request a reply without posting a human message. Placements retain the existing responsive layout.
  - **Continue shortcut** (`replyCommands.ts`, `ContinueAction.tsx`): Alt+Enter resolves the effective `message.continue` handler for the latest finished model reply with text. The button and shortcut share availability and next-model capability checks; disabling or replacing that action affects both. Alt is excluded from both composers’ send predicate. Keyboard continuation leaves composer text untouched and sends none of it as guidance; the visible Continue button retains its guidance behavior and names the shortcut in its tooltip. Commands ignore composition, dialogs and repeat events.
  - **Message toolbar/menu actions** (`features/chat/messageActions.tsx`): one entry can contribute a toolbar control, menu item and dialog, with order and availability. The chat feature registers edit, regenerate, continue, request inspector, delete version/message, write a version, regenerate with a model, branch, visibility, copy text/link and delete all versions through this API. `message.context` owns both inspector offers. Dialogs mount outside the menu popup, are tied to the effective registration, and close when it is removed, replaced or becomes unavailable. Hosts receive the viewed entry, session/fork, generation/visibility flags and error/undo reporting. The model-picker dialog and its menu item belong to `message.regenerate-with`. `chatBranchControls` registers the header branch picker and its naming/deletion dialogs as `chat.branches`; `messagePreviews` registers the inline history replacement preview as `history.message-preview`; `summaryPreviews` registers the opened summary's replacement preview as `history.summary-preview`. Both preview bodies read the preview provider and the effective History panel, so disabling History removes its inline preview too.
  - **Inserting messages** (`features/chat/insertMessage/`, registered in `features/index.ts`; spec/MESSAGE-INSERT.md §4, unit 9). Three registered message actions in "More actions": **Insert message below** (`message.insert-below`, order 28) on every message, **Insert message above** (`message.insert-above`) only on the first message of the path shown (the window starts at the root), and **Duplicate** (`message.duplicate`), which inserts a copy of the version shown directly below at once and is disabled, with the reason under it, for a version still being written or failed. The insertion editor fills the `chat.message-stream` slot (`presentation.tsx`; `ChatPage` renders it below each message and above the first), inline under its anchor, a sheet below 640 px. It holds the speaker (`SpeakerSwitcher`, defaulting by the registered `insertSpeakerDefaults`: below a character's message, the composer's speaker; below the user's own, the character who spoke last before it), the composer's text box with its `insert` draft (`MessageDraftTarget.Insert`, the session's message editors, so the text is saved, restored on reload and committed as every editor's is), **Generate as ⟨speaker⟩** (the text, if any, is the direction), **Insert**, **Cancel** (closes it; the draft stays, and opening it there again finds it) and **Discard** (the text to the recovered texts). Above them, the registered `insertEstimates` lines: what the next reply re-reads and the cache warmth, "Before the model's history start: the next reply won't see it." (before the stepped start and below nothing a summary covers), and "Inside summarized history: the summary is rewritten on the next reply." when a summary in use covers the anchor. Below the last message at the fork's tip, Insert and Generate are the composer's `SendOrQueue` and Generate X (a reply being written queues them), and that send commits the insert draft (the composer commit takes `composer` or `insert` drafts). Elsewhere they're `ChatSession.insertMessage` (the `InsertMessage` RPC). The new message shows in place, highlighted for 2.5 s (`ChatView.highlight`), with a toast "Message inserted" or "Message duplicated" and **Undo** (`undoInsert`: a running generation stopped, then `DeleteMessage` to the bin). While an inserted message is being generated, a note under it says the next reply won't include it until it's finished. Nothing marks the messages after an inserted or edited one (DESIGN §3.1 "No mark").
    Undo retains the inserting fork when the toast is created, including if the viewer switches to a fork inheriting the insertion before clicking it (`insertUndo.test.ts`). The browser insertion suite covers saved drafts over reload, directed and undirected generation, and appends after a duplicated display end whose structural tip was deleted, at both widths and themes.
    Slot events can arrive out of commit order. The session reads the authoritative window when an anchor is outside it, a newer sibling is already shown, or an unknown inserted slot has no position; inserted slots never enter the append path or become the structural tip. `insertMessage.test.ts` covers reversed sibling events, a child arriving before its anchor, repeated off-window events and a positionless inserted root. Ordinary append events also follow above-first insertions when the structural tip is null.
  - **Numbering by position** (spec §2.3). The header’s paged branch scan is scoped to the viewed fork and refreshed on path revisions, so insertions also renumber branch points outside the loaded window (`e2e/insertMessage.spec.ts`). The client numbers messages, and compares them with the server's positions (the history start, the summarized-through point, the LCM cursor, `cursorPosition`, and summaries' and coverage ranges, the image ranges, the branch header's and picker's numbers), by `positionOf(entry)` (`features/chat/position.ts`: `position`, or the depth in a window without insertions) and `lcmCursor`. An inserted slot's `Slot` event (`PathEntry.inserted` with its `position`) is placed by the session directly below its anchor, the messages after it renumbered; above the window only the numbers move, another fork's is ignored, and later events of a slot keep the position the window gave it. An inserted message is never the leaf: an append (here or from another tab) goes below the fork's tip (`leafSlotId`, the window's structural tip), including after an inserted message shown last. The branch header keys fork points by slot, since an inserted message shares the next message's depth. `test/insertMessage.test.ts` covers the actions' availability, the estimate lines, the speaker default and the placement; `e2e/insertMessage.spec.ts` covers the flow at 1440 and 390 px, light and dark.
  - Client registries expose a subscription and stable immutable snapshots of effective entries and explicit disablements. Panel, command, composer-control and message-action hosts derive their rendered output from those snapshots, so React Compiler retains registration, disabling and disposal as render dependencies. An empty chat-command menu removes its trigger. Routes are still assembled at startup; generic slot outlets also subscribe to captured snapshots. `chatContributions.test.ts` and `actionContributions.test.ts` register synthetic panels, commands, controls and message actions in Effect scopes, exercise replacement/addition/disabling through their hosts and invocation paths, and verify built-in restoration. `e2e/chatContributions.spec.ts` loads a synthetic plugin into the mounted app through Vite, closes its Effect scopes, and checks live panel/control/command/dialog removal, built-in restoration, dependent links and dynamic-family URL validation. The fixture is absent from production builds.
  - **Remaining chat presentation contributions** (`features/chat/presentation.tsx`, `registerPresentation.tsx`): the chat feature registers `card.edit`, `card.fork-for-chat` and `card.choose-version` in `chatCardActions`; both Cast rows and character panels render this outlet. Each action owns its dialog, so removing an action removes an open dialog. Library/editor/card-page actions, badges, provenance and family sections retain the `cardContributions` registry from card forks. A root card's comparison dialog is titled “Compare with family”; descendants use “Compare with parent”.
  - **Timeline rows** (`timelineContributions.tsx`): `timelineTransforms` applies ordered, reversible transformations to message rows. `timeline.history-budget` inserts the history notice; `timeline.lcm` inserts the LCM boundary and substitutes selected summaries. Built-in dividers locate their boundaries from the current rows’ depth ranges, so earlier plugin insertions, entry copies and range projections do not shift them onto another message. Rows have open keys, estimated heights, inclusive depth ranges, optional last-slot identity and a render function; the virtualizer consumes these rather than a closed feature-row union. The host retains paging, measurements, scroll anchoring and link positioning. Boundary, summary and rail bodies render `timelineBoundaries`, `timelineSummaries` and `timelineRails` outlets. Removing all summary bodies restores the originals; removing all boundaries omits their rows; removing all rails also removes reserved width and coverage reads. Recursive summary expansion remains implementation inside the registered summary body.
  - **Settings and message versions** (`presentation.tsx`): `chatSettingSections` registers Summaries and Roles; `accountSettingSections` registers the account's summary defaults; `summarySettingFields` supplies the shared LCM fields for both scopes. The message's refusal retry picker comes from `messageRetries`. `messageVersions` supplies version arrows/picker and the optional step handler used by both its buttons and touch swipes. Disabling/replacing that registration affects both paths; a replacement without a step handler declines touch navigation. Draft recovery, version selection, regeneration and error reporting retain their existing session operations.
  - **Chat composition** (`presentation.tsx`): `chatSurfaceProviders` composes ordered wrappers for history preview, preset workspace and portraits, passing the viewed session, view, resolved cast and panel navigation. `chatOverlays` installs the preset overlay; `chatComposer` installs the composer. Preset consumers tolerate a missing workspace. Built-ins register through `registerPresentation` from the chat feature; lazily loaded bodies keep their implementation in their feature modules. All these outlets subscribe to registry snapshots and support additions, replacement, disablement and disposal. `presentationContributions.test.ts` exercises scoped alternatives through every outlet and row projection, provider ordering and shared gesture resolution; `e2e/presentationContributions.spec.ts` exercises the mounted compiled hosts with a synthetic plugin.
  - The core features register through them (`features/index.ts`), as plugins will.
- **Secrets in forms** (`SecretInput`, `PasswordInput` in `ui/Field.tsx`). Keys, header values, proxy URLs and passwords are uncontrolled inputs read from the form on submit, so React never mirrors them into the DOM's `value` attribute, and dialogs that take them mount only while open, so Cancel forgets them.
  - **Keys aren't password fields.** Browsers' password managers offer to save any `type="password"` field whatever its `autocomplete`, and a saved key would later be offered on the login form. Key fields are plain text masked with `-webkit-text-security` (a browser without it gets a password field instead, so a key never shows in clear), with `autocomplete="off"` and the opt-outs of 1Password, LastPass, Bitwarden and Dashlane (`data-1p-ignore`, `data-lpignore`, `data-bwignore`, `data-form-type="other"`). Account passwords stay password fields.
- **Screens** (`features/`), on stock components (CLIENT-FRAMEWORK §8):
  - **Login:** wrong password, `429` with a countdown from `Retry-After`, proxy mode, and "no login needed" when the server signs the caller in without one. A disabled account fails like a wrong password, as the server intends.
  - **Connections:** list, create, and a page per connection: settings (with the row version, so a change made elsewhere is a `StaleWrite` with "load the latest"), key, check, models, custom headers, network route, delete.
  - **Keys:** keys go in and never come out; only the hint shows. A connection whose address its key doesn't allow says so, and allowing it takes the key typed again. Replacing a key lists the origins of the connections using it, then those it allowed before, none ticked, and flags each that the old key didn't allow (§3.12).
  - **Models:** the merged list per connection, each field marked as the user's, the provider's or unknown. Refresh, hide, edit overrides, add by id.
  - **Network** (admins): the proxy as the server returns it, the bypass list, `forceProxy`, and `InvalidNetworkSettings` as a list of problems.
  - **Account** for everyone (password, log out, how you're signed in), and **Users** for admins (create, set passwords, disable).
  - **Import** (`features/library/`, §11): pick or drop files (`FileDrop` in `ui/`: a label around a hidden file input, so it's a picker by click or keyboard too). They upload one at a time, each with its state (waiting, importing, imported, not imported, failed) and its report rendered as plain text, so file text that looks like HTML or Markdown shows as typed. A failure gives its reason, an ended session included. The picker is disabled until a batch is done, so a second batch can't interleave with it. A file over the 64 MB cap is refused before it's sent. With `?into=<ref>` it updates that entry from its new version and says what happened to each variant.
  - **Library:** cards, lorebooks and presets by label, with variant and "update available" badges, an export per entry (cards pick PNG, V2 JSON, V3 JSON or CHARX) downloaded through a blob link, and "Update from file" on upstream entries. Uploads go through `useAction` only to invalidate the `library` key.
  - **Characters** (`/`, the home screen; `features/characters/`, DESIGN §2.1): the user's character cards as a grid, personas left out, from one read (`ListCardUsage`, §12), with the user's groups among them as ST shows its group tiles (`ListChatGroups`, §12; both requested at once). Each character's tile has the avatar, the library label and subtitle, and "N chats · <time ago>" or "Never used"; a group's has its members' faces as one picture (`GroupFaces.tsx`: two side by side, three as one tall and two stacked, four or more in a 2×2 with "+N"), "Group · N" over it, its wrapping name and full wrapping member list (readable on touch screens) and "N chats · <time ago>". Search (a card's label, subtitle and in-chat name; a group's name and its members' names and labels), All / Groups / Never used, and sort by last message (never by last opened; on a tie a group before its members; never used last) or name, remembered per browser. A click continues the card's latest chat, or opens its pending chat (`/cards/$entityId/chat`) when it has none, so nothing is made. Each character's tile also has **New chat** and **Inspect**, shown on hover or focus with a mouse and always on a touch screen; a group's tile continues its latest chat on a click and has **New chat** (the group's pending chat, below) and **All N chats** (a one-chat group's **Group page**), which opens the group's page; on a narrow tile the second goes under the first (`e2e/homeGroups.spec.ts`, with the bin's readable names).
  - **A group's page** (`/groups/$chatId`, `GroupPage.tsx`, named by any of its chats; `ListGroupChats`, §12): its faces, name and use, **Continue the latest chat**, **New chat** (`/groups/$chatId/new`), its chats (`ChatList` given the group's chats, latest message first, each deletable with the toast; deleting the chat the page is named by moves the page to another of the group's chats), and its characters, each linking to the card's page. A chat that's in no group (gone to the bin, or down to one character) says so, with a link home.
  - **A card's page** (`/cards/$entityId`): avatar, label, subtitle, the in-chat name when it differs, **Continue** (its latest chat), **New chat**, **Edit card** (`/library/$refId`), every chat it joined as peers (`ListChats` by card, the list as below, those with a message first so the first is the one Continue opens), and its definition read from the ref's head (`GetRevision`), each field folded to four lines with Show more, measured again when its width changes (a `ResizeObserver`), keeping what the user opened. A card that isn't a character (a persona, or in the bin) says so and links to the library.
  - **Chats** (`/chats`, the sidebar's list as a page for phones, plus a chat without characters) and a chat (`/chats/$chatId`): §12. Phones have Characters and Chats in the top bar.
  - **The chat list** (`features/chat/ChatList.tsx`; the sidebar, `/chats` and a card's page): pages of 50 (`ListChats`), Load more after the last, and each page's cast, length and last message from `ListChatSummaries`. A row has the readable name and the time of its last message, then on a line of its own when it started (ST's file-name date, else its creation) and its message count, the other characters on a line that may be cut short, and the last message; a name or cast cut short shows in full on hover. A refreshed first page is right down to its last chat, so once later pages are loaded, their chats above its end are dropped (gone, or moved up into it) and the page after it is read again, so a chat pushed off it isn't skipped. Find a chat asks the server (`search`), so it finds chats not loaded yet, and pages its results the same way.
  - **Status** (`/settings/status`): the server's health.
  - **Bin** (`/bin`, from the header): §3.11. **Notifications** (`/settings/notifications`): §12.
  - **The card and preset editors** (`/library/$refId`): §14.6.
- **Styling** (CLIENT-FRAMEWORK §8):
  - **Semantic tokens only.** `styles.css` defines colors by role (`canvas`, `surface`, `text`, `muted`, `accent`, `danger`, `warning`, `success`, `overlay`, …), for light and dark, with `data-theme` overrides.
  - **The default Tailwind palette is removed** (`--color-*: initial`), so a class like `bg-zinc-800` doesn't exist. Only token utilities (`bg-surface`, `text-muted`) can be written. A redesign changes token values, not components.
  - **One import path for UI components.** Feature code imports from `src/ui/`, never from Base UI directly; ESLint enforces it. Base UI carries behavior and accessibility (labels, focus traps, keyboard), and `src/ui/` carries the look: buttons, fields, select, checkbox, switch, radio group, dialogs, cards, badges, alerts, menus, tooltips and icon buttons, and line icons (`Icon.tsx`).
  - **Portaled menus** (`ui/Menu.tsx`) use fixed viewport coordinates for both ordinary and action-menu positioners. A menu opened in a scrolled fixed phone sheet stays clickable instead of making the document scroll repeatedly under it. The notes walkthrough exercises mouse access and viewport bounds at 1440 and 390 px in both themes.
  - Inputs use 16 px text on touch screens, so iOS doesn't zoom into them. A submit the client refuses focuses the first invalid field, whose description carries the error.
- **Message text** is Markdown through `ui/Markdown.tsx` (§12).
- **Tests:**
  - `test/` (Vitest): the registries.
  - `e2e/` (Playwright, `npm run test:browser` for Chromium, `npm run test:browser:all` for Chromium/Firefox/WebKit, outside `npm run check`): both commands first build the client and database worker bundles (including for specs that explicitly use bundled workers), then run the built client against the real server and its database workers, each file with its own servers on random ports and fresh data directories, and a fake OpenAI-style provider on loopback. One flow per screen (the import screen's in `e2e/library.spec.ts`), a cross-user check through the UI (another user never sees someone's connections or keys, in a separate tab or after logout in the same one), every screen at phone and desktop width in light and dark with no sideways scrolling, and dialogs from the keyboard. `SCREENSHOT_DIR` saves screenshots. The library export test checks the exact blob handed to a native download, its filename, and saved bytes. If Chromium reports `canceled`, file saving is quarantined only when a separate plain synthetic download also cancels; the blob assertions still run, and the report is annotated. An unwritable browser download directory reproduces this even without the app or CSP; the original environment's cause was not established. The browsers come from `~/.cache/ms-playwright`; CI installs Chromium's headless shell. Other engines are opt-in, installed for the pinned Playwright version with `npx playwright install firefox webkit`. The queue conflict flow has its own server with a synthetic reply longer than the test deadline (`queue.spec.ts`), so the queue cannot drain between edits on a loaded host. The Firefox suite includes key-field CSS masking and password fallback checks (`secrets.spec.ts`). Firefox has an intermittent reload timeout in `chatSetup.spec.ts` (the second reload after changing chat settings), reproduced in repeated runs; its cause is unresolved. On this host WebKit cannot launch: its system libraries are missing, so its suite and masking remain unverified.
- **Development:** `npm run dev:server` (the development entry point, `src/dev/main.ts`, §12) and `npm run dev:client`. Vite proxies `/rpc` and `/auth` to the server.

---

## 6. Provider layer

`packages/server/src/providers/` (spec/PROVIDERS.md §4, §7, §9). It's the one entry point to models, for the assembler (phase 2) and later the agent loop. Dependencies are pinned: `@earendil-works/pi-ai` 1.0.3, OpenAI 7.19.0, Anthropic 0.129.0 and Google GenAI 2.21.0. Startup scrubs ambient SDK configuration, and requests use the controlled transport. Upgrades require another environment/transport audit; installed libraries are unmodified.

**The provider registry** (`providers/registry.ts`, `providers/builtins.ts`; the generic mechanism is `@rp/shared/registry.ts`, re-exported as `src/registry.ts`). This is the first registry of the structure in spec/PLUGINS.md §3.1: the core registers its features through the same registries plugins will use.
- **A registry** holds entries by id. Registering adds an entry or overrides the one with the same id, and `disable` hides one. Each registration lasts as long as its scope, and closing the scope restores whatever it hid, in any order (`test/registry.test.ts`). Wrapping is `get`, then registering a replacement that calls the original.
- **The `ProviderRegistry` service has five registries:**
  - **Provider kinds:** label, pi provider id, wire API id and implementation (pi-ai's `ProviderStreams` or the plugin's own), whether it takes our `fetch`, default base URL, whether a key is optional, a model lister, and an optional catalog.
  - **Request transforms:** payload rewrites through pi-ai's `onPayload`, applied in registration order to requests whose kind and API they apply to.
  - **Response observers:** readers of the raw stream events.
  - **Cost formulas:** each prices a finished request for the ledger, or passes (null). They're tried newest registration first, so a user's or plugin's formula goes before the built-ins. The ledger records the id of the one that priced it.
  - **Image token counters** (`providers/imageCounters.ts`): a provider's exact counting endpoint for one image, tried newest registration first; see "Image tokens" below.
- **The built-ins** are registered at startup:
  - the six kinds, in picker order: OpenAI Chat Completions, Anthropic, OpenAI Responses, OpenRouter, DeepSeek, Google
  - the Anthropic explicit breakpoints, as a transform
  - the usage capture, as one observer per wire API
  - the cost formulas `builtin` (the price table), `reported` (the provider's own figure), then `user-pricing` (the acting user's persisted rules); a later plugin registration can override any of them
  - the image token counters `anthropic-count-tokens`, `google-count-tokens` and `openai-input-tokens`

**Image tokens** (DESIGN §10.2a: image tokens are part of the prompt, and the estimate is accurate on every provider). What an image costs a model comes, in order, from: the provider's exact count, the provider's documented formula for the model's family, the ledger's calibration for that model, else it is unknown. Every estimate carries that basis (`counted`, `formula`, `calibrated`, `unknown`) to the context view and the composer. The assembly side is in §15 ("Image tokens").
- **Formulas** (`@rp/shared/imageTokens.ts`, pure, registered as image token rules in the assembler, §15). Sources, read and reviewed 2026-10-30:
  - Anthropic (https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/vision, https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/vision-coordinates): `⌈w/28⌉ × ⌈h/28⌉` after resizing to the tier's limits. Claude 4.7 and later (and a model name without a version) are on the high-resolution tier: 2,576 px long edge, 4,784 tokens; earlier models 1,568 px and 1,568 tokens. The resize is Anthropic's TypeScript reference implementation verbatim (binary search on the long edge, ties-to-even rounding). Its documented table says 2000×1500 resizes to 1269×952; the reference gives 1270×952. Both cost 1,564 tokens, which is what the tests pin.
  - OpenAI (https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/images-vision): tile models (gpt-4o, gpt-4.1, gpt-4o-mini, gpt-5, gpt-5.1, o1, o3) fit 2048², scale the short side to 768, then base + tiles × per-tile (85/170, 2833/5667, 70/140, 75/150); `low` is the base alone. Patch models (gpt-6-astra, gpt-5.6, gpt-5.5, gpt-5.4, gpt-5.2, gpt-4.1-mini) count 32 px patches after the detail level's size and patch budget, times the model's multiplier (1.2 or 1.62 for models with a documented sizing row). Our requests send `auto` (pi-ai's Responses sets it; Chat Completions omits it, which OpenAI defines as `auto`), so `auto`'s sizing per model is used. Retired gpt-5-mini/nano, gpt-4.1-nano and o4-mini no longer have a documented sizing row: they use counts or calibration, else remain unknown rather than assuming a different model's sizing.
  - Google (https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/image-understanding, https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/tokens, https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/media-resolution): before Gemini 3, 258 tokens up to 384×384, else 258 per tile with a crop unit of `max(1, ⌊min(w,h)/1.5⌋)` (Google calls it rough; the one-pixel floor keeps narrow images finite). Gemini 3/4 bill a fixed figure per media resolution; we send none, which is 1,120. Unversioned aliases and non-Gemini Google models have no assumed Gemini-2 sizing: they use counts/calibration or remain unknown.
  - Mistral (https://docs.mistral.ai/studio/conversations/vision): about `w·h/784` within 1540², Pixtral about `w·h/256` within 1024² (documented as approximate).
  - DeepSeek (https://api-docs.deepseek.com/guides/vision): no formula is published, only a cap of 1,024 tokens per image. The rule returns that as a bound, so the image is calibrated or unknown, budgeted at the cap meanwhile.
  - xAI (https://docs.x.ai/developers/model-capabilities/images/understanding.md) and OpenRouter (https://openrouter.ai/docs/llms-full.txt) publish no formula. OpenRouter passes images to the upstream, so its models (`vendor/model`) use the upstream family's rule. xAI reports `prompt_tokens_details.image_tokens`, which calibration reads. Local servers (llama.cpp, vLLM) document no image-cost endpoint; they are calibrated.
- **Exact counts** (`providers/imageCounters.ts`, `Providers.countImageTokens`, `chat/ImageTokenCounts.ts`). Each counter counts one image as the difference between a one-message count with the image and the same message without it, so framing and the text tokenizer drop out. Anthropic `POST /v1/messages/count_tokens` (base64 images; free, separate rate limits by tier: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/token-counting), Gemini `models/{model}:countTokens` with inline data (cost and limits not stated: https://ai.google.dev/api/tokens), and OpenAI `POST /v1/responses/input_tokens`, only when the base URL is `api.openai.com` (cost and limits not stated: https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/token-counting). Counting is never on a send's path: the cache-warmth estimate of an open chat (§12) returns the next context's images that aren't counted for its model (`uncounted`, stripped before anything is published or returned by `GetNextContext`), and `ImageTokenCounts` counts them in the background behind one shared permit across all chats and connections, with the with/without HTTP calls also sequential. Each job has a 30-second timeout, with at most 128 pending image jobs. In-flight deduplication uses the storage identity (owner, kind, model, detail, hash); the worker cache is checked again under the permit before HTTP, so stale previews and another connection cannot recount a stored image. It stores each with `RecordImageTokenCount` in `image_token_counts`, keyed by owner, content hash, provider kind, model key and detail (an image is counted once), then estimates that owner's watched chats again. Active composer uploads are included before Send and after model changes, using the owner/fork-scoped server draft; attachment add/remove schedules a new preview. A failed count, or a model whose connection has no counter, rests for ten minutes.
- **Calibration** (`providers/imageSamples.ts`). When a request whose final payload sent images finishes with reported usage, the ledger close records a sample with `RecordImageTokenSample` in `image_token_samples` (the newest 20 per connection and model): the images, their total pixels (read from the sent bytes' headers), and their tokens. The tokens are the provider's own image figure where it reports one (`prompt_tokens_details.image_tokens`, Gemini's `IMAGE` modality), else the reported input less the payload's text as the assembler estimates text (`derived`). An estimate fits `tokens = a·images + b·pixels` by least squares once the samples' sizes differ (models whose cost grows with resolution), else uses the mean per image. Zero/invalid reported image counts and zero calibration samples are rejected; a sent image never learns a zero cost. Missing dimensions can still use the observed per-image mean.
- **`kind` and `api` are open strings** (`@rp/shared/providers.ts`), checked against the registry where they're used:
  - an unknown kind can't create a connection
  - a connection whose kind is gone, for example its plugin was removed, is `unusable`
  - `ListProviderKinds` gives the client the kinds in effect
- `test/providerStream.test.ts` registers a gateway kind and a transform in a scope, the way a plugin will. It streams through them, then closes the scope and checks that both are gone.

**Cold-cache parallel requests** (DESIGN §5.5; `providers/cacheStagger.ts`, `providers/cacheIdentity.ts`). After SDK conversion and registered transforms, `stream` hashes the provider-formatted prefix in memory, including tool definitions, structured content and cache configuration. Source provenance locates the last assembly breakpoint in the final payload. For block-explicit caching, its last cacheable block must carry an applicable marker: that reusable endpoint is the scheduling key. Later SDK markers, including a volatile one-turn direction, contribute historical coverage without replacing that key. Automatic-cache requests also schedule on the assembly endpoint. Owner, connection/cache revision, route, model, conversion capabilities and final payload routing fields (including `prompt_cache_key`) participate.
- Gates are acquired at the controlled fetch boundary, where the SDK's actual session-affinity headers are available. Only routing values that reach that boundary enter the identity; an unused Anthropic session ID does not partition otherwise identical requests or historical warmth. Explicit header overrides take precedence naturally. Providers without this controlled fetch boundary bypass scheduling. Sent-context capture still waits until the gate releases.
- Anthropic's top-level `cache_control` enables automatic placement at the last cacheable block (excluding trailing thinking blocks). When no explicit marker was sent at the reusable boundary, that effective endpoint supplies the key. Its TTL/configuration participates in identity; an enabled top-level control is not a markerless request.
- Scheduling pins the final transformed request's documented lifetime (§15), with model/connection overrides taking precedence, just as sent-context capture does. Unknown policies remain unknown. Fixed-lived cache hits do not record a new expiry; historical coverage comes from the original write or miss. Follower hit metadata uses the same normalized read count as the ledger, including header-only Fireworks reads.
- Scheduling has its own **endpoint coverage** predicate. `CachePrefixWarm` checks whether one of the owner's 100 most recent unexpired eligible ledger rows covers the entire requested endpoint with the same identity. A shorter warm history cannot bypass a cold extension. The display comparison (§15) keeps its existing unchanged-old-prefix semantics.
- Migration `0020_cache_identity` stores only endpoint SHA-256 digests and the request's pinned expiry in the worker ledger; no raw tools, images or routing payload are added to storage or client DTOs. Closed records are immutable. Requests predating these hashes are conservatively cold. Without a TTL, simultaneous cold requests still coalesce but historical warmth is unknown.
- No reusable prefix, `cacheRetention: "none"`, disabled model caching, or no applicable marker in an explicit-cache payload means no gate. Unrecognized payload dialects also bypass it. Callers supply the provider-neutral request and model in `inspection`, including future summary jobs.
- The first cold request starts immediately. Matching followers wait for its first **provider response chunk**, including Anthropic's `message_start`, rather than its first text token. All six built-in kinds default to this rule: Anthropic, OpenRouter (Claude included), OpenAI Chat/Responses, DeepSeek and Google. Claude Bedrock/Vertex kinds supplied by plugins inherit the same default. `ProviderKindEntry.cacheRelease` can instead select successful completion and change `timeoutMs` (default 10,000). The reference establishes no different automatic-provider release point; first-chunk readiness remains an assumption to measure for those providers, particularly best-effort caches.
- Timeout, leader failure or stop releases followers too. Stopping a held follower aborts its wait and never sends it. Gates live for one attempt, so retries cannot strand followers. A solitary request has no scheduling wait or timer; caps and rate-limit pauses still apply. Owners cannot hold each other's requests.
- Migration `0019_cache_stagger` adds follower metadata to the usage ledger: leader request id, release reason, and `hit` from the actual provider hit field (null when unavailable). Existing normalized read/write counts and raw usage stay available. This makes first-chunk releases, timeouts and misses distinguishable.
- `test/cacheStagger.test.ts` exercises actual swipe RPCs through assembly, SDK HTTP, worker storage and subscriptions with synthetic providers: one Anthropic write and N−1 reads, automatic-cache followers through OpenAI and the Gemini REST adapter, growing conversations, different one-turn directions sharing cold history, warm requests, timeout, leader/follower stop and owner isolation. `test/providerCacheIdentity.test.ts` checks tools, image blocks, session routing, final routing overrides and disabled/markerless caching at the SDK boundary, including historical warmth, ignored session IDs, actual affinity headers and their overrides, volatile tails, top-level Anthropic control, documented/unknown/fixed lifetimes and normalized header hits. The browser tests cover the waiting label and reconnect.
- **Opt-in live measurement**, never CI: `npm run measure:cache-stagger -- --live --provider anthropic --model MODEL --key-env ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`. Repeat for each built-in kind and actual model; use `--base-url` for a gateway, `--release complete` to compare readiness, `--timeout-ms` and `--requests` to vary the cohort. It sends a unique synthetic prefix of roughly 10k tokens, reports follower cache reads/hits and send/hold timings, and closes workers and removes its isolated database under `.cache-stagger-measurements/` (or `--scratch`). It captures the named key before scrubbing provider environment configuration, then imports the application; it never reads production storage. It incurs real provider usage; no live measurement runs as part of checks.

**Calling providers.** `providers/sdk.ts` loads pi-ai's lazy OpenAI and Anthropic wire adapters directly, never its `Models` collection. Production and development entry points first run `providerEnvironment.ts`, then dynamically import the application: SDKs cannot observe ambient provider configuration during import. The scrub mutates only this server process's environment; its parent is unaffected. Server-owned names are declared in `serverEnvironment.ts`, used by configuration readers, and checked against every scrub rule before anything is deleted. An overlap, even for an unset server variable, fails startup. SDK upgrades still require an environment/transport audit; there are no source patches, provider bundles or hash checks.

**Google transport** (`providers/google.ts`). The pinned pi-ai adapter rejects custom fetch. genai 2.21.0's generateContent `HttpOptions` exposes headers, body and retries, but no fetch hook or dispatcher; its fetcher hooks belong to other APIs. Our lazily loaded adapter sends Gemini's REST generateContent stream directly through the request's controlled fetch, reusing pi-ai's exported transcript/tool converters, thinking helpers and event stream. The payload hook receives SDK-shaped parameters, including the request signal; `googleRequest.ts` maps the supported options to REST, normalizes model/tuned-model and cached-content resource names, and rejects unsupported options before HTTP. Generation fields are allowlisted; service tier, system instructions, tools and safety settings live outside `generationConfig`. Full JSON Schema fields are supported; the legacy `responseSchema`/tool `parameters` dialect and unimplemented SDK tools are rejected. Transport settings and cancellation remain request-owned. It preserves thinking and tool signatures, usage, status and aborts. SSE accepts LF, CRLF and CR across arbitrary byte boundaries. Raw JSON and SSE error envelopes retain their details; premature EOF uses pi-ai's recognized retry message, and output still prevents retries. Request, thinking-budget and error parity tests compare against the pinned SDK over local HTTP. No global fetch mutation or library-source patch is needed.
- **Each request gets:**
  - an explicit key: a placeholder for keyless local servers, since pi's direct calls require one
  - `env: {}`
  - `maxRetries: 0`, so retries happen only in our layer and are visible
  - the fiber's abort signal
  - our `fetch` (§2, outgoing requests)
  - `onResponse` for the status and `Retry-After`
  - `onProviderStreamEvent` for raw usage
- **The result:** provider configuration comes only from the connection, resolved model and explicit request options. Environment keys, custom headers, organization/project, cloud modes, cache retention and logging do not override them. The effective model/catalog headers join the request inventory before any capture or HTTP; connection headers retain their normal override precedence.
- **The pi model** is built per request from the merged description (`resolveModel`). When pi's catalog entry is for the same wire API, it's the base, so its request settings carry over. For example, DeepSeek's `max_tokens` field and reasoning replay come from the catalog (`test/providerStream.test.ts` checks the request body).
- **Catalogs** (`catalog.ts`) belong to their kind and load on first use. pi-ai's per-provider catalogs serve the built-ins.

**Audited environment defaults** (`providerEnvironment.ts`; pi-ai 1.0.3, openai 7.19.0, Anthropic 0.129.0, genai 2.21.0). Matching is case-insensitive, by whole family prefix so new SDK variables are covered:
- `OPENAI_`, `ANTHROPIC_`, `AZURE_OPENAI_`, `AWS_`, `GOOGLE_`, `GEMINI_`, `GCLOUD_`, `CLOUDSDK_`, `PI_`. These cover keys, organization/project, base URLs, cloud modes, profiles/credential files, headers, logging and cache retention, including `AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK` and lowercase Google auth aliases.
- pi-ai's other credential/OAuth families: `ANT_LING_`, `QWEN_`, `NVIDIA_`, `DEEPSEEK_`, `GROQ_`, `CEREBRAS_`, `XAI_`, `TYPESAFE_`, `RADIUS_`, `OPENROUTER_`, `AI_GATEWAY_`, `ZAI_`, `MISTRAL_`, `MINIMAX_`, `MOONSHOT_`, `HF_`, `FIREWORKS_`, `TOGETHER_`, `BASETEN_`, `OPENCODE_`, `KIMI_`, `META_`, `CLOUDFLARE_`, `XIAOMI_`, `COPILOT_`.
- Exact names, also lowercase: `HTTP_PROXY`, `HTTPS_PROXY`, `ALL_PROXY`, `NO_PROXY`, `NODE_USE_ENV_PROXY`. Node can initialize its environment proxy before JavaScript runs (including `--use-env-proxy`); provider requests still use explicitly routed undici dispatchers, never that global dispatcher.

Explicit request keys and disabled credential discovery keep Anthropic out of ambient profile/config-file chains. Google is the key-authenticated Gemini REST backend, with no cloud credential chain. `providerInventory.test.ts` poisons every audited name, scrubs it, and compares real HTTP requests, captured mappings, stored captures and Version events against a clean baseline. It also checks future prefix members, server-setting overlaps, and Google in a fresh Node process with authenticated ambient/configured proxies. `startup.test.ts` checks both real entry points.

**Connections** (`Providers`):
- `createCredential`, `replaceCredentialKey` and `allowCredentialOrigin` (which checks the retyped key) handle credentials, and `setHeaders` and `setProxy` encrypt the other secrets (§2) before calling storage (§3.12).
- `createConnection` and `setKey` accept a key typed for a connection: it becomes a credential named after the connection, bound to its origin.
- **Before a request or a model-list fetch,** the connection's origin is checked against its credential, before the key is even decrypted. An origin the credential doesn't allow is `unusable`: "enter the key again to allow it", and nothing is sent.
- `refreshModels` fetches the upstream's list (`modelLists.ts`; OpenAI-style, Anthropic and Google formats, decoded with Schema) and merges each model over pi's catalog. What the list reports wins: OpenRouter's context length, pricing, modalities and parameters, vLLM's `max_model_len`.
- `testConnection` fetches the list and reports the route the request took.

**Streaming** (`stream`): a `Stream` of events:
- `attempt`
- `route`, e.g. "direct: no proxy configured"
- `text` and `thinking` deltas
- `retrying`
- `done`, with pi's final message, the counts and the cost

It fails with a typed `ProviderError`.

- **Error classes** (`errors.ts`):
  - `unusable`: no key, an unreadable secret, a route the API can't take
  - `auth`, `rate_limited`, `overloaded`, `context_overflow`, `refused`, `bad_request`, `network`, `aborted`, `unknown`

  They're classified from pi's final message, the HTTP status (or the status the SDK left at the start of the message), and pi's overflow and retry checks. A refusal carries Anthropic's category when given.
- **Retries happen only before the first token:** `rate_limited`, `overloaded` and `network`, with `Retry-After` honored or exponential backoff. After output, a failure is never retried; it ends the stream with `afterTokens`.
- **Rate limits:**
  - The status and `Retry-After` are read in our own `fetch` wrapper, because pi-ai's `onResponse` only fires for successful responses (the SDKs throw first).
  - A `Retry-After` pauses the whole connection. A request waiting on it emits a `paused` event, and the pause is checked after taking a concurrency permit, so queued requests honor it too.
  - A pause longer than the retry policy's maximum (60 s by default) fails fast as `rate_limited` instead of stalling silently.
- **Overflow:** a completed generation is never turned into a failure afterwards. An error counts as `context_overflow` only against a context window that some source reported, never against the default used for unknown models.
- **The stream always ends.** Typed failures go through its queue, and a defect becomes a `ProviderError` (`unknown`) instead of leaving the stream open. An invalid base URL is an `unusable` error.
- **A per-connection cap** (`max_concurrent`) queues requests.
- **Every attempt is a ledger row** (§3.12), opened and closed with `acquireUseRelease`, so it closes on every exit:
  - a stopped stream aborts the HTTP request and closes the row as `aborted`, wherever the interruption lands
  - a call that throws instead of reporting, and a defect, close it as `failed`
- **Secrets never appear in errors.** Provider errors, including ordinary response errors published as `Version` events and model-list response bodies, use the same request inventory as context capture and ledger metadata. It covers keys, effective connection/catalog headers, bare Bearer tokens, Basic base64/decoded credentials and their username/password components, both proxies, and endpoint URLs with userinfo and encoded/decoded path/query values. Routes have no sensitivity annotations, so all their nonempty path segments and query values are treated conservatively as secrets [P]. This can redact ordinary matching text; cache comparison still uses the unredacted digest. Redaction replaces matches once, longest first, so short secrets cannot rewrite a redaction marker. Metadata is then capped.
- **Google:** the REST adapter requires the controlled fetch. Direct, instance/custom proxy routing, forced-proxy policy, redirects, status observation and aborts use the same `Network` transport as the other providers. Neither startup `--use-env-proxy`/`NODE_USE_ENV_PROXY` nor ambient proxy variables select its route.
- **A kind's wire API is fixed per connection.** If a plugin overrides a kind with another `api`, existing connections become `unusable` rather than silently speaking another API.
- **Forcing the proxy** (owner, 2026-10-11): with `forceProxy` in the network settings (admin), non-admins' connections ignore `direct` and custom proxies and use the instance proxy, and setting either is refused. The bypass list still applies; the admin sets it. Forcing needs a proxy (`setInstance` refuses it without one), and without a usable one, non-admins' requests are `unusable` rather than direct.

**Caller fields:** `options.payload` is set over the body pi-ai built, before the registered transforms run, for what the caller decides that pi-ai doesn't model: the context assembler's exact Chat Completions messages and per-provider samplers (§15). Objects merge key by key (Google's `config`); anything else replaces.

**Explicit cache breakpoints** (`breakpoints.ts`, PROVIDERS §8), for Anthropic Messages so far:
- **The caller names positions:** `options.breakpoints` lists indices into the request's `context.messages`, most important first.
- **The `onPayload` step finds their wire messages by alignment, not by index.** It walks the transcript and the payload in order and matches by role and text. pi-ai drops empty user messages, merges consecutive tool results and may append mid-conversation system messages, so positions shift.
- **The marker** goes on each message's last cacheable block (never a thinking block). It reuses pi-ai's own `cache_control` value, so TTLs match.
- **Limits:** nothing is added when pi placed no marker (caching off), and the total stays within Anthropic's four.
- **pi-ai's own markers stay:** the system prompt, the last tool, the last message.

**Usage and cost** (`usage.ts`):
- **Counts:** pi-ai's normalized usage (uncached input, cache reads, cache writes with Anthropic's 1-hour subset, output, reasoning) when the provider reported usage. The documented raw hit fields also normalize reads, including DeepSeek/Together fields that the compatible adapter can omit, Bedrock metadata, Google/Vertex usage and Fireworks' dedicated cached-token header. A reported header count survives tool-only, interrupted and failed output even when other counts remain unknown. Discounts alone never verify a hit.
  - The provider's raw usage object is kept with it. OpenRouter's reported cost and routed upstream are read from it.
  - Usage is estimated from the context and the output when the provider produced output without reporting usage, and unknown when nothing came back.
  - A stopped or failed request records as output at least the tokens of the text that arrived, marked `estimated` when that's more than reported. Anthropic's `message_start` reports only a placeholder output of 1.
- **Cost:** from the cost-formula registry (above). With the built-ins only: the provider's reported cost when there is one, otherwise the price table.
  - The formula uses per-million rates from the model's price (catalog or list), with the context-size tier on total input.
  - Long cache writes are priced at twice the input rate.
  - **User formulas (U12, 2026-10-28):** `@rp/shared/costFormulas.ts`, `providers/userCosts.ts`. The registered `user-pricing` contribution reads an owner-scoped snapshot supplied to each stream (shared across retries) and estimate. It is replaceable/disableable through the existing cost registry. `cost_settings` in the baseline stores each user's revision and rules; `GetCostSettings` and `SetCostSettings` run in reader/writer workers. Writes compare the expected revision, check connection ownership, and replace the rules in one transaction; foreign connections are `NotFound`. Each cost records `user:<rule-id>:<settings-revision>`; later changes or removal leave historical ledger costs untouched.
  - A rule names an exact model, optional provider kind, connection and exact reported upstream, a display-only currency label, and five non-negative per-million rates (uncached input, cache read, short cache write, long cache write and output including reasoning). Context tiers replace all rates for the whole request above their threshold, based on total input including cache reads and writes. A connection condition wins over kind/upstream-only conditions, then upstream, then kind, with the last rule breaking ties. Unmatched requests keep reported/catalog pricing. Unknown counts remain unknown. Validation limits rules to 100, tiers to 16 distinct non-negative integer thresholds, identifiers/names to 160 characters, labels to 16 and finite rates to 1e9. These declarative weighted sums never evaluate code.
  - **Admission:** registry entries can claim a reservation even without a point estimate (`reserves`); upstream-only overrides therefore cannot fall through to cheaper catalog bounds. The user contribution's upper bound covers every possible route before dispatch, every reachable tier (including decreasing rates), every cache partition and maximum output. Without a generic override it also covers catalog fallback. Unknown fallback prices or mixed currency labels return no bound, holding money-capped backfill before dispatch. Estimates without an upstream use the generic matching rule when no route can override it; otherwise the registered estimator quotes the conservative route bound, or unknown when currencies differ or fallback prices are missing. They do not guess a routed upstream.
  - **Editor:** the registered Settings → Costs route (`features/costs.tsx`, registered from `features/index.ts`) adds/removes formulas and tiers, edits all conditions/rates and labels, and saves explicitly. Non-secret edits use the existing server user-draft store and browser recovery copies, with field undo and recovered texts. Stale settings refuse the save and offer an explicit choice to carry the draft onto the saved revision; discard returns to saved pricing. Formula names wrap within the editor, with the remove action on its own row on phones, so even a maximum-length unbroken name keeps controls reachable. No past-row recompute action is implemented.
  - **Tests:** `userCosts.test.ts` exercises weights, exact matches, specificity, tier boundaries, unknown counts, validation, and conservative bounds; `providerStream.test.ts` checks worker persistence, an in-flight rule change, estimates and immutable historical costs through the real adapter; `userCostBackfill.test.ts` checks custom-currency estimates and model/upstream admission holds before the provider sees a request. The isolation matrix probes both new RPCs in both directions, including a foreign connection. `e2e/costs.spec.ts` covers explicit save, reload, drafts, tier editing and removal at 1440/390 in light/dark, including viewport bounds for the remove action beside a maximum-length unbroken name. Screenshots honor `SCREENSHOT_DIR`.

**Tested** (`test/providerStream.test.ts`) against a scripted provider speaking the real wire formats (`test/support/mockProvider.ts`):
- OpenRouter streaming with reported cost and upstream, and the built-in cost
- a 429 retried with each attempt recorded
- a mid-stream failure kept and not retried, a 401 not retried
- stopping, a missing key, Google through the configured proxy (including a failed proxy)
- Anthropic's cache split and catalog pricing
- explicit breakpoints through pi-ai's real Anthropic conversion
- DeepSeek's dialect
- a keyless local server

**Client RPCs** (`api/providers.ts`, contract in `@rp/shared/api.ts`):
- **Connections:** `ListConnections`, `CreateConnection` (with an existing credential or a typed key), `UpdateConnection`, `SetConnectionKey`, `SetConnectionCredential`, `SetConnectionHeaders`, `SetConnectionProxy`, `DeleteConnection`, `TestConnection`.
- **Credentials:** `ListCredentials`, `CreateCredential`, `RenameCredential`, `ReplaceCredentialKey`, `AllowCredentialHost` (the key typed again), `DeleteCredential`.
- **Models:** `RefreshModels`, `ListModels`, `AddUserModel`, `EditModel`, `SetModelHidden`.
- **Network and usage:** `GetNetworkSettings`/`SetNetworkSettings` (admins only, otherwise `Forbidden`), and `ListLlmRequests`.
- **Errors keep their tags** (`NotFound`, `StaleWrite`, `ModelKeyTaken`, `ProviderError`, `Forbidden`, from `@rp/shared/errors.ts`). Storage and worker failures are logged on the server and reach the client only as `ServerError`, including from the provider operations and network settings.
- `test/rpc.test.ts` checks over a real WebSocket that a key never comes back out. Cross-user access is checked by the isolation matrix (§4).
- **At startup,** requests a previous process left `running` are marked `interrupted`.


---

## 7. Deployment

`Dockerfile`: a multi-stage build on `node:24-slim`.
- The client and database workers are built in one stage. The final image holds only what runs: production dependencies, the root `package.json` and lockfile (the bundles' stamp includes it), the shared and server packages' `package.json` and `src`, the client build, worker bundles. No tests or scripts.
- `.dockerignore` keeps data directories, databases (`*.db*`, `*.sqlite*`), `secret.key`, `instance.lock` and `.env` files out of the build context at any depth (`**/`). Verified with throwaway builds: files planted in `packages/server/data/` (including `.sqlite`), `packages/shared/` (`.db-wal`, `.sqlite3`) and `packages/server/.env` didn't reach the image.
- It runs as the `node` user with `DATA_DIR=/data` as a volume, `HOST=0.0.0.0`, and a health check on `/healthz`.
- **It doesn't start without exposure settings** (§4), since `HOST=0.0.0.0` is beyond loopback; CI checks this. Connections from the host arrive from the bridge gateway (e.g. `172.17.0.1`).
  - Behind a reverse proxy: `AUTH_MODE=proxy`, `TRUSTED_PROXIES` set to the proxy container's address, and `PUBLIC_ORIGINS` set to the public URL.
  - Built-in login: set `ADMIN_PASSWORD_FILE` (a Docker secret). The server then starts with the admin protected, and no browser needs to reach loopback.
  - Single user without a password: publish on loopback only (`-p 127.0.0.1:8080:8080`) and set `ALLOWED_IPS` to the gateway.
  - Publishing beyond loopback (`-p 8080:8080`) makes the gateway allowlist unsafe: where Docker doesn't pass the client's address through (rootless Docker's default port driver, `docker-proxy` for IPv6 clients), outside clients also appear to come from it.
  - **The health check** fetches `127.0.0.1` on `PORT` inside the container. In proxy mode with `TRUSTED_PROXIES` set, add `127.0.0.1` to the list, or the container reports unhealthy. Inside a container, loopback is only the container's own processes, which can read `/data` and the server secret anyway.
- The image is about 325 MB on disk, unoptimized so far: it installs every workspace's production dependencies. Memory at idle is about 188 MB.

### ST differential harness

`npm run test:st` (`packages/server/test/st/`) checks the compatibility contract (DESIGN §1.2) against a stock SillyTavern. It's opt-in, outside `npm run check` and CI: it needs Docker, and a run takes about 4 minutes on the loaded host.
- **ST** is the official image at 1.19.0, pinned by digest (`container.ts`). It runs on the host network, listening on 127.0.0.1 at a random port, in whitelist mode with loopback only, as the calling user. Its config and data directories are fresh, under a temporary directory (`ST_DIFF_TMPDIR`, kept with `ST_DIFF_KEEP=1`). It never sees an existing ST install.
- **Cleanup:** the container and the directory go on any exit, signals included. The container's removal is registered before `docker run`. A run killed outright (`SIGKILL`) leaves both behind. The next run removes such a container only if it matches all of: a name starting `rp-st-diff-` and our label (`docker ps -a --filter name=^rp-st-diff- --filter label=rp-harness.st-diff.owner`, checked again in code), a label value naming this PID namespace, and an owner process that's gone. The directory stays until removed by hand. Nothing else is ever removed.
- **The mock endpoint** (`mock.ts`) is an OpenAI-compatible server on loopback. It records every request and answers with a fixed reply, streamed or not, so ST completes the turn.
- **Fixtures** (`fixtures.ts`) are synthetic. The card goes through our card importer and PNG exporter, and the lorebook through our World Info importer and exporter, so ST parsing them checks those too. The presets are the parser tests' fixtures. They're loaded through ST's HTTP API from a page, as its UI imports files. `settings.json` is written directly: ST's fresh-install settings with the custom source pointed at the mock, auto-connect, the fixture persona, and the presets' regex allowed. `bind_preset_to_connection` is off, so a preset can't switch the connection (connection data is out of scope).
- **ST builds prompts in the browser**, so the harness drives its UI with Playwright (`st.ts`). A capture opens a fresh page, selects the preset through ST's preset manager, opens the character's chat, and sends a message from the composer. It returns the body the mock received. Capture pages answer ST's write endpoints themselves, so every capture starts from the same files.
- **Macros run in ST's own engine** through `SillyTavern.getContext().substituteParams(text, { name1Override, name2Override })`, the entry point its prompt building uses. With an options object it runs the new `MacroEngine` when `experimental_macro_engine` is on, as it is in a fresh 1.19 install. The chat is open, the clock is fixed, the page runs in UTC, the variables are set per vector, and `Math.random` is reseeded per vector for `{{roll}}`. `{{random}}` can't be compared: ST draws it from an entropy-seeded generator.
- **Comparisons** (`comparisons.ts`), each a section of the report, with every difference as a JSON path and both values:
  - **The exported card, lorebook and chat:** with the baseline settings, ST's request carries their text, macros expanded.
  - **Macros:** each vector (`macroVectors.ts`) through ST and through our engine (`ourMacros.ts`, §10), comparing the output and the variables after it. Ours gets the context ST's page has: the vector's names and variables, the fixture card and chat, ST's model and token settings, the clock. The vectors are the engine's own tests (`packages/shared/test/macros/`) where ST can evaluate them, plus a few of the harness's. Left out: `{{random}}` (entropy) and `{{pick}}` (seeded differently, §10), and anything about origins, freezing or limits. A vector whose difference §10 documents names the line, and its differences are listed apart without failing the run.
