  - **Preset round trips:** each preset file, and its import and export through our format, loaded into ST for the same chat. The two requests must be identical.
    - ST applies only the settings a preset has, so a dropped setting would pass whenever the file's value is ST's default. So the file is loaded over the baseline (a fresh install's settings), and the round trip over a poison preset. In the poison preset, every setting ST applies differs from the baseline, taken from ST's own list (`settingsToUpdate`). Our importer fills a missing field with ST's default, as a fresh ST does, so the requests match only if the round trip carries every setting.
    - The file is captured twice, so an unstable capture isn't mistaken for a lossy round trip.
    - A preset whose request equals the baseline's is flagged as not applied.
  - **The context assembler** (`assembly.ts`, §15): for each of two gate cards, each preset and each generation type, the request ST sends against the one we build for the settings ST had in force (read from the page, through our preset importer), compared as JSON. ST's other generation types are started through `getContext().generate`, and its post-processing, a connection setting, is set on the page before sending.
- `ST_DIFF_ONLY=assembly` (comma-separated: `fixtures`, `macros`, `presets`, `assembly`) runs only those comparisons.
- **First results (2026-10-12):** the exported card, lorebook and chat all reach ST's request. The three preset fixtures (`default`, `full`, `legacy`) round-trip with identical requests, over the poison preset too. Checked against deliberate breakage, all reported: a card bound to a missing lorebook, a round trip with one sampler changed and a space added to one prompt, and a round trip without `temperature`, which only the poison preset reveals for `default`. All 318 macro vectors without a documented deviation match ST. The exit status is 1 when a difference isn't expected.

---

## 8. Import formats: cards and lorebooks

**Documents** (`@rp/shared/docs/`, Effect Schema). Each has a `schema_version`.
- **Card names:** standard `name` is the display name; `nickname` is the name sent to the model, falling back to `name` when absent or empty; `character_version` is the subtitle. All three travel with revisions and exported files.
- **Cards** (`card.ts`): the union of Character Card V2 and V3, in the specs' field names. Card modules are card notes (DESIGN §5.0): `modules[]` stays optional in the schema so older revisions decode, and only the 0049 migration reads it (§14.4).
  - `extensions` is the card's `data.extensions`, verbatim, so `extensions.<plugin-id>` survives (PLUGINS §3.3).
  - Keys the specs don't define are kept verbatim too: `unknown_fields` for those in `data`, `unknown_root_fields` for the top level. They can't go in `extensions`: an export would then write them to a different place, or a plugin's namespace could collide with them.
- **Lorebooks** (`lorebook.ts`): ST's World Info entry fields in snake_case, the same document for standalone lorebooks and a card's `character_book`. ST's numeric enums are named (`position: "at_depth"`, `role: "system"`, `selective_logic: "and_any"`). Each entry has a unique safe-integer `uid`, so the three-way merge (§3.9) matches entries by it. On import, usable ids are kept (the first wins), and every other entry, missing, invalid or duplicate, gets a number after the largest, so renumbering never takes an id another entry has. A card book id that isn't a whole number is kept in `unknown_fields` and written back on export. Our own fields are `decision_hint` and `placement_override`.

**Parsers** (`packages/server/src/formats/`) are pure functions over bytes: no database, no files. Each returns an `Effect` that fails with `FormatError` when the file can't be imported at all. Everything recoverable becomes an issue in the `ImportReport` (`@rp/shared/importReport.ts`) instead, so one bad field never costs the whole file.
- **Lenient fields** (`fields.ts`). A missing or null field takes its default quietly, as in ST. A field of the wrong type takes its default too, and the report names its path. Whole numbers must be safe integers (up to 2^53), as `Schema.Int` requires; file values are only converted to text when they are text or numbers, since an object's `toString` can throw.
- **Bounded reports** (`ReportBuilder`, `@rp/shared/importReport.ts`). At most 200 issues per file, then one `report.truncated` issue with the count. Paths keep 16 segments; file text quoted in a message or path is cut to 40 characters (`shownText`), and lists of file-supplied names to the first 10 plus a count (`shownList`). The preset importer (§9) uses the same builder.

**Card import** (`importCard`, `cardImport.ts`, `card.ts`). The format comes from the bytes, never the file name.
- **JSON:** detection follows ST: any `spec` with a `data` object is V2 or V3 (an unknown spec is read as V3, with a warning), otherwise a `name` means V1. V1 is mapped as ST maps it (its placeholder creator note dropped, comma-separated tags split).
- **ST's top-level copies** of V1 fields and its local state (`chat`, `create_date`, `fav`, `avatar`) aren't kept; other top-level keys are.
- **The embedded `character_book`** is mapped as ST's `convertCharacterBook` does: ST's World Info fields come out of each entry's `extensions` into the entry, with its defaults; whatever else is in `extensions` stays. One deliberate difference: the spec's own `case_sensitive` is the fallback when the extension has none (ST ignores it). An entry without `enabled` is disabled, as in ST, with a warning.
- **Card notes** an export carried stay in `extensions.rp_harness` for the card importer (`importers.ts` `exportedNotes`), which makes each of `notes` (and an older export's `modules`) a card note, keeping its id as `module_id` so a re-import finds it. An invalid entry is dropped and reported; an invalid placement becomes "with the definitions"; `agent_editable` is never read.
- **PNG** (`png.ts`): the card is base64 JSON in a `tEXt` chunk. As in ST (`src/character-card-parser.js`), `ccv3` wins over `chara`, keywords match in any case, and a bad CRC or a missing IEND rejects the file. The avatar returned is the PNG without its card chunks. V3's `chara-ext-asset_` chunks are skipped with a note (the spec discourages them; ST ignores them).
- **CHARX** (`zip.ts`, our own reader of the central directory; stored and deflated entries only): `card.json` at the root, or else, as in ST, the first entry whose name ends in `card.json`. It must be V2 or V3. Assets come from `embeded://`, `embedded://` and `__asset:` URIs, normalized as ST normalizes them. The avatar is the main icon, as ST picks it. Only ST's image formats are extracted; other assets (audio, Risu modules and scripts) and unreferenced files are reported and skipped. Each returned file keeps the URI the card's `assets` use for it.

**Card export** (`cardExport.ts`) flattens a card document to a standard card.
- **V2 and V3 carry the same `data`,** V3's fields included, as ST's two PNG chunks do. V2 also has the V1 fields at the top level, for V1 readers. Fields the specs don't define go back where they came from, written first, so they never override a defined field.
- **The embedded lorebook** is written as ST's `convertWorldInfoToCharacterBook` writes it, our own entry fields in `extensions.rp_harness`. One difference: `use_regex` is false unless the entry says otherwise. ST writes true, but its keys are plain text, which a V3 reader would take as patterns. An imported `true` is kept, though it's usually ST's boilerplate: ST never reads the field. The keyword matcher ignores `use_regex` and recognizes ST slash syntax (DESIGN §1.3).
- **Card notes** (`importer/exporters.ts` `withNotes`): ST's Character's Note to `depth_prompt` as before (the first note when it is exportable, in chat and on in every chat; one that is not goes with the others, so an import does not switch it on everywhere), and every other exportable card note into `extensions.rp_harness.notes` (id, title, content, placement, `scan_lore`, `on_by_default`; a linked one as its shared note's text, which `ExportSource` puts in it). Private notes never leave; conditions stay behind, since they name cards by this library's ids.
- **PNG:** existing card chunks are removed, then `chara` (V2) and `ccv3` (V3) go in before IEND, as ST writes them. The image must be a valid PNG.
- **CHARX:** `card.json` (V3), the avatar as the main icon (at its own path if it came from a CHARX, otherwise `assets/icon/images/main.<ext>`), and each embedded asset whose file is given. An embedded asset without a file, with a path outside the archive's folder, or with a name already taken (`card.json`, the avatar's, another asset's) is left out of the card, so our own import takes every export back.
- **Round trips are lossless:** an exported card imports back to the same document, in every format, and keeps every field of the file it came from (`test/formats/cardExport.test.ts`). The exceptions are ST's top-level copies and local state, and private modules.

**Lorebooks** (`worldInfo.ts`, `lorebook.ts`).
- **ST World Info files** import with `importLorebook` and export with `exportLorebook`. A file must have `entries` (an object keyed by uid, or a list), as ST's import requires. Missing fields take the defaults of ST's `newWorldInfoEntryDefinition`. Entry `extensions` are kept verbatim, as ST keeps them. The book is named after the file unless it names itself. ST's `originalData`, a copy of the card book a World Info file was made from that goes stale with every edit, is dropped with a note.
- **A card's `character_book` and World Info** convert through the same document, following ST's two conversions (`convertCharacterBook`, `convertWorldInfoToCharacterBook`). What one format has no place for is lost going to the other, as in ST: a card book's settings (description, scan depth, token budget, recursive scanning) and its entries' `name`, `priority` and `use_regex` don't go into World Info, and World Info's character filter doesn't go into a card book.
- **Our own entry fields** (`decision_hint`, `placement_override`) are written to `extensions.rp_harness` in both formats and read back from there.
- Round trips are lossless within each format: an export keeps every field of the file it came from (except `originalData`) and imports back to the same document.
- Not supported: other lorebook formats ST converts (NovelAI, Agnai, RisuAI), and V3's standalone `lorebook_v3`.

**Hardening** (`safety.ts`). These parse untrusted files, so every input is capped (`defaultLimits`), and each cap has a test with a crafted file that fails when the check is removed.

| Cap | Default | Why |
|---|---|---|
| File size | 64 MiB | Bounds memory and work for everything below |
| Card JSON | 8 MiB | About 2M tokens of text, more than any model's context holds: a bigger card couldn't be used. Applies to a JSON file, a PNG's card chunk once decoded, and a CHARX's `card.json` |
| World Info JSON | 32 MiB | Lorebooks are scanned, not sent whole, so they can outgrow a context: a full lorebook with 2 KB of content per entry fits. The value cap, not this one, bounds the parse work |
| JSON nesting | 64 levels | Real cards nest about 8 deep. Counted in the bytes before parsing, which also bounds the recursive walk after it |
| JSON objects and lists | 250,000 | Counted before parsing: 10M empty objects in 32 MB cost `JSON.parse` 1.1 GB. A full lorebook has about 60,000 |
| JSON values and keys | 1,200,000 | Counted before parsing (every comma and colon): parsing costs about 1 µs each, and a 30 MiB list of zeros took 4 s and 1.1 GB without it. ST writes about 110 per entry, so a full lorebook has 1.1M |
| Prototype keys | 100 | Each is dropped with a warning; more make the file hostile |
| Lorebook entries | 10,000 | An assumption to revisit with real files: a lorebook of a few thousand entries is already exceptional. 20,000 full ST entries would need 2.2M values, about 2 s of parsing |
| PNG chunks | 16,384 | Bounds the chunk walk |
| PNG chunk size | 32 MiB | Checked against the declared length before anything is read |
| Zip entries | 1,024 | Bounds the directory walk |
| Zip entry, uncompressed | 64 MiB | Checked against the declared size before inflating |
| Zip total, uncompressed | 128 MiB | Over every entry extracted from one archive |
| Compression ratio | 100 | For entries over 1 MiB; a zip bomb's is in the thousands |

- **Inflating stops at the declared size** (`maxOutputLength`), so a header that understates an entry can't make the parser allocate more than the caps allow (`zip.bomb`). Sizes and CRCs are checked after.
- **Zip paths** must stay inside the archive: no `..` segment, leading `/`, backslash, drive letter or NUL. One bad path, duplicate name or encrypted entry rejects the whole archive, so an importer that later writes files by these names can't be pointed elsewhere.
- **Prototype keys** (`__proto__`, `constructor`, `prototype`) are dropped right after `JSON.parse`, with a warning. `JSON.parse` makes them plain own keys, but a later assignment, `Object.assign` or merge can turn one into a prototype change.
- **Nothing in a file is executed or rendered.** Scripts in a card (RisuAI's modules, Lua assets) are skipped, never stored.

**Known limits:**
- **The importer must run the parsers off the main thread.** They're synchronous: measured on this host under load (2026-10-11, load average 25), the worst file within the caps (one object of 595,000 keys) took 850 ms and 80 MB of heap, a list of 1.19M short strings 460 ms, and a full lorebook (10,000 entries with every ST field and 2 KB of content, 29 MiB) 480 ms. The walk after parsing steps through lists by index and skips scalars, and unknown fields and extensions are kept by deleting the known keys from the parsed objects instead of copying them. On the main thread that would stall every live stream (§3.1).
- The registered card and lorebook importers store documents, avatars/assets and reports through the format workers and import workflow (§11; PLUGINS §8).

---

## 9. Import formats: presets

ST chat-completion presets (DESIGN §1.2). Pure functions with no database access; the importer that writes into the library comes later.

**The document** (`@rp/shared/docs/preset.ts`, `PresetDoc`, `schema_version` 1) is the prompt portion of ST's chat-completion preset, normalized:
- `prompts[]`, keyed by `identifier`, with ST's per-prompt fields: role, content, `system_prompt`, `marker`, in-chat injection (`injection_position` `absolute`, at `injection_depth`, by `injection_order`), `injection_trigger` and `forbid_overrides`.
- `order[]`: one prompt order with enabled flags (DATA-MODEL §16.3). Every entry names a prompt; the end-of-request texts are never in it (below). Import appends prompts outside the ST order, off, and reports their names; the markers ST still sends when its order leaves them out (world info before and after, description, personality, scenario, persona description) are appended on instead, in ST's order, so the request is unchanged. `presetOrder.ts` presents older revisions the same way without rewriting them. The `newestMessage` marker defaults to immediately after `chatHistory`; export preserves its position for reimport.
- `utility_prompts{}`: text used in specific situations. Model settings are separate (below).
- `pin_examples`, `strip_examples`, `collapse_newlines`: optional prompt-content switches (U20, 2026-10-28), absent means false for older revisions. ST stores these globally in `power_user`, outside its chat-completion presets; our importer/exporter carries explicit top-level values as a portable extension. A folder import copies the source `settings.json` power-user values into each imported preset and includes them in its reimport hash, so changing only those settings updates the preset. They stay on the prompt preset, never on model settings. The registered preset editor exposes them under Text and context using the existing server drafts and revisions.
- `prefill{assistant, impersonation, continue, continue_postfix}` keeps the text and continuation behavior. Wire formatting lives on the model.
- `regex[]`, keyed by `id`: ST's regex scripts as data. `prompt_only` scripts apply to the assembled prompt, `markdown_only` ones are display rules.
- `regex_approved`: whether the user allowed this preset's scripts to run, as ST asks once per preset. **The assembler and the display must run none of them, prompt or display, until it's true.** An import always sets it `false` and drops a `regex_approved` field in the file, and export never writes it (DESIGN §11: an imported file never grants itself capabilities).
- `extensions{}`: the preset's other ST extension data. `unsupported{}`: top-level ST fields the document doesn't model. Both are kept for export.
- The schema refuses duplicate identifiers and order entries that name no prompt, since the library merge (§3.9) matches array elements by identity.

**Legacy parsing** (`packages/server/src/formats/preset.ts`, `importStPreset`): a file's bytes or text to the document plus an `ImportReport` (`@rp/shared/importReport.ts`). Report messages are plain text that quotes the file: clients render them as text, never as Markdown or HTML. The import follows ST's own loading (`openai.js`, `PromptManager.js`):
- **What it accepts.** A file with any field only chat-completion presets have. Instruct, context, system prompt and reasoning templates, text-completion and NovelAI presets, and Advanced Formatting exports are recognized by ST's own tests and reported as unsupported, with no document (DESIGN §1.2). Anything else is an error.
- **ST's migrations:** `names_in_completion`, `custom_prompt_post_processing: "claude"`, the old inlining and system-prompt flags, and `main_prompt`/`nsfw_prompt`/`jailbreak_prompt` into the prompt list.
- **Missing fields get ST's defaults** (`default_settings`), and the report lists them. ST itself keeps the previously loaded preset's value for a missing field; a fresh ST has the defaults.
- **Prompts are sanitized as ST does:** the built-in prompts are added when missing, a prompt without an identifier gets one that nothing in the file uses (ST's random one means it's never sent), and of duplicate identifiers only the first counts. A prompt without `system_prompt` is `true`, because ST sends a relative prompt from the order only when the flag is exactly `false`.
- **One prompt order:** ST uses only the global one (`character_id` 100001), or its default order when there's none. Other orders are dropped and named in the report. A global order without a list of entries is empty in ST, so ST would send none of the preset's prompts; the import uses the default order instead and says so. Entries naming no prompt are dropped, as in ST. A prompt listed more than once is kept once. An in-chat prompt is enabled if any entry is, and since ST injects it once per enabled entry, a repeat is a warning. A relative prompt keeps its first entry, which is the one ST checks.
- **Prompts ST never sends are imported disabled**, with a warning: an in-chat prompt with a depth that isn't an integer from 0 to 10000, or with no valid role (the markers ST fills, like `charDescription`, default to `system`), and any prompt whose non-empty list of generation types has no valid entry.
- **Values go through ST's conversions:** numbers through `Number()`, checkboxes by truthiness, text as strings. An unusable value gets the default and a warning, including text fields of prompts and regex scripts that hold something else.
- **Regex depths as ST reads them:** a minimum below -1 or a maximum below 0 is no bound. An infinite maximum is no bound; an infinite minimum, which excludes every message depth, becomes `Number.MAX_VALUE`. Both are reported.
- **Connection and model fields are dropped** and listed in one info entry (DESIGN §1.2). They include proxy URLs and passwords, which are never stored. `custom_prompt_post_processing` is a connection field in ST but is kept, as `postprocess`.
- **What's kept for export:** other fields go to `unsupported{}`. A known ST field set to a non-default value is a warning; an unknown field is info. A prompt's or regex script's unknown fields go to its own `unsupported{}`. Other `extensions` entries are kept and reported.
- **Regex scripts** come from `extensions.regex_scripts`, as data. Ids are kept; a missing or repeated one gets an id nothing in the file uses.

**Legacy export** (`exportStPreset`, used by the differential fixtures): the document as an ST chat-completion preset, with the `unsupported{}` fields and `extensions` back in place and the regex scripts in `extensions.regex_scripts`. The one order is written as the global order, which is the only one ST reads. No connection or model data is written, so ST keeps its current connection when it loads the file. Modelled fields win over an `unsupported{}` entry of the same name.
- Tested on the fixtures: every field ST reads comes back with the value ST would use, and importing the export gives the same document, with nothing dropped or defaulted the second time.
- **Differences from the source file** that ST treats the same: missing fields are written with ST's defaults, prompts get all their fields explicitly, numbers written as strings become numbers, and regex depths ST ignores become `null`.

**Hardening** (the file is untrusted, review tier A). `importStPreset` never throws on a file's content; every problem goes in the report.
- **Parsing goes through the shared `parseJson`** (`formats/safety.ts`, §8), with a preset cap of 2 MB (`MAX_PRESET_BYTES`, in bytes) and the shared limits on depth, containers, values and prototype keys, all checked before `JSON.parse`. ST's bundled default preset is 8 KB, so 2 MB is 250 times it. A 2 MB import takes about half a second of CPU on the shared host; 8 MB took 3.4 s. The parser's `FormatError` becomes the report's one error.
- **Prototype keys** (`__proto__`, `constructor`, `prototype`) are dropped at any depth with a warning each. An own `constructor` key can't reach a prototype by assignment, but a deep merge walking `target.constructor.prototype` can, and the library merges documents (§3.9).
- **Conversions:** `String()` and `Number()` only ever see primitives. An object whose `toString` and `valueOf` aren't functions would make them throw.
- **Nothing is executed.** Regex scripts are stored as strings and never compiled. **Compiling one later needs a ReDoS guard**: a pattern like `(a+)+$` takes exponential time on some inputs, and a script runs on every message.
- **The report is bounded** by the shared `ReportBuilder` (§8): 200 issues, then one `report.truncated`, with file text, lists and paths shortened, so an issue is about 1.5 KB at most.
- **Every output matches the schema:** a seeded fuzz test mutates the fixtures with hostile values (infinities, wrong types, unconvertible objects) and decodes every document the importer returns against `PresetDoc`.
- **Linear time:** lookups go through maps and sets, including the renaming of repeated regex ids, which was quadratic at first (30,000 repeats took 46 s).
- Each limit's test was checked against its bug: with the check disabled, the test fails.

---

**Conditioned prompts and the end-of-request texts** (`@rp/shared/presetConditions.ts`, `@rp/shared/endOfRequest.ts`; DESIGN §2.2, owner 2026-10-27 and 2026-10-29). Who the model writes as and the one-turn direction reach the model through the preset:
- Every prompt of the order has a `condition`: `always`, `as_character` (the model writes as any card the user doesn't play), `as_user` (it writes as a card in the played-by-me set) or `with_direction` (the generation has a non-blank direction). Absent means `always`.
- **ST's triggers stay a filter of their own beside the condition** (owner 2026-10-28): `injection_trigger` lists the generation types a prompt is sent for, as ST's `shouldTrigger` (none: every type), and both it and the condition must allow the prompt (`triggersFor`, `collectionOf`). The types are ST's: `normal` (a first reply), `swipe` (a new version of a reply, which also matches `regenerate`: there's no regenerate that deletes the reply), `impersonate` (the speaker is a card the user plays), `continue` and `quiet` (a summary request). The import keeps a prompt's triggers as they are and maps none onto a condition, so a `normal`-only prompt isn't sent on swipes, as in ST. The preset editor shows them as **Generation types** checkboxes on every prompt (Normal, Continue, Impersonate, Swipe, Regenerate, Quiet; none ticked: every type), with a badge in the prompt's header when any is ticked.
- **The end-of-request texts** are three prompts of the preset with fixed identifiers, so layering, the library merge (§3.9), revisions and drafts treat them as any prompt: `groupNudge` ("When a character speaks"), `impersonationPrompt` ("When your card speaks") and `directionPrompt` ("When you give a direction"). They're never in the order, have no condition and ignore ST's triggers; the assembler sends them as the request's last message (§15 "Who writes, and the direction"). Defaults: `[Write {{speaker}}'s next message. Write only for {{speaker}}.]` for both turn texts and `[What happens next: {{direction}}]` for the direction, each closing its own bracket. The built-in preset and newly created prompt presets use these defaults; ST imports retain their own nudge and impersonation wording.
- **`withEndOfRequest`** gives a preset its three texts: ST's `group_nudge_prompt` becomes `groupNudge` with `{{char}}` as `{{speaker}}`, ST's `impersonation_prompt` becomes `impersonationPrompt` with `{{user}}` as `{{speaker}}` (each reported), any text still missing gets its default (an empty ST field keeps ours), and none of the three stays in the order. Their `utility_prompts` fields are emptied as they move, so the move happens once; export writes them back (below). The assembler never reads those two fields. It's pure and idempotent. The import runs it (so ST's built-in default preset, read through the same import, has the texts too), and so does every read of a stored preset: the assembler's preset source, the editors' `OpenEditor` and `CommitEditorDrafts` (`editedDoc`, also for a draft's base revision), the impersonation notice and export. No revision is written until the user saves; the first save writes the converted document. `presetOrder` never lists the three, even in an order that names them. There's no data migration: deploys start from an empty database.
- **Export**: `groupNudge` and `impersonationPrompt` go back into `group_nudge_prompt` and `impersonation_prompt` with `{{char}}`/`{{user}}` restored, and the three are left out of `prompts` and the order. **`directionPrompt` isn't exported**: ST has no field for it, so a reimport has the default direction text. A prompt's triggers go out as they are, and a condition becomes the types it allows (`as_user`: `impersonate`; `as_character`: `normal`, `swipe`, `regenerate`, `continue`), narrowed to the prompt's own triggers when it has any (`triggersWithCondition`); a condition that isn't `always` is also written as a `condition` field, which ST ignores and our import reads back; a `with_direction` prompt is written off in the order, since ST would send it every turn, with a `sent_with_direction` field, so an export reimports to the same document. **A prompt whose condition and generation types never meet** (`gatesNeverMeet`: none of its triggers is a type its condition can hold for here, as the assembler decides it: `as_user` holds for `impersonate` and `continue`, `as_character` for `normal`, `swipe`, `regenerate` and `continue`, `with_direction` for all but `quiet`, which only `always` prompts are sent for) is never sent here, so it goes out **off** in the order, its triggers as they are (ST would otherwise send it on them: "when writing as your card" with only Normal would run on every normal turn there), with an `enabled_here` field holding its switch here; the import turns it back on when that's true and its gates still never meet, so the export reimports to the same document. Turned off here, it stays off.

**The three import surfaces** (`formats/presetSettings.ts`). Normal file and folder library imports use `importPromptPreset`: prompts, order, roles, injection positions, utility prompts, prefill text and regex data. Samplers, budgets, wire formatting and model/provider options are excluded, with an explicit report. The legacy parser remains for format compatibility and the ST differential oracle; it is not the library import surface. Normal exports use `exportPromptPreset`, so they carry no samplers or wire settings. The exporter hydrates legacy-only fields from a recognized chat-completion default before validation, allowing prompt-only revisions to export without restoring model settings to the file. Known secret fields are discarded rather than retained in the unsupported round-trip bag.

`PreviewStSettings` parses a supplied file in the format worker and returns a bounded report and only the requested settings portion. The shared model editor advances its write version from each successful save, including when kept open in the composer panel. On a model's settings screen, the model import loads samplers, context/reply budget, wire formatting, stop strings, logit bias, streaming and supported routing settings into the form; Save applies it through `EditModel`. Connections offers a separate import taking API type, URL and model id, creating a keyless connection and optional user model through the existing operations. URL credentials are refused; keys, passwords and unrelated content are ignored and never echoed. Each surface reports ignored fields. These settings imports do not run during folder import. The folder allowlist still excludes `secrets.json` and connection settings; ST's selected prompt preset is adopted under §13's existing rules.

---

## 10. Macros

`{{user}}` follows the single selected persona (its in-chat model name), or the pinned `macro.binding` text, including an explicitly empty pin; without a selected persona it is `User`. It never joins persona names. Other persona cards in the cast are ordinary characters until selected.

**Opt-in display transformations** (U10, 2026-10-28; `client/src/features/chat/displayPipeline.ts`, `registerDisplay.tsx`). Human and model message text stays verbatim by default, while template greetings retain their existing expansion. The registered **Display** chat panel has two independent account choices, `outputMacros` and `displayRegex`, both off by default and merged by the existing worker-owned `SetDisplayPrefs` transaction. Output macro expansion applies only to finished model text and uses the position's names and speaker fields with the shared display-only macro flag; it drops variable effects. Streaming stays literal until completion. The viewer's choices never enter assembly: editing, copying, storage and later requests use the original version text.

The reversible `displayTransforms` registry runs up to 20 ordered transformations; built-ins are `display.macros` then `display.preset-regex`. The preset reader and panel are registered through `chatSurfaceProviders` and `chatPanels`. Regex reads only the current preset's committed document and requires both the viewer switch and `regex_approved`. Library invalidation, cross-tab library signals, window focus and server context notifications refresh it, so a script edit or approval change from another tab or device reaches an open chat even when the preset id and assembled prompt stay unchanged. Only enabled `markdown_only && !prompt_only` scripts run, with user/assistant-side placement (as in prompt assembly, independent of who wrote the version) and message depth relative to the viewed fork's tip. An off-window tip is read with a one-message `ReadPath`; bounded-depth scripts wait with an explanation until that read succeeds. Unsaved drafts, reasoning, prompt-only and storage rewrite scripts never enter this display runner. `run_on_edit` does not gate ordinary rendering; it belongs to edit-time rewriting. Capture replacements and trim strings use the same pure `@rp/shared/regexReplace.ts` runner as prompt regex. Replacement/trim macros and raw or escaped pattern macros use the bounded macro engine; escaped scoped block macros are skipped with an explanation.

Imported regex executes in a separate browser worker, with one queued worker active at a time, at most 128 pending jobs, and a 500 ms deadline including startup. An overflowing queue keeps the original text with an explanation. Every job terminates its worker, on success or failure. Limits per message: 200,000 input/result characters, 100 scripts, 2,000 pattern characters, 100 trim strings and 1,000,000 produced characters across replacements. Invalid or oversized scripts keep their input with a plain-text explanation. A macro limit in a pattern, replacement or trim string skips that script with an explanation too, preserving its input rather than using an unresolved macro as regex data. Timeout discards the job's partial work and shows its original input with an explanation. The normal Markdown sanitizer handles transformed output. `displayPipeline.test.ts` covers opt-in, human/assembly preservation, registry override/disable/restore, script filtering, depths, captures, trimming and limits. `e2e/displayTransforms.spec.ts` covers approval (including revocation and reapproval from another editor tab and a separate RPC session), saved viewer choices, rendered Markdown, original editor/storage text and the next provider request at 1440/390 in light/dark under the production CSP.

`packages/shared/src/macros/` (DESIGN §5.6): a pure engine, for the server's context assembler and for the client when it renders text. It follows SillyTavern's current macro engine (`public/scripts/macros/`, ST's default), read at ST commit `06bde939`.

**Expanding text** (`expand.ts`): `expand({ text, origin, purpose, displayRule?, registry, context, frozen?, random, limits? })` returns `Expanded` or `LimitExceeded`. It's the module's only way in, so the origin check can't be skipped.
- **`origin` and `purpose` are required** and decide whether anything expands:
  - card and preset text always does (lorebook entries and notes count as card text)
  - a chat message does when its stored macro mode is `template`, whoever wrote it (DATA-MODEL §15, invariant 7). The defaults stay as DESIGN §5.6 says: greetings are `template`, typed and generated text `verbatim`.
  - for `purpose: "display"`, the opt-in display rule (`displayRule: true`) expands verbatim messages too
  - display never changes state: its variable writes are dropped
- **`context` is the state at the text's position** (`context.ts`): names, the card's fields as stored (unexpanded), the messages before it, the clock and time zone, and whatever else ST's macros read. Card text expanded against it follows the state, so `{{user}}` in a greeting follows a persona change until the message is sent.
- **The caller's side of the contract:**
  - `messages[].text` is resolved text, as shown: a macro's output is never expanded again, so a template message passed raw would make `{{lastMessage}}` say `{{user}} said hi`.
  - Re-rendering or re-assembling a message passes the state from before that message's own `var.set` events, with its frozen values. Otherwise `{{incvar}}` in a greeting would count twice.
- **Freezing.** Random, roll, pick and the time macros are `volatile`. Each value drawn is keyed by its text (a card field's name, or none), offset, macro, its source as written (not its expanded arguments, so a name inside them doesn't move it), and repeat count. The result returns them as `frozen`, and passing them back makes a re-expansion identical, whatever the clock or random source. The time macros (`volatile: "clock"`) freeze the time they read, as epoch milliseconds, and work their value out from it again, so a date format naming `{{char}}` keeps its date and follows the name. Random, pick and roll freeze their value, and a frozen value the macro couldn't produce with its arguments now (not one of the choices, outside the dice's range; a clock outside 1900 to 2200) is drawn again (`accepts`), so moved text, changed arguments and forged values all draw again. Stored entries that aren't strings are ignored.
- **Variables are effects** (`variables.ts`). `context.variables` holds the chat's variables (`local`: the chat state's `vars`) and the global ones. Macros work on an overlay, and every write comes back, in order, in `effects` as `{ scope, name, value }`, with `value: null` for a deletion. `variables` is the state after the text, to pass to the next text expanded at the same position. The caller records local effects as `var.set` events. Global variables come back as effects too, but nothing persists them yet (DATA-MODEL §16).
- ST's variable rules are kept: values are stored as given, numeric text reads as a number (`007` → `7`), addvar adds, appends to a JSON array or concatenates, and the shorthands (`{{.name}}`, `{{$name}}`, `=`, `+=`, `-=`, `++`, `--`, `||`, `??`, `||=`, `??=`, comparisons) only expand their value when it's needed. Names are own keys only, so unlike ST `{{hasvar::constructor}}` is false.
- **Variable values are bounded.** A write that would produce a value over 1 MB is refused (ST has no cap): the macro produces nothing, no effect is recorded, and the result lists it in `refusedWrites`, so the caller can show it. Reading a value costs its length once per write, since the read and the JSON parse are cached; a shorthand comparison or test costs the value's length each time, and so does each message the last-message macros and `{{idle_duration}}` look at.

**The registry** (`registry.ts`, `builtins.ts`). Macros register by name and aliases, matched case-insensitively, through the generic registry (`@rp/shared/registry.ts`). The built-ins register through it like a plugin. A definition declares positional arguments (optional ones last, some whole numbers only), an optional list tail, delayed argument resolution (for `{{if}}`), and `volatile`. Arguments are strict, ST's default: a call with the wrong number of arguments, or a non-number where a whole number is expected, stays as written.

**The parser** (`parse.ts`) is a hand-written scanner in place of ST's Chevrotain grammar: no regex that can backtrack, each macro start parsed once, nested macros parsed with an explicit stack instead of recursion. A macro that's never closed stays text however deep it goes, as in ST.

**Hardening.** Imported card text is untrusted, so one expansion is bounded (`MacroLimits`, overridable per call):
- **Depth:** 32 macros evaluated inside one another (arguments, blocks, `{{if}}` branches, card fields), so deep nesting can't overflow the stack.
- **Expansions:** 10,000 macros evaluated.
- **Length:** 256K characters (262,144) in any one text, refused before parsing. That's about 60K tokens, far above any real card field, lorebook entry or preset prompt. The cap applies to each piece separately, since each is expanded on its own: a preset prompt, a card field, a greeting, a note, an `{{if}}` branch. It keeps the worst input to a fraction of a second on the server's shared event loop.
- **Work:** 4,000,000 units, about one per character produced by a macro, read from or written to a variable, or looked at by the parser, and one per die rolled. Pairing blocks costs 40 per macro, measured against parsing (the maps and sets it builds), plus one per entry its search looks at. This bounds the output and the time taken, and stops values that double. Each level pairs its block's content again (as ST does), and that's counted too, so deeply nested blocks can't cost depth × size for free.
- **The parser charges as it goes**, every 64K steps, so a budget that runs out stops it partway. The reviews' worst inputs at full size under the cap are tests: each stops within the default limits. Measured best of five on this host at load 46–60, each took 38–173 ms.
- Handlers count large output before building it (`{{space::N}}`, `{{newline::N}}`, dice, and `{{datetimeformat}}` at 13 characters per format character, its most).
- **Reaching a limit fails the whole text** with `LimitExceeded`: nothing partial comes back. It says which limit, and where: `scope` is `""` for the text itself or the card field it happened in (`card.description`), and `offset` is the innermost macro being evaluated. The caller fails that one piece (a preset prompt, a greeting) with a visible error saying where, not the whole turn.
- **Linear time:** no regex that backtracks (ST's `{{trim}}` regex, quadratic on runs of newlines, is a scan here); recovery after a name uses a precomputed table; pairing blocks searches square-root blocks of running sums, so many unclosed openers cost about n√n. The parser and the pairing count their steps as work, so the tests assert linear time as a work bound rather than a time.
- **No code evaluation:** nothing calls `eval`, `Function` or a dynamic import, and a test scans the sources for them.
- **Untrusted names** are looked up as own keys or in `Map`s: variables, frozen values, outlets, macro names.
- **Nothing throws out of an expansion** for bad input: a macro whose handler fails stays as written, as in ST, and so does a variable shorthand whose operation fails (deeply nested JSON, say), where ST gives up on the whole text.
- Each guard's test was checked against its bug: with the check disabled, the test fails or times out.

**ST's behavior reproduced** (vectors in `packages/shared/test/macros/`, most from ST's `tests/frontend/MacroEngine.e2e.js`):
- arguments as `{{a::b::c}}`, `{{a:b}}` or `{{a b}}`, trimmed; after a single colon or a space the rest is one argument
- nested macros evaluated inside-out, left to right; an unknown or wrongly called macro stays as written, its nested macros expanded
- scoped blocks `{{name}}…{{/name}}` as the last argument, trimmed and dedented unless the opener has `#`
- `{{if}}`/`{{else}}` with only the chosen branch expanded; `{{trim}}`; `\{` escapes; the legacy `<USER>`, `<BOT>`, `<CHAR>`, `<GROUP>`, `<CHARIFNOTGROUP>` and `{{time_UTC+2}}` forms
- card fields expanded once per expansion, on their own, with card-field macros inside them empty
- moment's `en` formats and humanize thresholds for the time macros
- quirks, each with a test: `{{char's}}` resolves like `{{char}}` (Chevrotain drops what can't follow a name); an unknown macro with a block comes back as `{{foo}}bar}}`; a comment's argument is still evaluated; `{{if}}` matches `if` and `else` case-sensitively when splitting

**Deliberately different from ST:**
- Text completion is out of scope (DESIGN §1.2), so the instruct, system-prompt and context-template macros resolve as in ST with instruct mode off, instead of reaching the model as text (`builtins/prompts.ts`): instruct sequences are empty, `{{systemPrompt}}` is the card's own system prompt, or empty without one, where ST's default returns its default system prompt ("Write {{char}}'s next reply in a fictional chat between {{char}} and {{user}}."), a text-completion prompt that doesn't exist here, `{{defaultSystemPrompt}}` is empty, and `{{chatStart}}` and `{{exampleSeparator}}` are ST's default `***`.
- `{{pick}}` is seeded like ST's but without seedrandom, so it picks differently for the same seed.
- Where ST's parser recovers from a malformed macro (a bad nested macro, a stray operator), the macro stays literal here and scanning resumes after its `{{`. ST's recovery depends on the input and can swallow text after it.
- `{{hasExtension}}` is always false: ST extensions don't exist here.
- Names inside a card field's macros resolve against the same bindings as the text around them. ST resolves them from the page's persona and character and ignores the call's name overrides. That's the rebinding DESIGN §5.3 moves away from: a card field nested in a prompt follows that prompt's bindings.

---

Card name bindings use `nickname || name` throughout assembly: card blocks, greetings, example messages, transcript speaker names, group nudges, notes, lorebook keys and postprocessing. Browser greeting/template expansion uses the same effective name. UI cast metadata carries the revision's display name, model name and subtitle; pinned and chat-local card bindings resolve all of them from the selected revision, together with its avatar.

## 11. Importing

Files into the library and back out (DATA-MODEL §3, §11, §14; PLUGINS §8). The parsers of §8 and §9 do the reading; this section is what stores their output.

### 11.1 Storage

Card re-imports update only the ref named by `into` or matched by the ST folder’s `source_path`. The card’s own edits are three-way merged against its previous import, with conflicts kept pending for the normal update resolver. Card edits, imports and resolved updates return no follower outcomes; forks carry no source path/hash and keep their content and notes. The folder importer already routes through `ImportFile`; it creates no card variants or overrides. Non-card refs retain following and merge propagation.

One write operation, `ImportFile` (`db/libraryWrites.ts`), stores a parsed file in one transaction:
- **A new file** becomes an entity, an `upstream` ref and a revision with `origin = import`. The ref records the file's name (`source_path`) and the SHA-256 of its bytes (`source_hash`), and the revision's `origin_ref` both, as JSON.
- **A re-import** names the upstream ref (`intoRefId`). As §3.9 describes: a new revision on the import line, merged into the upstream; non-card updates then reach everything following it, transitively. The result lists non-card follower outcomes with their labels; cards return none. Only the user's own upstream of the same kind takes it: another user's ref is `NotFound`, a variant or another kind `RefOperationRefused`.
- **Documents cross as JSON text.** The parsed document reaches the main thread from a format worker and goes on to the writer as a string, so the main thread copies it but never walks or decodes it.
- **The writer only stores.** Every user's writes wait on the one writer (§3.1), so the format worker works out the document's content hash and search text too, from the JSON as stored, and the writer inserts them as given. Only a card with media, or one that keeps its avatar, is parsed in the writer, to name its files; it's also hashed and given its search text there. That's left as is: cards are capped at 8 MiB, and a 7.7 MiB card took 187–244 ms of writer time on the shared host. A test checks that the worker's hash is the writer's own: an edit that changes nothing makes no revision.
- **Known limit:** a large import still holds the writer while its revision and its full-text index entry go in, and a re-import while it merges. A reviewer measured a 19.6 MiB, 10,000-entry lorebook within the caps holding it for seconds on the loaded shared host (about 440 ms of that was the index insert, unloaded). Lorebook entries as rows of their own, or a lower lorebook cap, would bound it.
- **Media** (`media_assets`, `media_refs`, already in `0002`; no migration was needed). A card's avatar and embedded assets are files under `DATA_DIR/media/<2 hex>/<sha256>`, written before the transaction. The transaction records each file once for everyone (`ON CONFLICT (sha256) DO NOTHING`) and gives the user a reference to it. The card document then names them: `avatar_media_id`, and `asset_media_ids` by each asset's URI (built with `Object.fromEntries`, since a URI comes from the file and could be `__proto__`). A file written for an import whose transaction then fails, or before the server stopped, stays on disk with no row naming it. The sweep (`sweepMedia`, at startup and every hour) deletes those once they're an hour old, and leftover temporary files with them. It reads the recorded hashes in a reader worker (`ListMediaHashes`) and works through the files with libuv's file I/O, which doesn't block the main thread. Files aren't deleted when an import fails: another user's import of the same bytes may have recorded them meanwhile.
  - **An import in progress keeps its files.** Imports hold the hashes they've written in an in-process set until they commit or give up, and the sweep skips those. A file an import reuses has its time refreshed, so an old orphan's bytes uploaded again count as new. A candidate is checked again just before it goes (`IsMediaRecorded`, and the set), so a commit that lands after the listing is respected. It's then moved aside, checked once more, and deleted, or moved back if an import took it meanwhile; an import that found it gone writes its own copy. The age alone doesn't decide, so a forward clock jump can't take a file an import is about to record. A file moved aside by a sweep the server stopped in the middle of (`<hash>.<id>.swept`) is put back by the next sweep, the one at startup included, if its hash is recorded or held and its file isn't back already; any other is deleted. `test/importMedia.test.ts` covers each case: a commit landing after the checks and after the move, through a hook at each step, and a crash between the move and the delete.
  - **Links are never followed.** Every entry is read with `lstat`: only plain folders and files under the media folder, itself not a link, are looked at, and every path deleted must resolve inside it. A linked folder, a linked file and a linked media folder each leave the file they point at alone.
- **ST's Character's Note** (`data.extensions.depth_prompt`) becomes the card's first note (DESIGN §4.1a): a note entity whose document (`@rp/shared/docs/note.ts`) has the depth, the role, `owner: {card}` and `on_by_default: true` (ST always sends it), and `scan_lore` when the folder import's settings have ST's `allowWIScan` (`characterNoteScan`). A re-import of the card updates that note the same way, merged with the user's edits to it. "The card's first note" is the oldest live note whose upstream head names the card and isn't a note made from a module or a Character Author's Note (`cardNote` in `db/library.ts`). The card's other notes an export carried (`notesDocJson`) are made, or merged into the one with the same `module_id` (`cardNoteFrom`), in the same transaction.
- **The embedded lorebook** stays in the card document (§8).
- **A new version without the card's files keeps them.** A JSON card holds none, so re-importing one into a card imported as a PNG or CHARX would otherwise drop its avatar and embedded assets as the new version's change, and a PNG export would fall back to the placeholder. The import carries the upstream's current `avatar_media_id` forward when the file has no avatar, and each `asset_media_ids` entry whose URI the new document's `assets` still name but the file doesn't hold. It returns `keptAvatar` and `keptAssets`, and the route adds an info line for each to the report.

`ExportSource` (`db/importReads.ts`) reads what an export takes: the ref's head document, the card's first note, its other notes (a linked one with its shared note's head text; one whose shared note is in the bin is left out), and the stored files the document names. **Only files the user holds a reference to come back.** A document is data, and an edit could put any media id in it; another user's file is left out as if it weren't there (`test/importStorage.test.ts`).

### 11.2 Importers and detection

**The importer registry** (`importer/importers.ts`, PLUGINS §8) is a registry of §6's kind: entries by id, overridden or disabled for the length of a scope. An importer has a format id, a `detect` test and a `parse` from bytes to a document, its report and the files that came with it. The built-ins register in order: `card` (PNG, CHARX, or JSON with a `spec`), `st-world-info` (JSON with `entries`), `st-preset` (any field only ST's preset and template files have, `isStPresetFile` in `formats/preset.ts`, so instruct and context templates reach the preset importer and are reported as unsupported), and `card-v1` (JSON with a `name`, asked last since the key is common). Parsing runs in a format worker (§11.3), so the registry lives there: a plugin's importer will be a module that worker loads.

**Detection reads the bytes, never the name** (`importer/detect.ts`). PNG and zip by their magic bytes; for JSON, the top-level keys, read in one pass without parsing: strings are skipped by their quotes and nesting is a counter, so a hostile file costs a linear scan and can't overflow a stack. The importer that's chosen parses the file properly, under its own caps (§8). A file no importer recognizes, and a file its importer rejects (`FormatError`), become a report with one error and nothing to store.

**Every document an importer returns is checked against its kind's schema** (`validateDoc`, in the format worker): one that doesn't pass is an importer bug, logged, and the file's report says it can't be imported (`file.invalid_document`), with nothing stored. The preset importer once kept a regex placement of `1e20`, a whole number but not a safe one, which its schema refuses.

**What a parse returns** (`parseFile`): the report, the SHA-256 of the file, the document and its label as JSON text, and each file that came with it, with its SHA-256, size and image type from its magic bytes (and a PNG's size from its header).
- **Labels:** a card's name, a lorebook's name, a preset's file name (ST names presets by file), falling back to the file name; control characters removed, at most 200 characters.
- **ST's Character's Note** leaves the card's `extensions` and becomes the note document, so it lives in one place. ST's defaults apply: depth 4, role `system`. An empty one, which ST writes into every card, stays in the extensions untouched.
- **Files are copied to buffers of their own** before they cross to the main thread. An extracted asset can be a view into the upload or into Node's shared buffer pool, and transferring that buffer would detach the pool under the worker.

### 11.3 Format workers

The parsers are synchronous and take up to about a second on a hostile file (§8), so they run in worker threads (`importer/worker.ts`), never on the main thread. `Formats` (`importer/Formats.ts`) is the main thread's side.
- **The pool is the database readers'** (`db/readerPool.ts`, §3.1), with workers of its own. A worker is busy until it answers, so the pool's size is how many files are parsed at once, and the rest queue in order. A worker that dies (a crash, or its heap limit) fails only the file it had, with `FormatWorkerFailed`, which is logged, and is respawned a second later. The files queued behind it wait for the new worker, and fail only if it dies before it's ready too. A file whose send fails (§3.1) also fails alone, even first in line on a new worker. Unlike a database read, the file isn't retried: a file that stopped one worker would stop the next, and its bytes were transferred to the first. `test/formatWorkers.test.ts` checks this with a test worker that dies on request: one stop, the file fails, and the files behind it parse, also behind a file that can't be sent. A CHARX whose assets share one file exports (`test/libraryImport.test.ts`): the export reads the file once, so its buffer is in the request twice.
- **The heap limit,** 512 MB (`resourceLimits.maxOldGenerationSizeMb`), bounds what one file can cost the server: the worst file within the caps took 80 MB, and a worker from source needs about 85 MB for its modules.
- **The upload is transferred, not copied,** when its bytes have a buffer of their own. A view into a larger buffer is copied first: small Node Buffers share a pool, which can't be transferred, and transferring the buffer under other views would empty them.
- **Measured** in the test: an 11 MB lorebook parsed on the main thread blocks it for hundreds of milliseconds. Through a worker, the main thread's longest stall stays under a quarter of that and under 250 ms; measured alone, 130 ms against 1,224 ms under a load average near 90. The bounds are loose because the host is shared.
- **Bundled** like the database workers (§3.6): `formats.mjs`, stamped with them, and refused when missing or stale.

- **A job has 30 s** (`jobDeadline` in the pool, format workers only). A parse or export still running then, such as a parser bug that never returns, gets its thread terminated (`Formats` keeps the newest thread of each worker id for that). The pool then treats it as a crash: only that request fails, and the worker is respawned for the files queued behind it. The worst file within the caps parses in about a second unloaded. The test stops a looping worker at a 2 s deadline, and fails when stopping it does nothing. The database readers have no deadline and behave as before.

### 11.4 Upload and export

**Plain HTTP, not RPC** (`importer/routes.ts`). RPC messages are JSON, so a file would cross as base64: a third larger, one WebSocket message held whole, and decoded on the main thread before the format worker could have it. Over HTTP the body arrives as bytes, is capped as it streams, and goes to the worker as it is.

**`POST /library/import?name=<file name>[&into=<upstream ref>]`**, the file as the body:
- **Who:** the same resolution as an RPC (`Accounts.resolve` on the request's headers: the session cookie, proxy identity or local admin, §4); `401` without one. The cross-site guard of §4 runs first, as for every request.
- **Only `Content-Type: application/octet-stream`** (`415` otherwise): a cross-site form can't send it, and a cross-site `fetch` with it needs a preflight the server never answers. That's on top of the `Origin` check.
- **Size:** at most 64 MiB, the parsers' own file cap (§8). A larger `Content-Length` is `413` before anything is read. Without one, or when it lies, reading stops as the body passes the cap, and the `413` closes the connection.
- **Reading the body** (`importer/body.ts`, from Node's request directly). First the upload waits for its first bytes, then takes a memory permit (two per format worker), then reads the rest. So the server holds at most that many bodies, and an upload that sends nothing holds no permit; the rest wait in their sockets, where TCP holds the sender back.
- **Stalls and trickles are dropped,** each with a `408` sent before the connection closes:
  - a pause of 10 s between chunks, or before the first
  - while it holds a permit, less than 32 KiB/s averaged over each 10 s, the first 10 s being its allowance; so a trickle of a byte every few seconds, which never pauses for 10 s, holds a permit for about 10 s
  - a body still arriving after 15 minutes
  
  The numbers are for a slow phone. A 1 Mbit/s uplink, about 120 KiB/s, sends a 30 MB card in about four minutes: the minimum rate leaves it a margin of almost four times (a 0.5 Mbit/s one still passes), and the deadline gives a full 64 MiB file 15 minutes, about 73 KiB/s. The cost is that an upload that does keep up 32 KiB/s, from a user willing to spend that, holds its permit for up to the deadline.
- **Exports have permits of their own,** taken before they read their files, so stalled uploads never hold them up.
- **One transfer per user at a time:** a user's second upload or export waits for the first, for up to the 15 minutes an upload may take (`503` after that). So no user holds more than one permit, and an export started during one's own upload just waits. `test/transfers.test.ts` covers the `408`, exports and silent uploads going through while two other users stall, an upload going through while two others trickle, and the wait; each test fails against the previous design.
- **The name** is used as the label fallback and the source path, without folders or control characters, at most 255 characters. The format never comes from it (§11.2).
- **`into` is checked first,** before the body is read (`GetLibraryRef`): another user's ref, or none, is `404`, and a variant `409`. After the parse, a file of another kind is `409` before its media are written. So a refused import stores nothing (`test/importMedia.test.ts`).
- **Then:** the format worker parses the file (§11.3), its media are written to disk, and `ImportFile` stores it (§11.1).
- **The answer** is `ImportResult` (`@rp/shared/libraryImport.ts`) as JSON: the report, and what the file became, or `library: null` with the report saying why. `into` naming another user's ref, or none, is `404`; a variant or another kind `409`; a format worker that failed while it had the file (a crash, its deadline, a failed start) `500` with "The import failed on the server; try again." That wording never blames the file: a file the parsers can't read is a report, not a failure.

**The exporter registry** (`importer/exporters.ts`) uses §6's scoped registry inside each format worker, like importers. Each entry owns its format id, entity kind, client label, MIME type, extension, media requirement (`none`, `avatar`, `all`) and writer. Built-ins register in preference order; registering the same id overrides it, `disable` hides it, and closing the scope restores the previous entry. Worker bootstraps install registrations before serving requests; every worker in a pool must install the same registrations, including replacements after a crash. `workerHandlers.ts` supplies the shared export and parser handlers (including per-job trusted ST parser resolution), while `worker.ts` supplies the built-in bootstrap. Direct `exportFile` callers honor an explicitly passed registry or the current `Exporters` service, building scoped defaults only when neither is supplied.

`Formats.listExports` reads data-only metadata through the worker's `ListExportFormats` operation. The authenticated `ListExportFormats` client RPC exposes that catalog; the library uses its kinds, ids, labels and order for export choices, with no separate format map. The HTTP export route validates against the same worker catalog, reads only the media its entry requests and uses its MIME type and extension. It sends this metadata with the export; the executing worker refuses a changed id, kind, MIME type, extension or media requirement before writing, so a scope closing or inconsistent workers cannot silently label bytes with another exporter's headers. New formats need only a worker registration. Documents remain data and downloads remain attachments. `test/exporters.test.ts` installs a synthetic format and an override in a real worker: the client RPC lists them, HTTP uses their bytes and metadata, a disabled format is refused, and closing the plugin scope removes the addition and restores built-in choices and output. It also checks stale metadata and registrations/restoration on two workers and their respawns. `exporterRegistry.test.ts` checks dispatch through a supplied service. The existing import/export tests retain their fixtures and assertions.

**`POST /library/export`**, JSON `{refId, format}` (at most 4 KiB, `application/json` only): the ref's head as a file.
- **Built-in formats** (`importer/exporters.ts`): a card as `card-png`, `card-v2-json`, `card-v3-json` or `card-charx`; a lorebook as `st-world-info`; a preset as `st-preset`. An unknown, disabled or another kind's format is `400`, another user's ref `404`.
- **Written in a format worker** like a parse, since a large lorebook takes as long to write (`importer/exporters.ts`). The document is decoded against its kind's schema first; one that no longer matches (an edit made it invalid) is `422` with a sentence.
- **The card's note goes back** as ST's `depth_prompt`, unless the note is private.
- **Files:** the avatar and assets the user holds (§11.1), read from disk by the paths `mediaPath` makes and no other. Only the files the format holds are read: the avatar for a PNG, every file for a CHARX, none for JSON. Each file is read once, however many times the document names it. Their total is capped at 128 MiB, the CHARX reader's own (§8), before anything is read (`422` past it): a document can name the same file any number of times, and the archive holds a copy for each.
- **A PNG needs a PNG avatar.** A card with none goes into a 1×1 placeholder PNG. A card whose avatar is another image type is refused (`422`), suggesting CHARX, which keeps the image, or JSON, rather than having its avatar silently replaced.
- **The response** has the file's type, `Content-Disposition: attachment` with the label as the name (an ASCII fallback, and the real name percent-encoded), `nosniff` and `no-store`.
## 12. Chats

The chat RPCs, live updates and generation. Contract in `@rp/shared/chatApi.ts` (merged into `AppApi`), handlers in `packages/server/src/api/chats.ts`, the services in `packages/server/src/chat/`.

**Last live fork.** The registered branch-menu contribution reads `ListForks` when opened and disables Delete this fork until the live fork count is known and whenever only one remains. The visible explanation is “The only branch: delete the chat instead”. The server still refuses deleting the last live fork. `e2e/branchNaming.regression.spec.ts` checks the disabled action after deletion and reload/rename at 1440 and 390 pixels; deletion and Undo remain covered on a fork with a survivor.

**Independent navigation shell (2026-10-26).** `/` shows account pins above All / With chats / Never used / Groups / Archive · later, with separate Recent / Created / Name sorts for pins and the grid. Groups shows individual multi-character chat forks (current, named or pinned peers), rather than combining chats by title and cast. The older group RPCs remain for existing group pages. Recent for a card is the maximum of its addition, current revision and its latest speaking turn; Created is its immutable installation-local addition time. Personas are excluded from the home grid.

`GetShellLibrary` (`ShellLibrary` reader operation, `db/shellReads.ts`) reads owner-scoped graph metadata and bounded 160-character excerpts at visible endpoints, folds selected-version state on each visible path, and returns card dates, individual fork rows, message counts, present cast, ever-joined characters and each character's last speaking slot/time/category. Character lists and jumps use that character's speaking activity, so other characters' turns cannot advance it. Just X excludes other present characters written by the model; With others includes them; departed characters appear under Was in. The iterative walk handles deep imported paths without recursive JS calls. Metadata reads start with the live owner chats using `chats_owner_live` (migration `0046_shell_owner_reads`), then indexed slot/event lookups, enforced by CROSS JOIN order. Query-plan regressions use the production statements in `shellQueries.ts`. Metadata work grows with the user's graph size; it is performed in a reader worker. Fork rows carry only the chat's id and title, and their cast as entity ids; `people` describes each cast card once, and the client's `shellAtom` puts the cards back on each row. At 1,000 cards / 5,000 chats / 50,000 slots this halves the response (3.8 MB to 1.9 MB without excerpts). Fork rows carry departed participant ids independently of the model cast; silent departures use those ids, so a present character played by the user keeps its present classification.

The left panel has independent in-memory scope (All chats › display name · version › chat) and a character-thumbnail switch. Browsing crumbs or switching the left view leaves the chat and right panel unchanged. Character tiles select all three parts and open the character's last-speaking line at its speaking slot; an unused character opens an unsaved greeting with its definitions. Cast names offer the same character navigation. Header cast chips select that character's right-panel tab in the current chat. Ordinary chat rows change the middle, retaining the right tab when applicable and falling back to This chat for an unrelated character tab (implementation proposal). Groups tiles also scope the left panel to the selected chat's forks, clearing any previous character scope and grid mode. Ordinary rows resolve both URL and account-remembered right tabs before checking the destination cast, including navigation from home. Explicit card-detail pages retain search over every associated fork and show 50 rows at a time, with Load more; row metadata includes the original start date, cast, message count and excerpt.

Migration `0045_shell` adds `chats.current_leaf_slot_id`, the legacy `chat_pins` table and `shell_preferences`. Since forks (§3.8), `chats.current_fork_id` determines the shell's current row; its tip is mirrored by `active_leaf_slot_id`. Nothing writes `current_leaf_slot_id` any more. Opening a fork is the client's view alone. Chatting in a fork (a send, swipe, continue, or import advancing its leaf) makes it current.

**Stable shell fork identities (U01, 2026-10-28).** The shell enumerates every live owned `forks` record, including interior checkpoints, empty forks and several forks at the same tip. Rows and React keys use the actual fork id. The slot query materializes the live fork views once and enumerates their chain segments with indexed depth ranges. An indexed check for overrides per view lets untouched chats read base selections and deletion flags directly; chats with overrides resolve selections, replacements and deletion in each fork's bounded lineage. Event visibility uses the same lineage helpers. State folds positional events on that path and registered branch-owned state last. Deleted messages contribute neither excerpts, counts nor speaking activity; a surviving endpoint supplies the bounded excerpt. Names, creation dates, pins and current status come from the fork record. Character activity is rebuilt from that fork's selected, surviving messages, and keeps the fork's own cast and departure classifications. Work scales with the visible fork paths and applicable event metadata, in reader workers.

`SetChatPin` accepts the row's actual `forkId` for both pinning and unpinning and writes `forks.pinned_at`, the same storage as `PinFork`; it validates the owned chat, live fork and any supplied leaf's membership. Legacy leaf-only callers resolve the fork through that leaf using the ordinary `forkAt` rule. No new pins are written to `chat_pins`. Home pins, Groups tiles, character clicks and left-list rows navigate with an explicit `fork` URL parameter, alongside any message/leaf location; same-tip peers therefore retain their own state and drafts when opened. The character feature and shell remain registered through `features/index.ts`. `SetHomePinsCollapsed` keeps its account-owned writer transaction. Tests: `shellForkIdentity.test.ts` covers checkpoints, peers, empty paths, selection-specific excerpts, cast/departures, deletion, pin isolation and wrong-chat pin rejection; existing shell navigation, edge and scale tests remain. It also covers user-played turns across chained card swaps: activity checks the played identities before and through the swaps, since existing messages keep their original speakers, and a later change to model-written turns resumes activity only at that position. `e2e/shellForkIdentity.spec.ts` covers exact fork URLs through pins, Groups and character navigation at desktop and phone sizes in both themes.

The right panel retains Cast, This chat, Lorebooks, Notes, Summaries and the full-image Portrait tab, plus character-card tabs (`panel=card:<entity id>`) with definition, Edit card and the existing variant refs. No origin or lines-and-forks section is added. On phones the panels are independent sheets; the left sheet keeps its breadcrumbs above the scrolling rows. Character selection closes the left sheet even when the middle chat id stays the same.

**RPCs.** Every one acts as the caller, and another user's ids are `NotFound` (each has a cross-user probe in the isolation matrix, §4).
- **Chats:** `ListChats` (most recent first, by last message, else creation; paged by `(time, id)`; with `entityId`, only the chats that card joined, those with a message first, paged by `(has a message, time, id)` with the cursor's `empty`, and `NotFound` unless the card is the user's; with `search`, only the chats whose title, or a card in which by label or in-chat name, has the text, matched with `LIKE` with its wildcards escaped, through the cards that match and `state_events_participants`), `CreateChat` (a title only, for now), `GetChat`, `RenameChat`, `DeleteChat`. `RenameChat` is an owner-scoped `RenameChats` writer transaction: whitespace is normalized, a blank name restores the cast-based title, and an imported ST timestamp is retained. It marks `title_source = user` so re-import does not replace the choice, advances the chat row version, and publishes `Chat` to open tabs. The header title opens the rename dialog; bin entries and deletion toasts read the new title.
- **Groups:** `ListChatGroups` (`ChatGroups` in the readers, `db/chatSetupReads.ts`, `groupsOf`). There's no group entity: a group (`ChatGroup` in `@rp/shared/chatSetupApi.ts`) is the chats with the same readable name (`chatName` in `@rp/shared/chatName.ts`, which the client's `chatTitle.ts` uses too: an ST group chat's "<group>: <date>" title gives the group's name, a chat made here its title, the cast's names) and the same set of characters the user doesn't play (the cast folded at each chat's active leaf: joins and leaves on that path, less the latest applicable `played_by_me.set`; the persona tag alone does not say who the user plays), two or more; chats in the bin don't count. Each has its members (in the latest chat's join order, with in-chat name, label and avatar), its count, its last message and its latest chat (the one with the latest message, else the newest), the groups by that. One owner-scoped reader operation loads each active path's cast events (indexed by chat), card metadata and purged references, then uses the shared `foldState` and `withoutPurged` rules before grouping. Superseded events, deleted slots and unselected-version events are excluded as in `StateEventsAt`; message text is never loaded. `test/chatGroups.test.ts` covers replacements, leaves, sibling branches, the realistic ST folder's three groups and a 2-second budget at 5,000 chats with ten-message active paths. `ListGroupChats` is the group a chat is in with its chats, latest message first (`Chat` rows; NotFound when it's in none). `RenameGroup` reads that group then renames its chats in one writer transaction, publishing `Chat` for each. Chats renamed independently between the read and write keep their new titles. The grouping key stays the readable name plus current cast: renaming a single chat separates it into its own group tile (or joins an existing matching tile); the header dialog explains the effect and offers to rename the whole group. The group page has a Rename group action. These grouping semantics are a proposal, avoiding a separate group identity and migration.
- **Characters:** `ListCardUsage` (`CardUsage` in the readers, `db/chatSetupReads.ts`): every live card that isn't a persona, from the ref `loadCards` picks, with its label, subtitle, in-chat name, avatar, head revision, and the chats it joined on any branch (group chats included, chats in the bin not): their count, the last message among them and the latest of them: the chat with the last message, a chat without messages only when none has any (an empty chat's creation never outranks a message). One query: per card, a seek on `state_events_participants` (migration `0034_chat_participants`, a partial index on each `participant.join`'s card and chat), written `= +cards.entity_id` so the column's affinity doesn't keep SQLite off the indexed expression. 1,000 cards and 5,000 chats read in about 40 ms (`test/cardUsage.test.ts`); without the index it scanned every user's state events. The participant-events subquery uses `CROSS JOIN` to seek the card’s indexed joins/swaps before looking up their chats; allowing SQLite to start with live chats caused one probe per card per chat. The 1,000-card/5,000-chat regression bounds both cold and warmed reads without raising its timing limits.
- **Reading:** `ReadPath`, below, and `ListVersions` for the version picker.
- **Writing:**
  - `SendMessage` and `Generate` add a message under a fork's tip (`forkId`; the current fork if none is named), which makes that fork current: the user's text, or a model reply that streams. Without `branch` the parent must be the fork's tip (`LeafMoved` otherwise); with it, any slot, which starts a fork there (DESIGN §3.2; `AppendMessage`, §3.8). `SendMessage` takes the speaker (Send as X); `Generate` the speaker (Generate X) and a one-turn `direction`, stored on that reply (DESIGN §2.2). Onto a message whose selected version is still generating, both are queued instead (the send queue, below), and return `Queued` rather than `Posted`. Both take an optional `composer`, the draft the text was taken from (its id and session): it's committed in the send's transaction, posted or queued, only if it still holds exactly that text (§3.10), and both return that draft as the send left it, with `recovered`.
  - `SwitchBranch` finds the fork through another of the alternates at a message, by index, for the client to open (below). `CreateFork` is "Branch from here"; `NameFork`, `PinFork` and `ListForks` are §3.8's operations.
  - `Regenerate` accepts any message, including a human-written or impersonated turn. It is a swipe: a new model version of a slot, selected and streamed, with the speaker and the `direction` of the version it replaces (DESIGN §2.2, guidance on swipes), unless a `direction` is given with it (the guidance editor's box, §12), an empty one meaning none; later turns never see a direction. A speakerless old version uses another version's speaker or the slot's first speaker; on the user side it falls back to the first card played at that position. An optional model choice is ownership-checked and stored on the new version in the same transaction; normal generation already records its model during assembly. `Version.model` resolves the stored model id to “connection · model” for headers and picker previews, or null for older/imported versions without a recorded model. `EditVersion` saves an edit as a new version "edited from" its source, and `WriteVersion` is "write your own". Both record the message's speaker on the new version, as a swipe does: an edit its source's, a written version the shown version's, so a message keeps its speaker whichever of its versions is live. All three take the fork they're done in (`forkId`, the current one if none is named), select the new version there only and make it current, and check the slot's row version as that fork shows it (`StaleWrite`), through the writer's `AddSelectedVersion`. A path entry carries the message's `side` as the context reads it when its speaker isn't one of the cast: its first version's (a human's is the user's, unless a greeting), deleted or not, so which versions are live never changes it. It also carries that first version's speaker (`firstSpeakerEntityId`), whom the message shows as when no live version names one.
