The all-match mode adds secondary scores only when every secondary slot matches. Selection uses score, override, descending order and stable input ties. Each entire group string is treated as one name. Admission and returned entries also use descending order with stable input ties.

The token counter receives content with appended newlines. Counts are cached for each distinct input, including invariant content from earlier passes. Combined content is still counted together, so tokenization need not be additive. An entry that reaches the allowance exactly is rejected. Global exhaustion stops ordinary admissions. A positive book budget applies only to that book; its rejected content does not spend another book's allowance. An `ignore_budget` entry bypasses both checks but its content remains charged. All content stays charged across recursion, including `prevent_recursion` content, avoiding ST's undercount.

Regex keys use the scripts' linear-time engine and its ASCII-case and Unicode limitations. Both the original and rewritten pattern have a 2,000-character cap. Regex execution charges an estimated expanded pattern size times input length. One evaluation may spend 250,000 units, and an activation may spend 500,000. Indexed word matches and exact ASCII substring matches spend scan work instead. Invalid syntax falls back to literal matching and produces an issue. Oversized slash patterns are refused before syntax validation. Refused constructs produce a specific explanation and a suggested edit. An untestable secondary condition excludes the entry, including negative conditions.

Compilation has separate memory limits, checked before invoking V8. At most 4,096 patterns are cached. Their keys and estimated rewritten source together may use 131,072 characters. Compiled programs may use 262,144 estimated expansion units in total. The estimate multiplies source size by every counted repetition's upper bound, including nested repetitions. It deliberately overcounts disjoint repetitions. Escaped braces and braces inside character classes are literal. Case rewriting is included in the estimate. This prevents short sources with large repetitions from retaining unexpectedly large programs. Ordinary literal keys are not kept in the pattern cache.

These memory limits were measured on Node 24.15 with synthetic fixtures in October 2026. The review's 1,000-pattern repeated-literal case previously added 234 MB of heap. It now stops after eight compilations and added 2.24 MB in the measured run. Nine stress variants exercised literal patterns, repeated groups, nested counts, the cache-count ceiling, case rewriting, captures and alternatives. The combined case reached 4,096 cached patterns and 261,440 expansion units. Its 7.5 MB heap increase was the largest observed. Tests assert the deterministic count, character and expansion ceilings, rather than a heap reading affected by garbage collection.

Retained scan data has its own bounds. An activation allows 4,096 buffers, 8 million characters of prepared buffer and index text, 100,000 indexed words and 20,000 cached regex results. Lowercase storage is charged conservatively before allocation. Each activation also allows 100,000 key evaluations, 128 million characters of text preparation and scanning, and 8 million characters sent to the token counter. Indexed lookups charge the key spelling without charging another traversal of the buffer. Substring searches and uncached general regexes still charge their traversed input. Candidate content and literal boundary checks also spend scan work. Scanning and general regex execution share an additional allowance: scanned characters plus 256 times regex work may not exceed 128 million. The charge is checked before the work runs. This prevents an activation from spending both full allowances independently.

The work allowances leave room for ordinary books. The measured 500-entry book, with three keys per entry and ten 2,000-character messages, activates its five expected entries. It charges 74,653 scanned characters without recursion and 131,778 with recursion. Tests also cover 300–1,000 entries, depths 2–10 and messages of 2,000–4,000 characters. The largest indexed case charges 256,778 scanned characters and 378,656 retained characters. A 500-entry substring case charges 123 million scanned characters. A mixed book with exact ASCII substring regexes on 50 of its 500 entries charges 11.6 million scanned characters and no general regex work. Both use ten 4,000-character messages and recursion. These cases run without limit issues.

The CPU allowances target roughly one second on the measured host. The earlier independent allowances permitted 28.2 seconds of work on a combined synthetic input. On Node 24.15.0 and an EPYC 7402, the new combined limit stopped that input in 449–489 ms over 20 worker-thread runs. The slowest run had host load averages of 10.24 / 13.62 / 18.71 over 1 / 5 / 15 minutes. A scaled nested-regex and scan input took 123–188 ms. Both produced identical results over 20 runs, reported a work-limit issue and remained replayable. A scan-only input spending 127.97 million characters took 926 ms at load 10.33 / 13.49 / 18.58. The 500-entry word, mixed, substring and boundary-regex books took 4.8–26.1 ms and admitted their five expected entries without issues. Each typical variant also replayed identically over 20 runs. These measurements exclude fixture construction and use a synthetic token counter. They are observed times under load, not a guarantee for arbitrary host scheduling or caller-supplied functions.

At most 1,000 diagnostic occurrences are collected. Repeated issues collapse to a count and the first three examples, with key excerpts capped at 160 characters. Callers may lower the limits. Reaching an aggregate limit stops work with one limit issue and the admissions already completed. Candidates still awaiting admission are left out. Results include work counters and explain unfinished entries.

A five-second VM deadline remains as an emergency guard around activation. It can interrupt a regex engine call. If it fires, the result explicitly says it is not replayable. Evaluation failures become issues and preserve completed admissions. Ordinary activation depends only on its inputs and the injected functions. Regex scripts keep their separate cumulative 200 ms deadline and existing behavior.

Sticky/cooldown state, character filters, weighted or overlapping groups, expanding history, source tiers, decorators, external/vector activation, World Info postprocessing remain deferred. Unsupported retained entry controls produce issues. Tail-block persistence and exact tokenizer/global settings resolution come later.

**Generation** (`chat/ContextGenerator.ts`; DATA-MODEL §1, principle 7: every generation is a rollout). The chat's generator:
- assembles the context in a reader worker. A context that can't be built fails the version with the reason (`no_model`, `budget`, `invalid_document`); legacy per-chat model bindings are ignored, including bindings whose targets are gone.
- opens the rollout (`StartRollout`, one write): the row (`running`, role `narrator`, the model), its frozen snapshot in `rollout_payloads`, the version's rollout, connection and model, the variables the preset's and cards' macros wrote as `var.set` events of the version (origin `rollout`), and the values template messages drew, kept on versions that had none (`frozen_macros`, `{}` when a template drew nothing but wrote variables), with the variables each wrote as `var.set` events of its own version, recorded unless that version has some already, so a `{{time}}` in a greeting stays what it was. The version must be the user's and still generating, and the model one of the user's live connections'.
- maps the request to the provider (`wire.ts`). Chat Completions gets the assembled messages exactly, names included, in `payload` (§6), since pi-ai's own conversion drops names and moves later system messages to the top. Anthropic and the other APIs get a context pi-ai converts, as ST converts for Claude: the leading system messages as the system prompt (always, except for Anthropic and Google without `use_sysprompt`), later ones as the user's with names folded into the text, one message each so a breakpoint covers nothing after it, the prefill last, and the samplers pi-ai doesn't set in `payload` (Google's in `config`, as ST sends `topP` and `topK`). Breakpoints go only to models whose breakpoints are `explicit`.
- streams through the provider layer (§6), which writes a ledger row per attempt, attributed to the chat, rollout and version. Text deltas become the version's text. Ordinary replies omit the preset prefill from saved text, as ST does; Continue starts with the source version's full text and appends the returned suffix; a provider error fails the version with its reason (`bad_request`, `context_overflow`, …).
- closes the rollout (`FinishRollout`) on every exit, as a resource of the stream's scope, so a stop before the first token closes it too: `completed`, `failed` with the message, or `stopped` (a user's stop or the server's), with the usage its ledger rows add up to, on the rollout and the version's `usage_json`, in one transaction. At startup, rollouts left `running` become `interrupted`.

The production server, and the development server without `FAKE_GENERATOR_TPS`, generate this way. Tested against local mocks that record what they're sent (`test/generation.test.ts`): an OpenAI-compatible one, whose body must hold exactly the assembled messages and samplers (pi-ai adds the model, streaming and usage options around them); a failing one; an Anthropic-shaped one with a made-up key, checked for the system prompt, the prefill, the samplers and a breakpoint; Google's and OpenAI's o-series samplers; and 40 generations stopped 0 to 7 ms after they start, none of which may leave its rollout running (`test/generationEdges.test.ts`).

**Continue** (`ContinueVersion`, `features/chat/ContinueAction.tsx`; DESIGN §7.5). The latest message's selected version offers Continue beside Regenerate when its text is finished and nonempty, whoever wrote it: an edit of a reply (a `human`-written version, `origin: edited`) and a version the user wrote continue as a generated one does, and a persona's turn continues as an impersonation (parity finding 1). The client action and `ContinueVersion` apply the same rule (`NotContinuable` only for a version still being written or without text); the model's prefill capability is checked as before. It adds a selected generated version in the same slot with `source_version_id`, the original text and speaker, and only the `direction` given with it (the composer's guidance), not the source's. It also copies the source reasoning, appending any new reasoning a paragraph apart [P]. The source remains unchanged. Streaming offsets begin after that starting text; only the wire prefill trims trailing whitespace, which Anthropic refuses. The assembler recognizes the source as a continuation, sets generation type `continue` for preset triggers, excludes the target from ordinary history and budgets its text as the trailing assistant prefill. The frozen inspector capture identifies it as “Message N as it was, continued from its end.” The source text replaces the preset's ordinary prefill [P]. Tests: `test/continueReasoning.test.ts` (an edited reply continued into a third, generated version, both originals kept; a blank edit refused), `e2e/continueReasoning.spec.ts` (Edit → Save → Continue at 1440 and 390 px).

Resolved `prefill: assistant` models use a trailing assistant message: Anthropic and native-prefill OpenAI-compatible Chat Completions. DeepSeek additionally marks only the final prefill message `prefix: true` and requires the configured Base URL to end in `/beta`; an incompatible endpoint produces a model-specific explanation at the action and fails before dispatch. The endpoint is never changed automatically. Capability and adapter availability are retained independently of context budgeting, so a budget failure cannot enable an unsupported model. An unresolved capability keeps the action unavailable.

`prefill: tool_schema` supports OpenAI Responses and Chat Completions. Like reference-harness's anchored strict-schema `analysis` argument, it forces a single function call with an anchored string pattern; the delta is a dedicated `continue_reply` tool with a `text` argument containing the whole reply, including the exact original prefix [P]. The schema includes that prefix and regex-escapes it. It replaces the trailing assistant prefill, disables parallel calls, streams parsed argument text after stripping the prefix, and validates the provider’s original tool-argument JSON, the final call and unchanged streamed text before completing. Raw Chat Completions argument deltas and authoritative Responses completion arguments are retained separately from pi-ai’s permissively repaired preview objects. When provider usage is absent, the forced reply tool’s serialized arguments contribute estimated output counts, including on stop or failure; ordinary tools retain their existing usage semantics. Preambles are ignored; malformed or prefix-changing replies fail with `bad_prefill`. The assembler reserves the serialized tool/schema input and the repeated prefix’s JSON output, in addition to the configured continuation length. The wire maximum includes that prefix allowance, and a known output ceiling that cannot fit it fails with an explicit budget explanation before dispatch. No tool is executed and no agent loop is needed. Other APIs explicitly explain that their schema adapter is unavailable. Ordinary preset schema prefills use the same mapping. The source version retains its exact text, including trailing whitespace; native wire prefills trim trailing whitespace for Anthropic, while schema prefixes preserve it.

The model editor distinguishes absent provider information, explicit no support, native assistant support and schema support. Users can declare either supported prefill method. Frozen inspection captures only the dedicated forced schema tool's parameters and prefix as a message with continuation provenance, or DeepSeek's prefix flag; these fields are scrubbed with the rest of the display. Arbitrary tool definitions remain excluded. The picker refreshes when the selected version's status or saved row version changes; opening a list with a generating record also refreshes it. Completion status and preview stay current after switching to the original version. Tests in `reviewContinue.test.ts` cover budget-independent capability, DeepSeek protocol/endpoint validation and Responses schema suffix streaming; browser tests exercise both protocol adapters and unavailable capability at both widths.

**Reasoning** (`features/chat/Reasoning.tsx`; DESIGN §7.4). Provider `thinking` deltas are saved in the existing `versions.reasoning` column, also used by ST imports; no migration is needed. Stop, failure, orderly shutdown and periodic saves retain it. `ReadPath`, `GetVersion` and `ListVersions` expose it; `Hello` restores live reasoning after reconnect or reload. A separate atom per version batches reasoning independently of reply text. The new component renders a Markdown block above the reply, expanded while generating by default, folded when done and expandable in one click. Toggling during a run saves the user's streaming choice through `GetDisplayPrefs`/`SetDisplayPrefs` to `user_prefs`, key `display`; later runs and other devices read it. A completed block's local fold choice does not alter the streaming preference. Switching versions resets the local choice. The header beside the toggle renders the `message.reasoning-actions` slot (`reasoningActions` in `presentation.tsx`, props: the version, the raw text, streaming), whose defaults are registered in `reasoningActions.tsx` (parity finding 7): **Copy reasoning** (`reasoning.copy`) copies the raw text, stored or streamed so far, never the rendered Markdown (`copy.ts`, shared with Copy text, toasts the outcome); **Collapse all** (`reasoning.collapse-all`) bumps a module counter every mounted block watches, closing each as a local choice for that block, so the streaming preference in `user_prefs` is untouched. A plugin can replace, disable or add to either by id. Tests: client `test/reasoningActions.test.ts` (order, replacement, removal, the counter); `e2e/continueReasoning.spec.ts` (two replies opened, Copy's exact text, Collapse all closing both, the preference unchanged, at 1440 and 390 px).

Reasoning is display-only in these plain chat generations: the assembler and `wire.ts` replay text blocks only, and never add stored reasoning to history. The provider layer already preserves signatures/encrypted reasoning in its own typed transcript conversion, but this unit has no tool transcript or agent loop requiring those blocks. Signed/replayed agent reasoning remains separate future work. `FAKE_GENERATOR_REASONING=<tokens>` adds a synthetic reasoning phase ahead of the fake generator's text at `FAKE_GENERATOR_TPS`; production does not wire it. Tests: `continueReasoning.test.ts` checks Chat Completions and Anthropic prefill, exact old-text preservation, independent reasoning, inspector provenance and unsupported dispatch; `session.test.ts` checks live delta/reconnect handling; the isolation matrix covers the new RPCs; `e2e/continueReasoning.spec.ts` checks stop/continue, the old picker entry, persisted reasoning, sticky preferences and unsupported explanations at 1440 and 390 px.

**Current account settings** (`GetGenerationDefaults`, `SetGenerationDefaults`, `ChangeGenerationDefaults`; `db/generationSettings.ts`): `generation.model` holds the connection/model pair and `generation.preset` the prompt ref. Each is independent. On changing to another connection, its optional `presetOverrideRefId` sets the current prompt preset; another model on that same connection does not reapply the lock, and later prompt switches remain possible. The connection's settings show the lock. Selection checks, the previous-connection read, and lock adoption run in the same writer transaction. Ownership checks return `NotFound`; deleted choices read as unset.

**Model settings** (`@rp/shared/modelSettings.ts`, `ModelOverrides.settings`). Samplers, context/reply limits, history target, thinking effort, verbosity, stop strings, logit bias, streaming display, cache retention, provider routing and wire names/squashing/system formatting belong to the connection's model. Existing model overrides retain capabilities, image limits, prefill support, cache policy, compat and sampling. Assembly uses a transient ST compatibility view containing those model settings plus the prompt document; this view is never stored as a prompt preset. The model's context size and response length (`samplers.openai_max_context`, `openai_max_tokens`) are settings, apart from its reported limits (`context`, `maxOutput`), which assembly still reads as the model's window and output ceiling. Without its own, the context size is the reported window (8192 when unknown) and the response length is at most 4096, within the reported output limit and half the context size in use (the model's own when set, within the window) (`withModelSettings`), so a model reporting an output limit as large as its window, or one given a small context size, still leaves room for history. A schema continuation with that default response length shortens it to the output limit less the source prefix; only a response length set on the model can fail the continuation's output-limit check. Sampler defaults are temperature/top-p 1, penalties 0 (repetition 1), no seed, other optional samplers off. The preset's old budget never supplies these values. Streaming off buffers the generated chunks until completion while preserving the controlled SDK stream for transport and cancellation.

Model settings live on models, not in prompt presets: no preset sampler is copied onto a model, and no account model or preset is chosen from historical chat events. New library writes strip model fields from prompt documents. Assembler version 23 pins the new semantics; older saved request snapshots remain inspectable through the existing version-mismatch path.

**Composer and settings panels.** Each chat composer shows account model and prompt-preset selectors, each opening its list with one click. Model lists leave hidden models out except the current one, which stays named and marked hidden (`modelChoices.ts`), here and in Settings → Chats. Adjacent settings buttons open the Model right-panel tab (`GenerationControls.tsx`) and the Prompt preset tab (`PresetTab.tsx`, below under **Preset overlays**); the model editor is reused on the connection page, and the prompt editor retains only prompt content. This chat no longer offers per-chat model/preset controls or their provenance chain. Settings → Chats also edits the current account selections. The built-in preset is displayed as “Default (built-in)” (`BUILTIN_PRESET_LABEL`), apart from ST's own Default preset, which a folder import brings in under its library label. The model editor's number fields keep text as typed until it parses (“0.” on the way to “0.7”); an empty field means the default, and Save refuses text that isn't a number.

**What a version shows of its context.** `StartRollout` keeps the snapshot's issues on the rollout (`context_issues_json`), so a path window reads them with each version (a lookup by the version's `rollout_id`, as `versionExtras` in `db/rows.ts` adds it to every version read) without reading a snapshot. Migration 0016 backfills valid issue arrays using the kinds from `ContextIssue`, including lorebook warnings; malformed arrays remain null. Snapshot presence is checked independently of issues, including legacy rollouts without a snapshot. `Version.context` is null when nothing recorded a context (typed and imported messages, greetings, a generation that failed before its rollout opened), and otherwise holds the issues.

**The request inspector** (DESIGN §7.4; `GetRunContext`, `@rp/shared/inspectorApi.ts`, `api/inspector.ts`; the reader op `RunContext`, `db/inspectorReads.ts`). From a generated version, the context it was generated from, as it was sent: its rollout's stored snapshot, not a rebuild, so it's exact whatever changed since, a card or preset deleted for good included [P]. Only the chat's owner reads it, and a version, message or chat in the bin is `NotFound`, like every read (the isolation matrix).
- **The sent provider mapping** (`wire.ts`) is captured in the SDK's `onPayload` hook, after SDK conversion and registered transforms. Before HTTP, `RecordSentContext` writes only text messages, numeric samplers, source indices, server-only content digests, sanitized display metadata and the capability identity actually used to `rollout_payloads.sent_context_json`, in one owner-checked transaction. Assembly reads the complete display metadata in the same reader transaction: model and preset labels, source labels and identities, issues and activation decisions. Capture scrubs every display string (including lore keywords) on the main thread with the complete effective request inventory (§6), including catalog and authentication components resolved before capture, and excludes headers, keys, proxy settings and arbitrary payload fields. SDK environment defaults are disabled; Google uses the same controlled transport. Ordinary provider errors published in Version events use this inventory too. Retries replace this projection with the latest attempt's mapping. Responses' `developer` and Google's `model` roles are preserved. Combined system blocks retain all their sources. Before transforms, wire messages receive explicit provenance on JSON-invisible symbols, retained through object spreads and moves. Inserted or replaced messages without that provenance have no assigned sources; source labels never fall back to row position [P]. Missing final captures, including legacy captures without sanitized display metadata, return `capture: unavailable` with empty messages, samplers and issues and an explicit “Not sent / unavailable” notice. The inspector never falls back to raw assembly text or reconstructs labels from mutable refs. Database workers receive no decrypted credential inventory. Display metadata annotates the captured wire messages without storing their text twice.
- **Each message** with its role, name, text, estimated tokens (the assembler's estimate: four characters a token, three a message, plus its images), each image's estimate with its basis and detail level (`ContextMessage.images`: tokens or unknown, what the budget counted, whether it was sent; captured wire messages are text, so the images come from the snapshot's request messages through each wire message's `inputs`) and its sources, named for the user from the pieces that went into it: `Preset prompt “Main Prompt”` (the name in the preset revision the context used), `Preset: new chat prompt` for the preset's other text, `Card “Mira”: description` (the name in the card revision it read), `Note “…”`, `Message 12` (by depth, linking to the message), `One-turn direction`, and the assembler's own blocks. A message squashed from several pieces lists each. A history message's source carries its `version` (its place among the slot's versions when sent, from 1), shown as `Message 12, version 2`; a lorebook entry is `Lorebook “Harbor”: entry 3 “The lighthouse”` with the entry's title (ST's comment) in the book revision read; a card's, lorebook's or preset's source carries its library `ref` to link to; notes take their title.
- **Parts** ("debug symbols", PHASE1-DECISIONS §1). Each captured message records where each source's text sits in it: `parts`, character offsets into the sent (scrubbed) text, in order and never overlapping, each naming one of the message's sources. The reader hands the main thread each request message's pieces as a tree of text by source id with the inspection inputs, never stored: each outermost piece, and inside a labelled block the pieces it holds (a card's wrapped field holding its join prefix, field and suffix; the characters' definitions holding each card's; a lorebook block its entries; a prompt with `{{summary}}` its summaries). A preset prompt that read `{{description}}`, `{{personality}}` or `{{scenario}}` holds the joined fields' recorded `cards:macro:*` piece when its text contains it, so a format prompt shows which card each line came from. Final capture (`inspectWire`, `locate`) scrubs each text as the content is scrubbed and finds it in the sent message, searching from the end of the previous sibling, else at its first free occurrence, and a block's pieces only inside the block. Leaves become `parts`; blocks become `groups` (same offsets, nested ones inside their outer ones). A card join prefix or suffix inside a block is marked `frame`. Assembler-side rewrites are per piece and recorded as the piece's text (regex scripts' output for a history message, `collapse_newlines`), so their spans are found as any other. A piece whose text isn't in the sent message verbatim (a registered provider transform rewrote the whole message after assembly, which no per-piece span survives) takes the text between the siblings found around it, trimmed of edge whitespace, marked `rewritten`; a run of such siblings shares that text, the first as `source` and the others (including nested sources in inseparable blocks) as `also`, and a lone rewritten block's pieces are searched inside it. When located neighbours moved out of order, the remaining unclaimed text is boxed with all unresolved sources instead of being attributed to the assembler. These shared fallback boxes may include joins: they identify the candidate sources without claiming an exact division after a whole-message rewrite. Wrappers and blank pieces never take text, and an empty gap (a rewrite removed the text) produces no box: then the source is listed under "Also from" as before. Text between parts is the assembler's joins, wrappers and folded names. Only offsets are stored, never the text twice.
- **Not sent.** The capture's display lists what this turn left out (`notSent`): notes whose condition didn't hold (`due: false`), lorebook entries skipped, and the snapshot's `skippedPrompts`, each a source like any other, with its activation decision. A prompt left out by its ST generation-type triggers (§9) is listed like one left out by its condition, reading “(sent only for normal generations)”; a prompt excluded by both is listed once, by its triggers. Notes have no hidden kinds, so none is special-cased here.
- **The fix batch's request shapes.** Each summary of the layer is its own system message, shown as its own turn under a titled group (“Summary of messages 1–2”, an assembler piece around the summary's text, so the `<summary …>` framing is the group's wrapper); `{{summary}}` shows only the imported summary note's text, as a note part. A persona's description is its own wrapped `<Name>…</Name>` block under “Tobin's persona”, inside the characters' definitions. The end-of-request pieces are labelled by their three editor labels; the empty-text fallback is labelled “End of request fallback: who writes next”.
- **Older captures** (before parts, versions, refs and `notSent` were recorded) open the same way: their verbatim messages with the sources they recorded, one box per message, and a "not sent" list collected from the skipped note and lore sources those messages carry. Nothing is reconstructed from current references.
- **The cache breakpoints** between the messages, with why each is there (the end of the preset's part, or the end of the history the next request repeats), and whether they were sent: only to models whose breakpoints are `explicit` (§6).
- **The model, the preset** (its label and scope), the generation type and mode, the rollout's status, the effective samplers after provider mapping, and **the usage the provider reported**, per attempt (an inspector-specific DTO: status, uncached input, cache read, cache write, output, usage source and cost). The SQL uses an explicit column projection. On phones usage appears as a two-column list of labelled values per attempt, keeping numbers and “unknown” on one line without horizontal page scrolling; desktop retains the table. `e2e/inspector.spec.ts` checks all six labels and unbroken values at 390×844.
- **The issues** in the inspector come from the sanitized final display capture. Version warnings still use the validated rollout column; malformed legacy arrays display as none.
- **Source explanations and identity.** Lore sources retain status, step and constant/keyword reasons, with secondary-key logic. Notes retain explicit versus default activation, their condition in words, whether it was due, and in snapshots from before intervals were dropped the interval and its user-message count. The client renders these historical decisions beside the source. Deduplication and React keys use stable hashes of book/ref + revision + UID for lore, entity + revision for notes, and the other source references for other kinds. Equal labels remain distinct.
- **The client** (`features/chat/ContextPage.tsx`, `/chats/$chatId/context/$versionId`, a lazy route). Every version with a recorded context, a swipe included, has a "Request it was generated from" button among its message actions (`Message.tsx`), beside the same item in its "More actions" menu. The page shows the request summary (model, preset, generation type, breakpoints, samplers), the issues, then one block per sent message in request order, headed by its number, role, name and tokens; inside, each group is a titled box holding its parts and inner groups, and each part is a titled box: the title says what the text is and where it came from (linking to the chat message or the library item), with the activation decision under it, and the text follows exactly as sent. A group's text outside its parts is its framing, shown verbatim without a box: quiet when an assembler block's (with `frame` wrappers labelled by their preset source), as plain prompt text when a preset prompt's own; line breaks at the framing's edges are left to the layout. Prompt text is set in the monospace face on the canvas colour; the inspector's own titles, labels and links are in the interface face on the raised surface, so they can't be mistaken for prompt text. A rewritten part or block is boxed like any other, its title "<source> (rewritten)", so a transform's output is never attributed to the assembler. Text outside every box shows as quiet framing labelled "Added by the assembler" (whitespace-only joins are left to the spacing); "Show as one text" shows a message's whole content in one box. Then "Not sent this turn", then usage: per attempt the counts and cost, and its times from the ledger (`InspectorUsage.startedAt`/`firstTokenAt`/`endedAt`): Time (start to end, "running" or "unknown"), First token (start to the first text or reasoning delta) and Tokens/s (output over first token to end), each "unknown" when a figure is missing. A context of at most 60 messages and 120,000 characters opens with every turn expanded; a larger one opens collapsed to previews of the first 200 characters; "Expand every turn" / "Collapse every turn" switch all. Rows are TanStack Virtual rows measured as they expand, following the chat list's approach. Visible rows, a small overscan and the focused row mount. List items expose their full-list position and size. Expansion state lives above the virtual rows, keyed by version and message index. Browser coverage scrolls and opens a later row in a 100k-token context and the last row of a dense 10,000-message context, retains keyboard focus through PageDown, and restores expanded rows after unmounting. A version under another owned chat's URL redirects to its canonical chat; the back link uses the returned chat id. Both inspector offers carry the fork the message is viewed in (`?fork=`, the session's resolved fork, which needn't be the fork that generated the version nor the chat's current one); "Back to the reply" and every history source's link carry it on, so returning, or following a source, opens that fork at the message (`e2e/inspector.spec.ts`: two forks showing different versions at a shared slot, at 1440 and 390 px).
- **Generation time and tokens on a reply** (parity finding 5). The reader operation `VersionMetrics` (`inspectorReads.ts`; RPC `GetVersionMetrics`, at most 200 versions a call, another user's versions left out as if they had none) reads the `generation` ledger rows of each version that has rows of its own, together with those of the versions it was continued from, following the complete `source_version_id` chain with deduplicated identities (an edit continued counts the reply it was edited from; the edit itself, having no rows, shows nothing). `versionMetricsOf` (`@rp/shared/inspectorApi.ts`) projects them: requests, whether the last is still running, the summed start-to-end time of ended requests, the summed first-token-to-end time (unknown if any request lacks its first-token time), the output tokens of the ended requests that have a count with the number that have none (`outputUnknown`), reasoning tokens (null if any request reported none), whether any count is the app's estimate, and the last request's figures apart. The client's `message.header-details` slot (`messageHeaderDetails` in `presentation.tsx`, after the model name) gets `message.metrics` (`versionMetrics.tsx`): on a finished model-written version, "9.3 s · 209 tokens" ("~" for an estimate, "+" when some request had no count, "tokens unknown", "time unknown"), a button whose tooltip and click-open panel list generation time, time to first token (the last request's), output tokens, speed, reasoning tokens and "Reasoning time: not recorded" (the ledger has no reasoning end time) when the version has reasoning, and the request count explicitly saying it includes the original generation, retries and continuations, with the last request's time and tokens (labelled estimated when applicable) when there is more than one. Reads are batched per microtask per chat session (`metricsLoader.ts`) and repeated when the version's status changes; a failed read or one that still finds the last request running is repeated a second later, up to five times, independently of React effect changes. The metrics panel uses balanced columns and scrolls within its available height so the last request remains reachable on phones. Nothing shows while generating. The speaker's name keeps its width (up to 12 rem) so the model label truncates first. Tests: shared `test/versionMetrics.test.ts`, client `test/versionMetrics.test.ts` (texts, unknowns, the registration), `test/continueReasoning.test.ts` (an edit continued sums two requests; the inspector's times), `test/isolation.test.ts` (`GetVersionMetrics`), `e2e/continueReasoning.spec.ts` (the header, its panel and the inspector's columns at 1440 and 390 px).

**The cache-warmth estimate** (DESIGN §5.5; the reader op `CacheWarmth` in `db/assemblyReads.ts`, the pure comparison in `assembler/warmth.ts`, the service `chat/CacheWarmth.ts`). Whether the provider's cache for a chat is likely warm for the next reply, labelled as an estimate.
- **History budget explanation.** `NextContext` adds an optional `budget` with the effective context maximum (clamped to the model), stepped target, response reserve, and estimated tokens taken by everything except raw history, plus the preset's name and whether it is ST's built-in default. The maximum and target include the response reserve. Assembly records these counts in new snapshots; a fixed-content budget failure also carries them, so the next-context read can explain it without a successful assembly, a model or an earlier send. Other assembly failures return a null budget. The chat's virtualized list shows one explanation before the first retained raw message, or at the end when none fits, only in the loaded window containing that boundary. It names the preset and these token amounts, including the built-in fallback. Hidden and summarized messages keep their separate marks.

`NextContext.assemblyError` carries an assembler refusal through the reader’s next-context estimate and Cache events, including a newest message that cannot fit the history target while fixed prompts still fit the maximum. The registered `chat.composer-notes` contribution `composer.assembly-error` shows the refusal before Send; a successful estimate clears it. The existing history boundary continues to describe the last successfully assembled cut. Server `test/stPresetContext.test.ts`, client session/contribution tests and browser `stPresetContext.spec.ts` cover the refusal, its removal after repairing the budget, registry replacement/disablement, and both widths/themes.
- **From the last request** of the chat that reached its provider: the newest ledger row with the chat's attribution that is running, complete, or got a first token [P]. Its start is when the cache was written or read. Verified when the provider reported a cache read or write on it; assumed otherwise [P].
- **TTL eligibility:** model override, connection override, then the documented catalog policy (`providers/cachePolicy.ts`), with a source URL per value. pi-ai's `promptCache` guesses are not defaults. After transforms, capture pins the actual requested mode and its TTL, including extended retention and OpenRouter upstream model namespaces. Editing a TTL cannot extend an existing cache: expiry uses the historical captured TTL. An explicitly unknown final policy stays unknown through capture and readback; only absent legacy policy metadata permits fallback. Intentional user overrides still take precedence. Marker discovery checks shaped controls only at the selected API’s control locations, never inside schemas or tool arguments/results. Unknown policies have no default; the user can set one. Gemini cachedContents resources are not created by this app, so its explicit resource default is not a sliding timer here.

| Provider / mode | Built-in seconds |
|---|---:|
| Anthropic; Bedrock Claude; Vertex Claude | 300; 3600 when all requested markers use 1h |
| OpenAI GPT-5.6+ / later families; GPT-5.5 | 1800 |
| Earlier cache-enabled OpenAI, in-memory / requested 24h | 300 / 1800 (typical, not the 24h ceiling) |
| Azure GPT-5.6+; earlier in-memory / 24h | 1800; 300 / unknown |
| Moonshot K3 (including regional API), default / requested 1h | 300 / 3600; K2 and Coding unknown |
| Qwen / DashScope explicit | 300; implicit and token-plan contracts unknown |
| Groq supported GPT-OSS, including Safeguard 20B | 7200 |
| Bedrock OpenAI-compatible GPT-5.6+ / 6.1 | 1800 |
| MiniMax explicit Messages | 300; passive Chat unknown |
| Cerebras Chat | 300 from write |
| OpenRouter Claude, default / 1h unpinned / one pinned upstream | 300 / 600 / 3600 |
| OpenRouter Gemini implicit / explicit | 180 estimate / 300 fixed |
| Vercel known Claude / MiniMax route | 300; 3600 with requested 1h controls |
| Other policies, including direct Gemini implicit, DeepSeek, xAI, Mistral | Unknown |

Fixed lifetimes (Cerebras, OpenRouter explicit Gemini) use both the matching prefix's original write/miss time and that write's stored TTL, found in the chat's last 100 write/miss requests. Subsequent hits, TTL edits and transformed mode changes never renew them. Without a known origin there is no countdown. Sliding estimates conservatively start at request start. A changed prefix still produces a rewrite estimate without a TTL; a countdown requires both a historical expiry and a next-request TTL. No model, a missing connection or a next request that cannot be built yields no warmth estimate, while its recorded history boundary can still be published.

- **Whether the prefix still matches:** the reader assembles the next request as a new reply at the requested leaf (the active path for pushed estimates) [P], maps it through `wire.ts`, and compares the provider-formatted prefix with the pinned previous mapping. Connection id and cache revision, model identity and capability digest, roles, names and text must match. New captures compare SHA-256 digests of the actual final role/name/text and each sent image's content SHA-256 and detail before redaction; only display text is scrubbed. Digests stay in worker storage/comparison and are excluded by the explicit `GetRunContext` projection. Older captures without digests fall back to their stored text and can conservatively read cold after redaction [P]. Unassigned transform insertions inside the reusable prefix also participate in comparison. The next Responses request uses the installed SDK's message converter with pinned reasoning and developer-role flags; the historical comparison uses the final captured mapping. A capability change is conservatively cold even if its effect on these particular messages is unknown [P]. Legacy snapshots without a pinned connection revision are cold until a new request records one [P]. Without a reusable breakpoint the estimate is cold, never warm by default. A mismatch reports the first changed source, including piece membership and positions, and estimates the **next** prefix's uncached suffix through its last breakpoint [P]. A combined system block is counted as one wire message. A history cut caused by reaching the maximum says “The context reached its maximum; older messages left.” History movement under a changed budget is identified through emitted piece membership/start and the pinned history; unchanged retained turns with moved offsets are instead attributed to notes or the reusable-prefix endpoint/layout; an amendment requires changed text at the same version, while a changed version is reported as an edit. Image changes, removals and exclusion toggles also invalidate the prefix. Final capture hashes inline bytes transiently and persists only the digest; image bytes never enter the stored wire or client DTO. Model, connection route, system conversion, edits, branches, hidden messages, preset/card/note revisions, macro values and layout changes all invalidate the estimate.
- **Pushed, not polled.** `SubscribeChat` watches the chat for its owner once the subscription is allowed, and asks for an estimate. The hub calls the service for every event it publishes to a chat someone subscribes to (`ChatHub.listen`; not deltas, not `Cache`), which estimates again 250 ms after the last such change, never two at once for a chat, and publishes `Cache` with the estimate (null for none), path leaf and next request’s history start, independently of TTL or a previous provider request. An inactive-path client uses that event to invalidate its path-specific `GetNextContext` read; both RPC and worker validate the leaf against the owned chat. Editor commits, restores, regex approvals and merges, connection/model edits, model refreshes, connection credential/key/header/proxy changes, dependency deletion (`DeleteConnection`, `DeleteCredential`, `DeleteNote`), all bin restorations/purges/emptying, and generation defaults re-estimate the user's open chats too. Graph deletions (`DeleteChat`, `DeleteMessage`, `DeleteFork`, `DeleteVersion`) also refresh the user's other watched chats, since any delete can evict a shared dependency from the bin. Instance network edits refresh all watched chats, since they change what a chat sends without an event of its own [P]. A change from elsewhere (a re-import) shows with the chat's next change.
- **The indicator** (`features/chat/CacheIndicator.tsx`, above the composer): "Cache warm (estimate)" with whether the provider confirmed it and the time left, counted down in the browser from the request's start and the TTL, then "Cache cold (estimate)" when it runs out, or at once with the reason and “Next send rewrites ~N tokens” when the prefix changed. Without a TTL this uses “Cache rewrite (estimate)” and has no countdown. The countdown can be hidden, remembered per browser [P]; hidden, the indicator still turns cold on time, from one timer.
- Tested over a real server (`test/inspector.test.ts`): the inspector against the request the mock provider received, with sources, breakpoints, usage and issues; warm after a reply, cold after an earlier message's edit, on another branch, and after the default preset changes; no countdown without a TTL, but a rewrite estimate on a changed prefix. `e2e/steppedHistory.spec.ts` checks dropped versus hidden messages, the pushed boundary before a first send, warning without TTL, truncation retained in the inspector, and two tabs with an inactive-branch deep link. The isolation matrix probes the final `GetRunContext` projection, the writer op `RecordSentContext`, and the reader op `CacheWarmth`, both ways. In the browser (`e2e/inspector.spec.ts`), against the production server and a fake provider: opening a reply's context and its sources and breakpoints, an earlier reply's and a swipe's request from the message's button, the indicator warm then cold after an edit, the long context (its chat with summarization off), and the phone width. New captures name macro-joined descriptions, personalities and scenarios by their fields, and label persona fields as **Persona “<name>”**. History sources pin their one-based position among the generating fork’s undeleted swipes at send time, matching the swipe counter after deletion; later deletions do not renumber a captured request. Older captures retain their recorded labels and optional-field fallback. `test/inspectorProvenance.test.ts` covers persona attribution and deletion before capture, and checks the groups (a card's wrapped field inside the characters' definitions, its wrappers as frames; a personality format prompt holding the card it read) and a part and its source for each kind (preset prompt with its preset link, card field, note, lorebook entry with its title, history message with the version sent, the closing user turn, a skipped note and a skipped conditioned prompt as not sent) and that a capture stripped of parts and `notSent` still opens verbatim; The provenance regression also restores and deletes a preceding swipe, checks the current picker’s numbering, and verifies that captured numbers and exact snapshot replay stay frozen. `test/assembler.test.ts` checks all three macro-join titles (descriptions, personalities and scenarios). `test/lcmAssembly.test.ts` checks the summary's part. `test/inspectionParts.test.ts` checks `locate`'s order, nesting and overlap rules and its rewritten fallback (one piece, a run, a removed text, a rewritten block's parts); `test/inspectorCapture.test.ts` a registered transform's rewrite captured as its card field's rewritten part; `e2e/inspector.spec.ts` the "(rewritten)" box at 1440 and 390. `e2e/inspectorWalkthrough.spec.ts` walks a realistic synthetic chat (a preset with conditioned prompts, two cast cards, a persona, notes with one on another card's turn, a lorebook hit, a summary, a swipe) at 1440 and 390, light and dark, including each card's box and a personality format prompt's per-card boxes.

`test/stPresetContext.test.ts` HTTP-imports synthetic 50-message chats with 3,500- and 5,000-token card descriptions and ST's selected 32,000-token preset. It checks that the whole history fits after adoption, all three preset report messages, re-import preservation of a chosen default, and the built-in budget on fixed-content overflow. Without adoption, the smaller fixture's history start is depth 50 (past its last message at 49); the larger fixture fails assembly and has no boundary. `e2e/stPresetContext.spec.ts` checks the explanation at the end in both cases and its disappearance after importing the selected preset, and the side panel's This chat tab naming ST's built-in preset and its 4,095 tokens, then “Roomy” as the user's default with 32,000 and a link to the editor; the server test checks `preset` (name and scope) in `GetNextContext`; `e2e/steppedHistory.spec.ts` checks its position before retained history. The new writer operation has lease and cross-user preset probes in the isolation matrix.

**Known limits:**
- A message whose speaker has left the chat is named as the first card of its side.
- Reasoning streams and persists separately on the version (see Reasoning above); it stays out of ordinary text history.
- With `use_sysprompt` and a greeting first, Anthropic gets an assistant turn first, as ST sends it; not checked against the real API.
- ST's per-model sampler rules are its model-name patterns, copied: they'll need updating as ST's do, until models carry them.
- The time zone is UTC: the client doesn't send the user's yet.
- The cache estimate assumes the next request is a new reply at the displayed path’s leaf: a swipe, another speaker or a generation type a preset prompt triggers on can differ from it.
- Each estimate assembles the next request in a reader, after every change to an open chat: what an assembly costs (above), up to a few hundred milliseconds of a reader at 100,000 messages with state events.


**Source descriptors** (`assembler/sourceDescriptors.ts`). The assembler's scoped `sourceDescriptors` registry names provenance kinds; all eight built-in kinds register through it and keep their existing labels, identities, activation metadata and library/message links. A plugin stage can record a `PluginSource` with kind `<plugin-id>/<kind>` and plain JSON `data` in a piece. Its descriptor maps that pinned source to the inspector's title (`label`) and optional existing library (`ref`) or chat-message (`slotId`) link, using pure code and the owner-scoped built-in label lookups where relevant. A plugin needing other metadata pins it in its source data; it does not query SQLite from a descriptor. Source identity and activation remain in the fixed projection. Missing or disabled descriptors fall back to the kind as a readable title, without inventing a link. Descriptors install through trusted `AssemblyModules` in source and bundled readers; inspection copies the active entries within the request's module scope. The final request capture freezes and scrubs the resulting title with the rest of the display, so closing the plugin's scope does not alter historical inspector output. The shared contract admits built-in and namespaced kinds; the existing inspector renders their titles and typed internal links without another client registry. `test/inspectorSources.test.ts` exercises a scoped custom stage and descriptor through generation and the inspector RPC, fallback, built-in override/disable/restoration, and identical built-in output in source and bundled workers. `e2e/inspector.spec.ts` also renders a captured namespaced source with its title and library link at 1440 and 390 pixels. Cache-expiry comparisons need only whether a prefix differs and use the fallback label without querying source names.

**Assembly modules in readers** (`assembler/modules.ts`). The server exposes the scoped `AssemblyModules` registry alongside `Reader`. A trusted entry (`id`, absolute `module` URL, `exportName`) names an `InstallAssemblyModule` function. That function receives the assembler and registers projections, stages, macros, source descriptors or final transforms through the same APIs the built-ins use. Every reader RPC carries the server registry's current snapshot in an internal header, overriding inherited headers. The worker validates that snapshot and provides it in the request's Effect context. Each assembly (reply, rebuild or summary) and projection-metadata read installs those modules in a scope around the operation, then removes their registrations. No plugin code or SQLite runs on the main thread. Requests already admitted retain their snapshot; new requests after a scope closes see the previous modules, including after worker respawn. ESM modules stay cached, so changed code needs a new module URL. This supplies trusted worker module execution, not a plugin installer or consent UI. `test/assemblyStorage.test.ts` verifies scoped additions, overrides, disablement, metadata and missing-projection fallback through source and bundled readers.


## 16. LCM: hierarchical summaries

Phase 3 slices 1–7 implement summary storage, freshness, context assembly, planning, provider-driven summarization, refusal handling, condensation, re-summarizing, the summary UI, and enabling LCM on a chat that has history already with its backfill and cost estimate. `spec/LCM.md` records the design; its remaining unbuilt UI items are cost/model per summary and summarization rollouts in the panel (§2.13).

**The summary schema** (in the baseline) is the five summary tables and their indexes, reference guards, immutable revision triggers, draft cleanup and FTS triggers. Summaries add `failed`, error JSON and a batch id; revisions add `imported`; L1 inputs allow a null version for an absent effective message; higher-level lineage pins each child's revision. Jobs add an indexed batch and a leaf inside their chat. All foreign keys are indexed. Inputs, lineage, revisions and summary identity are immutable, including INSERT OR REPLACE, and may be deleted only with their chat. A new summary starts unsealed. Insert guards require its declared primary-parent chain, ordered L1 inputs or contiguous children at strictly lower levels, with sealed children and no self-reference. With insertions the range may instead follow display order (below). `summaries_chain` keeps the primary-chain check first, then permits ancestor endpoints spanning inserted children or endpoints ordered across an inserted subtree. It never bypasses endpoint checks just because the chat has an insertion. `summary_inputs_chain` and `summary_parents_chain` also permit returning through inserted ancestors to an older inserted sibling (UUID descending) or the anchor’s ordinary successor; they cannot return from an ordinary branch or reverse inserted siblings. Input guards reject merged slots. Sealing checks the count and last slot; together the guards preserve ordered lineage, allowing skipped insertions in a stale summary. These graph guards are independent of current fork placement: the layer and completion additionally validate membership and order in the viewed fork. Sealing checks complete coverage and closes both lineage tables to further inserts. A sealed summary cannot be reopened. Its base selected revision is frozen; per-fork selection and absorption status are overrides.

**Writes** (`db/ops.ts`, `db/lcmWrites.ts`) run in the writer, one transaction each:

- `CompleteSummary` requires an owned processing job and running summarize/condense rollout. It walks the job's primary-parent chain, refuses merges, requires L1 inputs in exactly chain order, or lower-level children that partition it in declared order with revisions belonging to those children. In a job's fork that shows inserted slots it checks the job's display range instead (below): inputs, or parts, in display order with only inserted slots between them, so a job claimed before an insertion inside its range still completes, and its summary is stale there at once; `slot_count` is the inputs' count, or the parts' sum. It saves and seals the summary, generated revision and inputs or lineage, then saves permitted absorption supersessions, the completed job and finished rollout with ledger usage atomically. An invalid reference or chain rolls back everything. Claimed jobs also bind completion to their frozen request.
- `EditSummary` writes and selects a user revision with `expectedRowVersion`, and commits only the named draft/session holding exactly that text, returning recovery metadata. `SelectSummaryRevision` selects a revision of that summary or of a completed replacement linked to it by its job, with the same concurrency guard. Choosing a replacement records a `summary_choice` override; choosing or editing the original restores it in that fork. `lcm_summary_choices` remains shared base state only. Replacement revisions appear in the original's Revisions list. Both refresh the caller's cache estimates.
- `GetSummary`, `EditSummary` and `SelectSummaryRevision` are authenticated RPCs (`@rp/shared/lcm.ts`, `api/lcm.ts`). Every new storage operation and RPC has cross-user probes in both directions. References reach their owner through the chat; foreign or deleted chats are `NotFound`.

**Freshness** (`db/lcmReads.ts`) is derived in the read transaction, in a fork (§3.8: the fork in view, `ForkView`, else the current one). A summary is shared by every fork whose path holds its chain, and is fresh in a fork when its inputs match what that fork shows, so a swipe or an amend that copies in one fork makes it stale there only, and only that fork's planner (`PlanSummaries`, run in the fork named, a reply's own, else the fork through its leaf) has work for it (`test/forkSelection.test.ts`, `test/forkVersions.test.ts`). An L1 is fresh when every input still matches its slot's effective version: the version the fork shows (its selection, or its copy standing in for it, §3.8) if it's nonempty, nongenerating and nonfailed, of a live, unpurged, unhidden slot, otherwise null. The input also records the amendment count because human versions can change content without changing id. An L1 is also fresh only if the fork would send the images it was written with (below, "Images"). A higher level is fresh only if every child is fresh and selects the recorded revision. A completed explicit rerun makes its generated source inactive for live freshness, so generated ancestors must incorporate the new child; historical contexts retain the old immutable child pins. Edits, swipes, deletes, restores and hides write nothing to summaries; selecting the old version or restoring visibility can make them fresh again. Reading a summary never starts a generation.

Tests: a bare-schema oracle for each new trigger and foreign-key indexes, a real-server hand-built branching graph with recursive freshness, amendment and revision selection, completion rollback, and the isolation matrix. Fixtures use synthetic data only and write SQL in a worker.


**Selection and partition** (`db/lcmLayer.ts`, registered `assembler/lcm.ts` stage). The reader walks the primary-parent path and considers sealed complete or truncated summaries ending on it. Before freshness recursion or cursor advancement it independently checks each candidate’s real slot chain, ordered inputs and recursive lower-level child partition, including pinned revision ownership. An inconsistent candidate is ignored, retaining usable earlier coverage and the remaining raw text; a visited-node memo bounds malformed cycles. At an endpoint, a user-written or imported revision wins until the user chooses a replacement; other candidates prefer freshness, then higher level, then newer id. A queued absorption cannot displace a protected same-level summary even when its new range extends beyond it. Empty summaries with changed inputs are excluded so restored effective messages return as raw history. It walks candidates backwards, retaining only a continuous prefix from the root: a gap leaves the original messages raw, matching the baseline's no-loss rule. A stale summary remains usable when no fresh candidate ends there. Summaries in a pending or processing batch are withheld; overflow can use completed members, as described below. The selected layer is in path order and its last slot is the cursor. History reads strictly after that cursor, including an empty read when it is the target's parent; covered originals are neither sent nor pinned. Failed rows do not replace raw messages while those fit. Summary freshness reads share a memo within selection, so repeated child lineage is read once.

**Literal rendering and placement.** Model and user summary text is never macro-expanded. Each summary of the layer is its own system message, `<summary level="N" from="A" to="B">` around its text (`summaryBlock`), oldest first, immediately before the history and its failure markers, placed like the history's messages (DESIGN §8, owner 2026-10-28): a new summary is a message after the earlier ones, which stay as they were sent, so their cached prefix holds. `{{summary}}` is ST's one summary: it reads only the chat's switched-on summary notes that are due by their condition, never the summaries, in every chat (the one assembly path, §15). The imported summary note is sent only by its own switch, also beside the summaries. A `{{summary}}` prompt is volatile (never inside a cached prefix) while any summary note with text has a condition, since what it reads can change from turn to turn. With LCM off, existing imported-note behavior and the ST differential oracle are unchanged.

**Inserted messages** (`db/lcmDisplay.ts`; spec/MESSAGE-INSERT.md §3.4). In a fork that shows inserted slots (`insertionPieces` in `db/displayPath.ts`, null otherwise: one probe of `fork_overrides_point` per lineage entry), LCM reads its display path (§3.8 "Display paths") and every number is a position there: a summary's `from` and `to`, the layer's cursor (`cursorDepth` in the assembler's `SummaryLayer`, a position, equal to the depth without insertions), job ranges, failed ranges and `LcmSlot.position`. A fork that shows none runs the structural code unchanged.
- *Lineage.* `lineageValidator` with the fork's view checks a summary's inputs, or its parts' ranges, in display order with only inserted slots between them and its ends where it says; an input the fork doesn't show makes it inconsistent there, so a summary that includes an inserted message is ignored in a fork without it.
- *Inside a range.* `readSummary` makes a summary stale in the fork when its display range holds more slots than it covers: an insertion anchored at one of its slots other than its last, for a condensation anywhere its parts cover or between them. That is the edit rule: it stays in use until replaced, the next reply's pass writes it again over the display range (`lcmPath`), the inserted message included, and other forks are untouched.
- *At a seam* (`seamStale`). A summary followed only by inserted slots that no summary starts at, up to one that starts right after them, covers those slots for the layer walk and the planner, stale, with its last slot moved to the gap's end. So the walk keeps the later summaries, and the planner writes it again over its first slot to the end of the gap; for a condensation, its last part is written again first and the condensation over the new part after, by the existing cascade. The same holds for a summary preceded only by inserted slots (an insertion above the first message): it reaches back to the first one. An insertion directly after the layer's cursor isn't a seam: it is raw history.
- *Paths and planning.* `lcmPath` reads the display stretch from `first` to the leaf in one statement driven by the pieces (`windowPieces`), with `position`, `previous` (the slot before it there; its parent without insertions) and `inserted`; `children` counts only children that weren't inserted, so an anchor isn't a branch point. Summary requests take the slot before the job's first as `summaryBefore`. Candidates of the layer, failures, maintenance and the UI reads are summaries ending on the structural walk from the leaf or at an inserted slot shown up to it. `EnqueueSummaries` and `EnqueueCondensations` compare live jobs' display ranges with the chunk's or parts' positions (`nearInDisplay`), check coverage by the chunk's slots, and require parts next to each other there. `AssembleSummary` lets a condensation's parts have inserted slots between them.
- *UI membership.* Coverage and failed-summary reads require the whole lineage on the viewed display path, rather than only a shared last endpoint. In a fork without inserted pieces, the structural span/count check excludes summaries that include another fork's inserted inputs without adding lineage walks to ordinary coverage reads. In a fork with pieces, the display lineage validator rejects absent inputs even when an unrelated insertion happens to keep the range's length equal to its input count.
- *Client data.* `GetLcmState` adds `cursorSlotId` and `cursorPosition` beside `cursorDepth` (the cursor slot's depth); `GetLcmCoverage` takes `fromPosition` and `toPosition` (else reads `fromDepth` and `toDepth` as positions; the numbers agree without insertions), and the client's coverage reads send positions. The message list's summarized and dropped marks and its summary divider compare positions (`placeOf` in `features/chat/summaryBoundary.ts`: an entry's `position`, else its depth) with the cursor's position and the history start (`test/summaryBoundary.test.ts`); the coverage rail still compares depths, and numbering by position is the client unit's.
- *Claims.* `ClaimSummaryJob` resolves live level-1 job starts to display positions in forks with inserted pieces and keeps the existing depth ordering elsewhere. Earlier ranges finish before later ones in that fork, retaining continuity context even when an insertion is structurally deeper than the later range. The two-worker account cap and per-fork serial guard are unchanged.
- *Known limits.* Imported-summary adoption (`lcmImported.ts`, the enable copy) and the backfill estimate read the structural path; an imported summary over a range that later gets an insertion is stale by the rule above.
- *Tests.* `test/lcmInsert.test.ts`: inside a range (stale in F, fresh in a fork made before, the plan's rewrite including X, enqueue and overlap by position, the result ignored in the other fork); at a seam (the later summary kept, the earlier rewritten over the gap; inside a condensation, the part then the condensation; before the first summary); directly after the cursor (raw, no work); jobs, L1 and condensation, completing across an insertion; a refused completion that skips a message that wasn't inserted; the relaxed triggers on their own; a summary including X ignored in a fork without X and used in forks made after; raw-SQL rejection of reversed inserted siblings, ordinary-branch returns, reversed condensation children and reversed endpoints; display-ordered claims; deletion, restore and edits in a fork made at an insertion, with repeated plans proving repairs settle; and a random oracle (four seeds of 28 insert, fork, append, summarize and condense steps) comparing validity, positions and freshness of every summary in every fork, and the layer's continuity and seams, with first principles.

**Context membership.** `NextContext` and cache events carry the selected layer’s last covered depth alongside the raw history start, scoped to the viewed leaf. The client labels those covered messages “Summarized”; only uncovered messages before the raw start have the faded dropped marker. Hidden messages retain their separate hidden marker.

**Budget and cache.** With LCM enabled the summary cursor first removes covered messages. Assembly retains the recorded stepped raw start strictly after the cursor. It holds between sends and summary landings until that suffix exceeds the maximum, at which point it cuts toward the target. A cursor landing past the start advances it to the first remaining raw slot; a landing before it leaves it in place. The existing on-path restoration check fences branch state. Its runway is measured against the maximum allowance, not the smaller target used by that cut. With LCM disabled the recorded stepped start and restoration rules in §15 apply unchanged. Both modes pin their resulting raw start and example admission for replay.

In LCM mode examples are budgeted before the changing verbatim window. The summary block's target allowance (the chat's `layerTarget`, fitted as in §15 *Stepped history*) counts each summary message's rendered text, wrapper and message overhead; unused allowance is reserved from history, bounded by the remaining budget. Fixed prompts and the actual layer must fit. This keeps the example prefix stable while a growing layer stays inside the allowance. Its maximum (`layerMax`) is the budget condensation measures against (below); send waits are described below. Fresh failed leaf ranges beyond the selected cursor remain raw while the window fits. When a cut would remove part of such a range, it removes the whole failed range and sends `[Messages A–B could not be summarized.]` before the retained history. Overlapping failures share one marker; adjacent ranges stay separate so a later range can remain raw when it fits; its tokens and provider post-processing are included in admission, so trimming cannot silently drop it. It is an assembler source and a `summary_failed` assembly issue. Failed inputs that changed no longer produce a marker; selected successful coverage replaces it. Other omitted raw ranges are recorded by immutable path depths and reported in the inspector as “Messages A–B left out: not summarized yet”; LCM omits the repetitive history-truncation warning from reply events. Stable history, the layer and the prefix receive breakpoints in that priority. Provider system extraction ends before the layer, preserving independent Anthropic prefix/layer markers and source attribution. The provider’s reusable cache identity includes the summary layer, so a summary replacement invalidates the prefix from that layer onward and cold followers stagger at the new boundary. Idle summary claims use the same historical expiry calculation as the chat estimate, including fixed cache write origins; a model TTL edit cannot extend an existing cache. The cache-warmth estimate identifies summary replacements and cursor changes as “Summary of messages A–B replaced.”

**Pins and inspection.** A version-19 snapshot also pins the on-path failed summary ids/revisions and their immutable depth ranges, separately from the selected layer; the snapshot records the ranges whose markers were actually sent. Rebuilds use those ranges without querying live failure state, even after an edit, retry or successful replacement. A snapshot pins each selected summary id and immutable revision, freshness/status and cursor, plus selected, omitted and logical verbatim ranges. `RebuildContext` uses those pins without selecting again, even after summary edits, revision changes, staleness or a cursor move. Purging covered originals does not prevent replay. The inspector labels summary sources with level, depth range and revision number, and derives whether they have since become stale or been replaced from the current layer on the recorded path. Historical stale flags remain in the original capture.

**Defaults.** The typed `lcm.settings` state event is fork-scoped and accepted only at the initial position, without version or role scope. It applies throughout that fork and is inherited by later forks through their bounded lineage (owner Q5). Its fields (`@rp/shared/lcmSettings.ts`, DESIGN §8): `enabled`; `withPreset` (send summary requests with the full preset, or the instruction and messages only); `withNotes` (send the chat's notes with a summary request); `tail` (the instruction); `recentTarget`/`recentMax` (recent messages kept as written) and `layerTarget`/`layerMax` (the summary block), positive tokens, a target below its maximum when both are stored; `when` (`after-reply` while the cache is warm, or `next-reply`); `leafInput` (chunk size) and `leafTarget` (summary length); `style`, `fanout` and `condenseTarget` are not shown. The account default (`generation.lcm`, `LcmDefaults`) has the same fields, `enabled` included as the default for new chats; its old `spendAhead` is read as `when` (false is `next-reply`) while `when` is absent, and writing `when` removes it. `resolveLcm` (shared by server and client) takes each field from the chat, else the account default, else the built-in: on, with the preset, without notes, recent 12,000/20,000 and summary block 14,000/18,000 tokens (owner, 2026-10-28: summaries cover unbounded history and recent chat grows faster, so it gets the wider band), after the reply, length 600, and the chunk min(4,000, half the recent band once fitted) (`chunkSize`). `CreateChat` and `StartChat` store a full copy of the account default as it resolves then (`newChatLcm`; the instruction and chunk size only when the account sets them), so a later change of the default leaves them as they were (Q2); newly imported chats store the same copy with `enabled: false`; importing into an existing chat preserves its settings. Chats from before store only `enabled` and follow the account default field by field. Existing chats without the key remain off. Migration `0051_lcm_new_defaults` (`lcmDefaultsMigration.ts`) moved chats to the new built-in sizes when they held a copy of the old ones (16,000/24,000 and 8,000/12,000): a size moves when every `lcm.settings` event of the chat has it at the old value and the account default doesn't set it, and a pair moves back whole if its target would reach its maximum; it appends one new event per chat it changes. A size the user set, in the chat or in the account default it was copied from, stays.

`ChangeLcmSettings { chatId, forkId, change }` writes one partial change (null removes a field, so it follows the account default) as a new fork-scoped event in one writer transaction that reads the current one; a stored target at or above its stored maximum is refused (`LcmSettingsInvalid`), and other misfits are shown, not refused. It publishes `State` and `Lcm`. `SetLcmEnabled` is the same write with the switch alone. `ChangeGenerationDefaults` takes the account default's fields as `change.lcm`, one at a time, with the same check. `GetLcmSettings { chatId, leafSlotId, forkId }` (reader `LcmSettingsAt`) returns `{ stored, resolved, account, fitted, room, now, fit }` from one assembly of the next context with LCM forced on, as `EstimateBackfill` does: the pairs as fitted, the model's context, fixed part and reply reserve, the current recent and layer figures, and one sentence per misfit (`lcmMisfits`): both maxima beside the fixed part and the reply against the context ("Recent messages (24,000) and summaries (12,000) need 36,000 tokens; this model's context leaves 22,400 after the preset and reply.", under the pair with the larger maximum), the chunk twice inside the fitted recent band, and each target below its maximum. While the chunk doesn't fit twice, `PlanSummaries` plans nothing and returns as it does with summarization off: the misfit is the user's setting, shown under the chunk size, not a failure to log. Both RPCs and both operations have isolation probes in both directions. Enabling an imported chat, adopting its summary and the backfill are described under *Enabling and backfill* below. Edits and reads never start summarization.

**Tests.** `lcmAssembly.test.ts` exercises real OpenAI and Anthropic captures, both assembly modes, literal macros, freshness preference, coverage gaps, failures while raw fits, inspector/cache invalidation and replay after purging covered originals. `lcmPlacement.test.ts` covers depth and absolute placements, late main ordering for default and imported before/after-main summaries, card overrides, provider provenance and stable example budgeting across both zero-to-one and one-to-two layer transitions. `lcmFailureMarker.test.ts` checks originals while they fit, markers and maximum-budget admission in both layouts, stale failures disappearing, successful repair and exact replay of both decisions. Schema tests exercise publication, closure and lower-level lineage; real-worker corruption probes verify raw fallback for off-path endpoints, wrong input chains and self-links. The independent graph model (`test/protocol/lcm.model.test.ts`) runs four seeds of 150 steps with real workers, server and sockets and synthetic fixture summaries, checking path, exact partition, freshness, immutable retention and historical replay after every graph/summary step. Its independent in-memory graph oracle and shrinking traces extend the drafts model's pattern. Job and batch oracles are described below; refusal judging remains slice 4.

**Planner and durable queue** (`db/lcmPlanning.ts`, `db/lcmQueue.ts`, `lcm/Summarizer.ts`; slice 3). The planner uses the recent pair as the assembly fitted it (`lcm.recentBudget`). It keeps the target, plans once the uncovered recent tokens pass the midpoint of target and maximum (built in, Q6: summaries are usually ready before the maximum, where a send waits), and batches whole-message chunks from before the kept part; `over` is the maximum. The default chunk is min(4,000, half the band), so the runway fits two chunks; a chunk size breaking `maximum − target ≥ 2 × chunk` fails planning before a provider request, and the settings panel says so. Branch points end chunks above a quarter of their target; generating or merged slots stop planning. Chunks also stop after 128 slots, independently of tokens. The writer rejects oversized chunks before reading their chains, and its coverage query uses a bounded chain CTE rather than a parameter per slot. All-null chunks and condensations of empty summaries seal zero-token lineage without a provider call; the next pass continues from that coverage without a gap. An enqueue race rolls back and the scheduler retries from the frozen episode. The default output target is 600. Condensation and re-summarizing are described below; an edit never schedules work.

`EnqueueSummaries` rechecks chains and effective version/amendment pins in one transaction and rejects overlapping live L1 ranges and ranges already covered by complete or truncated L1 summaries with identical effective input pins.
